- **`std/lineedit.tom`** (the sheet: `docs/lineedit-api.md`): `Editor` (prompts, `history`, the hooks, `tab`, `colors`; `read(term)` gives a `Result`: `'text'`, `'eof'` or `'interrupt'`), the hooks as interfaces `Validator`, `Indenter`, `Highlighter` with default classes, `TermIO` (which `terminal.Terminal` implements, and a test's `FakeTerminal` too), the pure `Buffer`, `History` (the draft; edits to a recalled entry dropped when Up or Down leaves it, Davis's answer to question 6), the history file functions with a joiner the program chooses, `layout` and `Screen` (the region redrawn whole in one write per key). The keys are Python's and readline's (the sheet's table). No colors yet: the `Highlighter` hook exists, and stage 3 fills it.
- **Precise where the design was a proposal:** the hooks are never `None` (an interface type cannot be optional), so they default to `AlwaysComplete`, `KeepIndent` and `Plain`; `TermIO` has methods only, so the colors are `Editor.colors`; `History.next()` gives the draft itself after the newest entry (the design had `None` there); Down recalls a newer entry with the cursor at the end of its first line (Up puts it at the end of the last, so both walk through the block); Backspace with only spaces before the cursor goes back to the previous tab stop.
- **`compiler/src/repledit.tom`** (beside the lexer, not part of the compiler): the program `tomato-repl-edit [--history FILE] [--color=...]`, exit 0 with the piece on standard output, 10 for Ctrl-D, 11 for Ctrl-C, anything else a failure. Its validator is the old rule (a block opened by a line ending with `:` or starting with `@` ends at a blank line) plus the compiler's own `lex` for brackets, so `x = (1,` and `f(` go on to the next line (Davis's answer to question 5); a piece the lexer rejects for another reason runs at once, so the compiler reports it. Its history is the old file, `⏎`-joined, 1000 entries, without `:commands`, `leave()`, `exit()`, `quit()`, empty pieces, or a piece equal to the newest entry (a recalled piece run again unchanged, which the old repl did not record for a block either) (question 3).
- **The driver** (`native/tomato-repl`): runs the helper once per prompt when standard input is a terminal and `TERM` is not `dumb` (`native/tomato` sets `TOMATO_REPL_EDIT`); a single-line piece still goes through the `:commands`. When the helper is missing or fails it prints one line, `the line editor is not available; using plain input`, and goes on with `read -e` (no history) for the session; only exit 10 leaves. Piped input is the plain path exactly as before. `recall_block`, `show_block`, `BLK` and the readline history handling are gone. A program's last line left open (`print(x, end='')`) is ended before the next prompt, since the editor draws from the line's start.
- **Built at install time:** `install-native.sh` builds the helper with the staged `tomato build ... --mode release` (release, so no leak report or trace is drawn over the editor); the step is optional and prints a warning with the reason when it fails. The tarball (`release/build.sh`) ships `compiler/src/repledit.tom` and `lexer.tom`. `release/pkg.sh` builds a universal helper from one IR (`tomatoc ... release`, then `clang -arch` per architecture and `lipo`), fatal there, with a check that `lipo -archs` says `x86_64 arm64`. The compiler did not change (`std_modules()` had `lineedit` since stage 1), so no bootstrap refresh and no WebAssembly rebuild.
- **Tests.** `tests/lineedit_logic.tom` (32 `@test` functions, debug and release: `Buffer`, `History` and its file in the old format, `layout`, frames, whole sessions on a `FakeTerminal`), `tests/repledit_logic.tom` (the validator, the indenter, what is recorded), `tests/repl_history.exp` rewritten as pty tests of the real repl with the built helper (a typed block with auto-indent, recall and edit of its middle line, a line added and one deleted, Ctrl-C, a bracketed paste, UTF-8, a resize, Ctrl-D, the history file, `stty -g` the same after the session, a failing helper, a helper killed with `SIGTERM`, `TERM=dumb`; it fails, never skips, when the helper is missing), and a check that piped input never uses the helper.
- **Not done here:** syntax highlighting and `--color` for the editor (stage 3), a single-line mode with completion and a worked example (stage 4). Known limits: the width of a grapheme cluster (a flag) can be a cell off, as in `terminal`; Ctrl-C or Ctrl-D typed ahead while a piece is still running reach the shell's terminal settings, not the editor (Ctrl-C there stops the run, as before).

### D89a — Seeds (packages): Homebrew-like open use and a default index that starts with no requirements: accepted direction (Davis); the design is `docs/package-registry-design.md`, nothing is built

- Evidence: the TODO "decide the seed registry design" (stage 0 of `docs/package-registry-design.md`). Davis's words, verbatim and typo included: "I think we will do something like homebrew for seeds like a you can it username/whatever easy not paproval to get a full thing with just test you need star recommendations not relay reuirements becouse like fi you adj and your @74 you will probaly get acepted". In a clarification the same day: there are **no requirements for admission to the default index to start with** (no test requirement, no score threshold, no star requirement), and **requirements are increased over time**.
- Accepted direction (the earlier decisions of the design's first section stand: seeds, `owner/name`, no GitHub dependency, no curator and no human approval step, aliases for short names, `tomato.toml` and `tomato.lock`):
  1. **Open use, like a Homebrew tap.** Any seed can be used as `owner/name` at once, from its own git repository on any host, with no approval. A domain owner can serve its signed statements on its own website and need no registry at all (`add` from the owner's server, moved up to stage 4). There is no `tomato seed tap` verb: another index is added with `tomato seed registry add NAME URL`.
  2. **The default index launches at policy level 0.** What it accepts and lists is exactly the structural rule of section 1.5 (valid manifest, resolvable source, owner proof, name rules, no collisions, rate limits). Nothing about tests, stars or a score is required.
  3. **Requirements rise over time through a versioned `policy.toml` in the index**, a file of numbers and switches read by the checker, so tightening is not a code change. A tightening needs a notice period (proposed 60 days, enforced by `index-check`) and applies to **new listings only**; seeds already listed are grandfathered. Re-examining listed seeds (demotion) is a separate switch, announced separately, used last, gives a notice and a grace period, and ends in being **delisted, never deleted**.
  4. **Listing is not use.** "Usable" (`owner/name` works) and "listed" (appears in the default index's listing) are separate. Raising the bar changes discovery only: locks, `install --locked`, `verify` and `owner/name` requirements are never affected, and `tomato.lock` does not record the status. Only a user who sets `[registry] only_listed = true` is affected by a delisting.
  5. **Signals are collected from the first day so the later levels are set from data**: the acceptor's age stamps and version history; signed endorsements ("stars", by owners' keys, no accounts); sandboxed-runner test results; a shadow score and "would pass level N" for every seed in a generated `listing.toml`. Not collected: downloads, usage, telemetry.
  6. **The proposed ramp** (numbers and order are Davis's to confirm): level 0 (launch); level 1 a license and passing tests, for new listings; level 2 level 1 and a score of at least 40, where documentation, age and endorsements add to the tests (each level only adds conditions, so each is a tightening); level 3 level 1 and a score of at least 60 with demotion enabled. Raise a level when about 80% of what is listed would already pass it.
  7. **Minimal safeguards from day one**: hash pins and the lock, signatures and the owner proof, no code run at fetch or install, the structural checks (parse, compile, no `!` declarations), yank by the owner's key, a security contact (`SECURITY.md` and an optional `security` field), rate limits, the warn-only lookalike check.
- Alternatives: a curator or a maintainer review (rejected earlier by Davis); tests and stars as hard requirements from the start (the clarification rejects it); a bare-name namespace from the listing like homebrew-core's `brew install wget` (it brings back first-come naming, so deferred, open question 7; D100a later added short names by reviewed pull request, never first come); a free-text username (needs an authority, kept as domain or key owners plus aliases, open question 8); account-based stars (no accounts exist; replaced by signed endorsements with caps and weights); running seed tests inside the acceptor (would make the registry a remote-code service; replaced by a separate sandboxed runner that gates nothing at launch).
- Cost, stated plainly: at level 0 "listed" means "structurally valid" and nothing else, so malware, junk and lookalike owners are all listed and usable; the registry has no power to remove one except the owner's own yank (unless open question 1 is answered with named security keys); search is poor until the signals exist; endorsements are cheap to fake for a few domains' price, so they are capped at 30 of 100 points and never replace the structural rules; a tightened policy cannot be made to feel fair to a seed that was listed at level 0 other than by grandfathering and notice.
- **Interpretation to confirm (Davis)**: the last part of his sentence ("fi you adj and your @74 you will probaly get acepted") is not clear. It is read as an example that a project with roughly that many stars or that score would probably be accepted, so no number is decided; every threshold is a setting in `policy.toml`. It matters only when the first non-zero threshold is chosen (open question 10). Other points to confirm are in `docs/package-registry-design.md` section 19, questions 6 to 11: the usable/listed split, bare names, "username", who edits `policy.toml`, the ramp, and whether key owners' endorsements count.
- Still open from the design (not answered by this entry): questions 1 to 5 of the design (a narrow yank-only emergency power, key-only owners, how `.tom` files name a seed, `os.run` in `std`, whether `tomato run` may fetch). No language rule changes and nothing is built; `docs/specification.md` gets its section when stage 1 is built.
- Supersedes / superseded by: none. Refines the "no curator" bullet of the design with the listing ramp; D77g's `[limits]` and the two configuration files are unchanged. **Refined by D100a (2026-11-27):** the listing ramp stays automatic, and bare (short) names, which this entry deferred (open question 7), are now granted by a reviewed pull request.

### D90a — The repl colors the code as you type it: `lex_spans`, the highlighter and the palette (stage 3 of D86a): accepted (Davis)

Stage 3 of `docs/repl-editor-design.md`; Davis approved the recommendations of its section 14, among them question 2 (the lexer stays non-public) and question 7 (the colors). The editor of D88a now draws the code in color, using the compiler's own lexer.

- **`lex_spans(src) -> tuple[list[Span], LexState]`** in `compiler/src/lexer.tom`, next to `lex` (not a std module, so the token set is not a public promise, question 2). It never raises and its spans are lossless: the spans of a line, one after the other, are the whole line (`Span` is `kind`, `line` from 1, `col` and `end_col` in characters from 0; the kinds are `keyword`, `name`, `number`, `string`, `fstring-brace`, `op`, `comment`, `space` and `error`). The expression of an f-string field is lexed as code, its format spec is a `number`. Where `lex` would raise there is an `error` span and the text goes on: an unknown character, a tab in the indentation, a line indented differently from its block, an escape that is not known, letters stuck to a number, a `{` or `}` of an f-string that does not fit, and an empty span at the end of a text whose closing quote is missing. `LexState` says what is open at the end: `depth`, `indents`, where the first open bracket is, `message` with its place (the first problem, which is what `lex` raises, so a test can compare them) and `rejected` (something other than an open bracket is wrong).
- **Shared scanners.** `lex` was refactored so that the places where a number, a text, an f-string and its fields, a name and an operator end are found by `scan_number`, `scan_string`, `scan_fstring` (its parts), `name_end` and `op_len`; both `lex` (which makes tokens, raising as before) and `lex_spans` call them, so the two cannot disagree. The refactor changes nothing that `lex` does: its tokens and its errors (message, line, column) are identical to the old lexer's on every `.tom` file of the repository and on every prefix of every line of them (2.5 million lines compared before and after). `tests/lexer_spans.tom` (run by `tests/run.sh` with `tomato test`) lexes every file of the repository both ways: `lex_spans` never raises, its spans cover the text, `lex` raises exactly when `lex_spans` has a `message`, with the same message, and the kinds of the tokens agree and come in the same order (for a line with an f-string, which `lex` rewrites to synthetic tokens, the keywords and names in the fields agree); the same for every prefix of every line of a corpus of hard lines and of three real programs, and for every prefix of the corpus as a whole. A closing bracket with nothing open is an `error` span that `lex` lets through to the parser (the test counts it as an operator); an open bracket is *not* marked (the design had marked it), since it is the normal state while a multi-line expression is typed: `LexState.depth` says so.
- **`compiler/src/hltomato.tom`** (beside the lexer, not part of the compiler): `TomatoHighlighter` implements `lineedit.Highlighter` over `lex_spans` and adds what the lexer does not know: the name after `def`, `class` and `interface` (also after `async def`), a decorator such as `@main` (an `@` that starts a line outside brackets, with the name that touches it), the built-in functions and types (`is_builtin`: the lists of the website's highlighter, which `tests/highlighter_lists.sh` now checks, with the linter's `builtin_names`), and `True`, `False` and `None`; a name after a `.` is never a built-in. The editor draws the runs and wraps and places the cursor from the plain text, as before.
- **The palette** (`Palette`, one table): keywords red `#EB3C27`, texts green `#009639` (the brand colors, as `tomato paint` uses them), numbers cyan, `True`/`False`/`None` magenta, built-ins bright blue, comments dim, the name after `def`/`class` and decorators bold, the braces of an f-string field bold green, `error` spans underlined red, the prompt `>>> ` green and `... ` dim. No background, no white, black or grey: it has to read on a dark and on a light terminal, and the editor does not try to find out which it is (question 7). `terminal.sgr` lowers it: 24 bits as written; at 256 colors the keyword red is the palette's 196 (the nearest to `#EB3C27`, 166, is an orange) and the green its nearest, 29; at 16 and 8 colors the terminal's own red and green (`ansi(1)` and `ansi(2)`, not the bright red that the lowering of `#EB3C27` gives, which the stage 1 note asked for). The bright blue (the plain blue is dark on a dark background) is the one choice that departs from the design's words ("the terminal's blue").
- **`--color`, `NO_COLOR`.** `tomato-repl-edit` takes `--color=auto|always|never` and gives `terminal.color_depth` its mode: `always` and `never` decide (so `--color=always` wins over `NO_COLOR`, as in D61a), else `TOMATO_COLOR`, a non-empty `NO_COLOR`, `TERM=dumb`, and the depth from `COLORTERM` and `TERM` (24 bits, 256, 16, 8). The driver `native/tomato-repl` passes its own mode on, and runs the helper with `TOMATO_COLOR` set to that mode (it sets `TOMATO_COLOR=never` for the programs it runs). With no color the text is exactly what it was.
- **The validator** now reads `lex_spans` instead of matching the message of the lexer's error: `LexState.depth` says a bracket is open (and `rejected` that something else is wrong), and a block is opened by a line outside brackets whose last token is `:` or whose first is `@`. The same cases as before (`tests/repledit_logic.tom` is unchanged and passes); a text with a problem later in it now also sees a block that was opened before the problem.
- **Built and shipped:** `install-native.sh`, `release/build.sh` and `release/pkg.sh` copy `hltomato.tom` with `repledit.tom` and `lexer.tom`. The compiler changed (`lexer.tom`), so `compiler/bootstrap/tomatoc.ll` is refreshed (the fixed point holds) and `site/wasm/tomatoc.wasm` rebuilt with WASI SDK 24 (`runtime.wasm` is the same: the runtime did not change).
- **Tests.** `tests/lexer_spans.tom` (12 `@test` functions, above), `tests/hltomato_logic.tom` (8: the highlighter on a table of lines, half-typed text, blocks, the palette lowered at every depth, nothing white, black or grey and no background) and `tests/repl_history.exp` extended with the colors: a session at 24 bits (a typed line, a recalled line edited in place, an error span, a comment), 256 colors, 16 colors (by the environment alone, and by `TOMATO_COLOR=always`), `--color=always` winning over `NO_COLOR`, and no escape sequence at all with `NO_COLOR`, `--color=never` and `TOMATO_COLOR=never`.
- **Not done here:** a single-line mode, completion and a worked example (stage 4, built in D91a). Known limits: while an f-string field is typed its `{` is underlined until the `}` is there (the lexer refuses an open field, and so does the highlighter), and `1.` is underlined (`lex` refuses a dot after a whole number); no light-background palette and no `OSC 11` query (question 7).

### D91a — The line editor library, finished: single-line mode, `Completer` and Tab completion, kill ring, a worked example (stage 4 of D86a): accepted (Davis)

Stage 4 of `docs/repl-editor-design.md`: what other programs need from `std/lineedit.tom` and the repl did not use. Neither the compiler nor the runtime changes (no bootstrap refresh and no WebAssembly rebuild; the website's copy of `std/lineedit.tom` is refreshed).

- **`single_line` mode** (`Editor.single_line = True`): Enter always submits (the validator is not asked), Up and Down go straight to the history, a recalled entry of several lines comes back on one line, a paste has its line breaks turned into spaces, and Tab never indents. `lineedit.read_line(prompt, history_file='')` is the shortest use: a single-line editor on `terminal.open()` with the entries of a history file (one per line) and the submitted line appended to it. It needs a terminal (`TerminalError` otherwise): a program that also runs piped checks `terminal.is_tty(0)` and uses `await input()` itself, because `input` is async and a library function cannot await it for the caller.
- **`Completer`** is a hook like the others, an interface with one method: `complete(line, before) -> Completion`, with the current line up to the cursor and all the text up to it (the arguments of `Indenter.indent_after`), answering `Completion(start, candidates, after='')`: where the word starts in `line`, the whole words it can become (not the missing ends, so a completer may change the case or add a prefix), and the text `after` the word when it is the only candidate (a space after a command; paths would use `/`). The default is `NoCompletion`. **Tab**: one candidate replaces the word (plus `after`); several give their longest common start when that is longer than what was typed; otherwise the candidates are **listed under the text** in columns (down the first column, then the next, as `ls`; `Editor.menu_rows` rows at most, 8 by default, and never more than fit in the window; "... N more" when not all fit). The list is part of the next frame only (the screen's new `menu`): any key takes it away, even one the editor ignores, and the last frame, the submitted text, never has it. Rejected: cycling through the candidates with repeated Tab (a menu-select state to keep right with resizes and the tall-block window; typing more letters and pressing Tab again narrows the list, as readline's default), a pager for very long lists, and a candidate type with a separate display text and description (a list of plain words is what a first completer needs; a richer `Completion` can be added beside it).
- **A change to Tab, as asked for by the Python 3.13 model of the design:** with no candidate, Tab inserts the `tab` text only when nothing but spaces is before the cursor (an indent), and does nothing after other text. Stage 2's Tab always inserted four spaces, so `x = 1` then Tab gave `x = 1    `; that was never useful, and a completer that finds nothing for `foo.ba` must not put spaces into a name. In single-line mode Tab never inserts.
- **The kill ring is optional.** `Editor.kill_ring` is how many cuts Ctrl-Y can bring back: 1 (the default) is stage 2's behavior, the last cut only. With more, cuts made in a row (Ctrl-W, Ctrl-W) join into one entry (Ctrl-K at its end, Ctrl-U and Ctrl-W at its front) and Alt-Y right after Ctrl-Y swaps the text put back for the cut before it, round to the newest again; the ring belongs to the `Editor` and lasts over `read` calls. `KillRing` is exported and pure. One detail changed for everyone: a cut of no text (Ctrl-K at a line's end) no longer empties what Ctrl-Y puts back (it replaced it with nothing before). The repl does not turn the ring on.
- **The repl completes Tomato names** (`ReplCompleter` in `compiler/src/repledit.tom`), because it was cheap and is clearly useful: the keywords, the built-in names, the names the session uses, the standard modules after `import` and `from`, and `:commands` at the start of a line. The session's names come from `--session FILE`, a new option of `tomato-repl-edit`: the driver writes the session's program (`program`, the function behind `:program`) to a file in its temporary folder before each prompt, and the helper lexes it with `lex_spans` at the first Tab: every name that is not a keyword and does not follow a dot, so parameters and locals of earlier pieces too, which is more than "defined in the session" and needs no parser; the repl's own wrapper name is left out. The modules are the `.tom` files of `$TOMATO_STD`, which the `tomato` command sets. Nothing is offered after a dot (the type of the value is not known: attribute completion would need the compiler), inside a text or a comment, on a number, or with nothing typed. The keyword list is a copy in `repledit.tom` (the lexer's `is_keyword` is not exported and the lexer is the compiler, so it cannot change here); `tests/highlighter_lists.sh` fails when the two differ. Not done: file names after `:load` and `:save`, the names a module exports after `from m import`, and attribute names of the session's own classes.
- **The worked example, `examples/todo_shell.tom`**, a todo list shell with a custom `Completer` (commands; the numbers of the items `done`, `undo` and `rm` can take), a single-line editor with the history of the session and a styled prompt, and a `shell(term, todos, colors)` over any `TermIO`; with piped input it reads plain lines. It is built and run by `tests/examples.txt`, and left out of the website's example menu (it needs a terminal).
- **Documentation:** `docs/lineedit-api.md` (the sheet: every field, key, hook and function, `read_line`, the example, the repl's completion) and `docs/terminal-api.md` are the sheets of the two modules; `tests/run.sh` now fails when either sheet lacks a name that its module exports.
- **Tests.** `tests/lineedit_logic.tom` 50 `@test` functions (18 new: the kill ring, `common_prefix`, `candidate_rows`, frames with a list under the text and in a small window, Tab with one, several and no candidate, `after`, the list going away, single-line mode, Alt-Y); `tests/repledit_logic.tom` 14 (7 new: the completer); `tests/todo_shell_logic.tom` 8 (the commands, the completer, whole shells on a fake terminal); `tests/repl_history.exp` has a completion section (the session's name, the list and its removal, `import`, a `:command`, nothing after a dot, Tab in an indent), and `tests/todo_shell_pty.exp` runs the example with `tomato run` on a pty (Tab, the list, the numbers, the history, a bracketed paste, Ctrl-D, the terminal's settings unchanged). Run on Linux and on a MacBook (arm64).
- **Not done here (follow-ups):** the repl's Alt-Y and kill ring (one line in `repledit.tom`, a choice for Davis), the open stage 5 (the driver in Tomato, needs `subprocess`), and a bug found on the way: `list.sort()` on a list held in a field, after appends, leaks its elements in debug mode (the repl completer builds a local list and assigns it, which does not).

### D92a — The `tomato.toml` manifest shape: `[project]` or `[seed]`, `[seeds]`, `[dev-seeds]`, and a `tomato` key: accepted (Davis, 2026-11-26)

- Evidence: Davis chose the shape, 2026-11-26, relayed by his lead agent; it replaces the `[seed]`/`[dependencies]` shape drafted in section 2 of `docs/package-registry-design.md`. Nothing is built.
- Decision:
  1. **A folder has `[seed]` (a published package) or `[project]` (a program that uses seeds).** `[seed]` has `name` (`owner/name`), `version`, `tomato` and optionally `description`, `license` and `modules`; `[project]` has `name`, `version` and `tomato`; `tomato seed init` with no name makes a project.
  2. **`tomato`** (was `requires_tomato`) is the oldest compiler that builds it.
  3. **`[seeds]`** (was `[dependencies]`) lists seeds and their minimum versions; **`[dev-seeds]`** (was `[dev-dependencies]`) lists test-only seeds. `"1.2.0"` means at least 1.2.0 within the same compatible line; there is no range syntax.
  4. **`{ git = "...", rev = "..." }` and `{ path = "../x" }` stay unregistered** and are allowed only in a project; the index check rejects them in a seed.
  5. **The old `[seeds] vendor` setting moves to `[seed-store] vendor`** (the same table will hold `auto_install` if question 5 of the design is answered that way). Chosen over `[build]`, which is reserved for compiler mode and flags, so that `[seeds]` is only the list.
  6. **Unchanged:** the rule that unknown top-level tables are an error stays (not yet enforced: the reader ignores them, see Cost); `[limits]` and `[build]` stay reserved (and `[seed-store]` is now added to the reserved names); a used seed's other tables are ignored; `tomato.lock` keeps its shape except that a seed's `dependencies` field is now `seeds`, as in the index's `[[version]]`.
- Alternatives: `[package]`/`[dependencies]` as drafted (one name for both roles; Davis's shape names a program that uses seeds and a published package separately and uses the shorter `[seeds]`); the vendor folder under `[build]` (mixes where sources live with how they compile).
- Cost: two table names where one served both roles, and one new reserved table. The configuration reader (D77g, `compiler/src/config.tom`) reads only `[limits]` and ignores other tables, so it already accepts the new tables; it does not yet reject unknown ones (a follow-up).
- Supersedes / superseded by: refines the manifest shape in D89a; D77g is unchanged. Item 3's `[dev-seeds]` is replaced by D92b.

### D92b — Per-entry seed tables `[seeds.ENTRY]` replace `[dev-seeds]`: accepted (Davis, 2026-11-26); superseded by D94b (one table, `[test-seeds]`)

- Evidence: Davis chose it, 2026-11-26, relayed by his lead agent, to match the dotted entry markers (every `@` marker marks one function, entry names are dotted, running entry `x` runs all `@x.*`). It changes D92a item 3 and sections 2, 3 and 4 of `docs/package-registry-design.md`. Nothing is built.
- Decision:
  1. **`[dev-seeds]` is gone.** `[seeds]` lists seeds for every build; `[seeds.ENTRY]` (for example `[seeds.test]`, `[seeds.greet.loud]`) lists seeds used only when running that entry. Seed names always contain `/` and are quoted keys, so they never clash with a sub-table name.
  2. **Which tables a run reads:** `[seeds]`, every table that is the entry or a prefix of it (so `[seeds.test]` applies to a run of `test.parse`), and, **proposed by the agent and not yet confirmed by Davis**, every table below the entry (a run of `test` runs `test.parse`, so it needs `[seeds.test.parse]`; without it a run of `test` could not build `test.parse`). A plain `build`, `compile` or `run` reads `[seeds]` only.
  3. **A seed in two tables:** the higher minimum wins; a mismatch of forms (`git` or `path` against a version, or two different `git`/`path` sources) is an error.
  4. **A published seed's `[seeds.ENTRY]` tables are read only by `tomato test` in that seed**; a user of the seed reads only its `[seed]` and top-level `[seeds]`, and the index entry lists only `[seeds]`.
  5. **The lock holds every seed**, with `entries = [...]` (the manifest's tables that reach it) only on a seed that not every build uses. `tomato seed install`, `add` and `update` run MVS once over the union of all tables, and the lock holds one version per seed, used by every build; `build`, `run` and `test` do not resolve again: the tables the run reads only choose which locked seeds are compiled in, and the build stops with `run: tomato seed install` if one is missing from the lock or the locked version is below the build's own minimum. So a test-only pin changes plain builds too (`[seeds]` at 1.0.0, `[seeds.test]` at 1.2.0: a plain build compiles 1.2.0), and a test-only requirement on a different line of a seed (1.0.0 against 2.0.0) is the two-lines error, so it stops every build and run of that manifest, not only `test`. The seed check lets a seed's modules import seeds from any of its own tables.
  6. **Registered-only rule:** `git` and `path` forms stay project-only in every table; the seed check applies it to `[seeds.ENTRY]` too.
- Alternatives: keep `[dev-seeds]` (one test-only set that cannot say which entry needs a seed, and a name unrelated to the entry names); a separate lock per entry (several files to commit and review, no gain since hashes are per seed).
- Cost: a typo in an entry name (`[seeds.tset]`) is a valid table; `tomato seed check` should warn when no such entry exists (a design note, not built). The configuration reader (D77g) ignores unknown tables, so it already accepts these; whatever later rejects unknown tables must allow `seeds.*` sub-tables (a follow-up).
- Supersedes / superseded by: changes D92a item 3; D92a is otherwise unchanged. **Superseded by D94b:** the per-entry tables are replaced by the one table `[test-seeds]`.

### D92c — Build outputs are `[build.ENTRY]` tables, flags override: accepted (Davis, 2026-11-26)

- Evidence: Davis chose it, 2026-11-26, relayed by his lead agent. It fills the `[build]` name that D92a reserved. Sections 2 and 13 of `docs/package-registry-design.md` hold the text. Nothing is built.
- Decision:
  1. **Tables.** `[build]` holds defaults for every build; `[build.ENTRY]` (same dotted names as `[seeds.ENTRY]` and the `@` markers) holds one entry point's settings. Example: `[build] mode = "release"`, `[build.main] output = "bin/todo"`, `[build.test] mode = "debug"`.
  2. **Two keys in 1.0:** `mode` (`debug` or `release`) and `output` (a path: in the file relative to the project folder, as an `--output` flag relative to where the command runs; the agent's choice, to be confirmed). `target` (`native`, `wasm`, ...) is **reserved**, not accepted, until a WebAssembly build from a project is real. Unknown or reserved keys are errors in the bare `[build]` and in every `[build.ENTRY]`.
  3. **Commands and precedence.** `tomato build` builds the default entry (`@main`), `tomato build ENTRY` that one. The flags `--mode` and `--output` win over `[build.ENTRY]`, which wins over `[build]`, which wins over the built-in default. `tomato run` and `tomato test` read the same tables for the entry they compile and use `mode` (`output` matters only to `build`); this is the agent's reading, to be confirmed.
  4. **Which tables apply:** the dotted-prefix rule of D92b: a table for `x` applies to `x` and to the entries below it (a table that is `x` or a prefix of the entry), the most specific table winning key by key. D92b's proposed extension (tables *below* the entry) is not used, because `mode` and `output` hold one value per build; nothing here depends on how that proposal is decided.
  5. **Where read:** a project's `tomato.toml` only, never a seed's (a seed's `[build]`, its own or a used seed's, is ignored), as `[limits]` (D92a item 6). The user's `config.toml` does not hold `[build]` (Davis: only in a project; the agent's reading, to be confirmed).
  6. **Compatibility:** the two keys, their values and this precedence are part of the 1.0.x promise (D84a). A new key or value, `target` included, can come only in a minor release.
- Alternatives: flags only (`--mode`, `--output` every time; nothing new to learn or reject, but every build repeats them or lives in a script, and a project's release output path is written down nowhere); cargo-style profiles plus `[[bin]]` (a `[profile.release]` and a list of named programs; familiar to Rust users and fits several programs per project, but it adds a second naming system next to the `@` entry names, profile inheritance and a table array for a file that has two keys).
- Cost: another set of table names to validate; a `[build.ENTRY]` naming no entry (`[build.mian]`) is a valid table, so `tomato seed check` should warn, as for `[seeds.ENTRY]` (D92b). D95a has since built the reader (`compiler/src/manifest.tom`) and the `[build]` defaults of `--mode` and `-o`; `--output` as a flag spelling is not built.
- Supersedes / superseded by: fills the reserved `[build]` of D92a item 6 and section 13 of the design; D92a, D92b and D77g are otherwise unchanged. `[seed-store]` stays apart from `[build]`. **Changed by D99a (2026-11-27):** the user's `config.toml` may hold `[build] mode` (item 5). **Changed by D94b:** `tomato build ENTRY` (item 3) is `tomato build [FILE]` with the option `--entry ENTRY`, and a build with no `--entry` holds every entry.

### D93a — What a `File` does after `close()`: Python's rules; default chosen by the implementer, easy to change (2026-11-26)

- Evidence: Davis has not decided. Until now D82a left a `File` as a bare C stream and a use or a second `close()` after `close()` undefined: `f.close(); f.close()` passed a freed `FILE *` to `fclose` (it printed E382 "Bad file descriptor", and under AddressSanitizer it was a use after free), and `read()` after `close()` was the same (`docs/language-design-review.md` 5.1, Blocks 1.0). The implementer chose the one answer that is safe and that a Python programmer already knows, so that 1.0 does not ship an undefined case. **This is a default, not Davis's decision: say so and it changes.**
- Decision:
  1. **A `File` is a counted object**, like text or a list: it holds the C stream, and the stream is `NULL` once the file is closed. A name for it can be copied, put in a list, a field or a dict, passed and returned; all the names are one file.
  2. **A second `close()` does nothing** (and raises nothing, even when the first one failed: the stream is gone after `fclose` whether or not it reported an error, and the error was raised by the first call).
  3. **Every other operation on a closed file raises `ValueError`, E547, `I/O operation on a closed file`:** `read()`, `readline()`, `readlines()`, `write()`, `writelines()`, `flush()` and `for line in f:` (checked before every line, so closing the file inside its own loop ends the next turn with the error, as in Python). The file is not touched by the failed call.
  4. **`f.closed()` says whether the file is closed.** It is a method, not Python's attribute `f.closed`, because nothing built in has a field and `File` has only methods (`f.closed` without the call is a compile error that says so, E548).
  5. **A file is closed when its last reference goes away** (the name is reassigned, the function returns, the list or object that held it is freed, an error unwinds past it), with the C library's flush, so data written and not closed reaches the file. Before this only `for line in open(...)` (D82a) and an explicit `close()` closed a file, and a dropped `File` kept its descriptor and unflushed data until the program ended. A flush that fails when a file is dropped is not reported (there is nobody to tell); call `close()` inside `try` and catch `IOError` to be sure the data arrived (E382).
  6. **Binary files:** `open()` takes text modes only (D80a), so every `File` is a text file; the bytes helpers (`os.read_bytes`, `os.write_bytes`, `std/json` and MessagePack's `load` and `dump`) take a path and hold no `File`, so there is nothing for them to close twice.
- Alternatives: keep it undefined and document it (a safe language should not ship a use after free, and the cost here is one flag test beside a stdio call); make a use after `close()` an `IOError` (`IOError` is for the operating system failing, and Python, whose rules this copies, uses `ValueError`); make a second `close()` an error (it forces `try`/`finally` code to track whether it already closed, and Python allows it); a `closed` attribute as in Python (needs a first field on a built-in type); close the file at the end of the scope as `with` would (`with` is deferred in D80a; the counted file already covers the leak, and `with` can be added on top).
- Cost: every `File` is now a small heap object (24 bytes) and each call on it tests the stream once. A reference cycle that holds a `File` is closed when the cycle collector frees it (not at once). The playground and the native build behave the same.
- Test: `tests/programs/file_closed` (every text mode: double close, each other operation after close, `closed()`, closing inside the loop, drop on reassign, return from a function, a list, a field and an error; descriptor counts from `/dev/fd`) in debug mode and under AddressSanitizer, in release mode under AddressSanitizer, and `tests/runtime/file_closed_uncaught` for the uncaught error (debug and release). The compiler change and the runtime change regenerate `compiler/bootstrap/tomatoc.ll` and `site/wasm/*.wasm`.
- Supersedes / superseded by: replaces D82a's "still undefined" for a use or close after `close()` and its remark that a closed flag was left for later; the rest of D82a (the loop closes the file it opened) is unchanged.

### D93b — A class with no fields of its own inherits its parent's `@init`, and `Error` has an `@str` (the message alone): accepted (Davis, 2026-11-26)

- Evidence: item 3.3 of `docs/language-design-review.md` (`class MyErr(ValueError): pass` then `raise MyErr("neg")` was E128, every error class repeated a forwarding `@init`, and `print(e)` was E375), taken from TODO.md by Davis's lead agent on 2026-11-26 as "do soon, high" so the text of `str(e)` is fixed before 1.0. The two decisions below (inherit for classes with no fields; the message alone) are the proposals of that item, built as written.
- Decision:
  1. **A class that declares no fields of its own and no `@init` is made with the `@init` of its nearest parent that has one.** It applies to every class (not only to errors), as in Python and Kotlin. `class NotFound(Error): pass` is `NotFound()`, `NotFound(message)` or `NotFound(message, id)` (the built-in error `@init` methods now give `message` the default `""`, so a fieldless error class that was made with no arguments before still is); `class Missing(NotFound): pass` too. Fields it inherits do not count; a field of its own, with or without a starting value, does. Such a class needs an `@init` of its own, and a call with the parent's arguments is E128 with the help `'X' declares fields, so it does not inherit its parent's @init`.
  2. **`Error` has an `@str` method (`message_text`) that returns `message`.** `print(e)`, `str(e)` and `f'{e}'` give the message alone, with no class name and no id (Python's `str(e)`). Every error class inherits it; a class may give its own `@str`.
  3. **The uncaught-error report is unchanged:** `error: ClassName: message (raised on line N)` (E11 in a release build), `error[ID]: message` for a runtime failure, and the call trace. The report does not call `@str`: it reads `message` and the class name directly, so a class's own `@str` changes `print` but not what an uncaught error says. E-codes are unchanged.
  4. **`std/json.tom`'s two forwarding-only classes (`JSONEncodeError`, `JSONTypeError`) drop their `@init`.** The other forwarding `@init` methods in `std/` and the built-ins (`make_value`, `make_io` ...) are kept: some are called by name (`IOError.make_io(self, ...)`) and removing them is a separate, mechanical change.
- Alternatives: inherit `@init` into a class with fields that all have starting values (safe, but a rule with an exception to explain; the rule can be relaxed later without breaking a program, whereas the strict rule cannot be tightened); `str(e)` as `ClassName: message` (matches the uncaught report, but Python gives the message alone and a user who wants the class name can add it, whereas stripping it cannot be done); a library `repr`-style second form (none exists in the language).
- Cost: a class that adds a field to a class it inherits `@init` from stops being constructible with the parent's arguments (E128 with the help line). A new method name, `message_text`, exists on every error class, so a subclass that defines a method of that name needs `override`. The compiler and `site/wasm/*.wasm` were regenerated.
- Supersedes / superseded by: refines D22a (the error classes) and D12 (`@init`); changes nothing earlier.

### D94a — `int | None`, `float | None` and `bool | None` are optionals held in a union-style box: accepted (Davis, 2026-11-26)

- Evidence: D23g (“you don;t need expkicith optional int float ool | is just a operator”) says `T | None` needs no special feature, and `docs/language-design-review.md` section 3.1 ranked the gap "Do soon, high": `x: int | None` was E459, and `d.get(k)` was E380 for every dict of `int`, `float` or `bool` values, the most common optional (a missing number) being the one that could not be written. Davis asked for it, 2026-11-26, relayed by his lead agent, together with `d.get(k)` for every value type.
- Decision:
  1. **`int | None`, `float | None` and `bool | None` are types** wherever `str | None` is: declarations, parameters (with `= None` defaults), returns, fields, collection elements and keys, tuple items, generic type arguments (`Slot[int]` with a field `T | None`), task results and generator yields. `None | int` is the same type.
  2. **Representation:** the optional is a pointer, as for the other optionals, and `None` is the null pointer. A non-`None` value is a pointer to the same small heap box a union holds a member in (type code `box:int`, `box:float` or `box:bool`, the value at byte 16, the print kind at byte 24), so the runtime's release, cycle-collector walk and `tomato_box_str` printing are shared with unions and nothing in the runtime changed. The other optionals (text, lists, dicts, sets, objects, interfaces, tuples) stay the pointer to the value itself, with no box.
  3. **Using one as a value** works as it does for `str | None` and not by flow analysis: every use that needs the value takes it out of the box and stops with the catchable `RuntimeError` "None where a value is needed" when it is `None` (arithmetic and comparison operators, `-x`, `abs`, `min`, `max`, `float()`, `round`, `chr`, `ord`, format specs, assigning or passing to an `int`, `float` or `bool` place, a `bool | None` as a condition). Variables are not narrowed by `is None` (as for every optional today; narrowing optionals is a separate decision; **it is D102a**, which narrows locals and parameters of every optional type, so a use inside a check such as `if x is not None:` takes no unwrap and cannot stop). `x is None`, `x is not None`, `x == None`, `x in None` and `x in int` work on the pointer. A big integer or a float of any size in the box is owned by the box and copied out.
  4. **Assigning:** an `int` to `int | None` (and likewise `float`, `bool`) boxes it; `None` is the null pointer. Nothing converts between them (`int` to `float | None` is the same E324 as `int` to `float`; `int | None` to `float | None` is E324), and fixed-width integers (`int[length=8] | None`) stay E459 with a help that says to use `int`. A conditional expression with a number or bool on one side and a literal `None` on the other (`7 if c else None`) is the optional; for other expressions the variable's type is written (`y: int | None = ... if c else None`; E287 says so).
  5. **Printing** a value is `None` or the number or bool (`print(x)`, `str(x)`, f-strings, inside `list[int | None]` and the other containers).
  6. **`d.get(key)` gives `V | None` for every value type** `V` of a dict that can be held in an optional: `int`, `float`, `bool`, text, lists, dicts, sets, objects, interfaces, tuples. E380 stays for the value types that still cannot be `None` (a union, an optional, a function): `get(key, default)` or `key in d` first.
  7. **Errors:** E459 stays for the types that still cannot be combined with `None` (fixed-width integers, functions, tasks) and its text now lists `int`, `float` and `bool` among the types that can be `None`; E380 stays as above; no id is retired.
- Alternatives: (a) a general union with `None` as a member and a tag (the representation D23g's reading suggests): rejected because the null pointer already means `None` for the other optionals and every `is None` test, field default and zero-initialised slot relies on it; a tagged `None` would need a second check everywhere and a box allocation for `None` itself. (b) A flat two-word value (a tag and the number) passed by value: rejected, since lists, dicts, fields and frames all assume one word per value and the runtime frees by pointer. (c) A sentinel inside `int`, the way the language itself would encode it: rejected, because no `int` value is spare (`int` has no limit) and `float` and `bool` have none either.
- Cost: a box (32 bytes) is allocated each time a number or bool becomes an optional, and each use takes the value out again (a number copy for a big integer or a boxed float); a loop that does arithmetic on an `int | None` pays that, so keep hot values `int` and use the optional at the edges. A `None` stays free. `list[int | None]`, `xs.index(None)` and `None in xs` are as unsupported as for `list[str | None]` (E101). Entry-point inputs of these types are still E157 (D28b).
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes D23g and D23b for `int`, `float` and `bool` (their "only for heap types" status lines are updated); changes P87 (`d.get(key)` for numbers and bools); the TODO item and design review 3.1 are done.

### D94b — The project layout and the command line: `src/`, `tests/`, `builds/`, `tomato init`, `[test-seeds]`, built programs that take `[ENTRY [INPUTS...]]`, raw inputs and `File` inputs: accepted (Davis, 26 and 27 November 2026; the choices marked "agent" are not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: Davis's decisions of 26 and 27 November 2026, relayed by his lead agent in the request of the task "Project layout and CLI". It supersedes D92b (per-entry seed tables) and changes D92a item 3, D92c item 3 (`tomato build ENTRY`) and D95a (the entry tables, the lock's `entries`, the flat-folder lookup). Built here: `compiler/src/manifest.tom`, `seeds.tom`, `ctool.tom`, the entry code of `compiler/src/compiler.tom`, `runtime/tomato_rt.c`, `native/tomato`. The sheet is `docs/manifest.md`.
- Decision:
  1. **Layout.** In every project and every seed the code is in `src/`, the tests are in `tests/`, what a build writes is in `builds/` (never part of a build, left out of git, and left out of a seed's content hash), and the manifest is `tomato.toml` at the root. A project's program is `src/main.tom`. The folders of the Tomato repository itself are not moved by this decision (a later task reorganizes them).
  2. **One table for test seeds.** `[seeds.ENTRY]` is gone everywhere (D92b, the design, the reader, the lock). `[seeds]` stays the seeds of every build; **`[test-seeds]`** is the seeds only tests use, in the same forms as `[seeds]`. There is no `[dev-seeds]`. A seed's own `[test-seeds]` is read only by `tomato test` in that seed, never by whoever uses the seed. The reader refuses `[seeds.x]` (and `[test-seeds.x]`) with E590, which points at `[test-seeds]`. The lock marks a seed only `[test-seeds]` lists with `test_only = true` (it replaces `entries`). A build uses `[seeds]` and, with the option `--test-seeds` (which `tomato test` passes; `run`, `build` and `compile` take it too), `[test-seeds]` as well. Agent: the option, not the entry name `test`, selects them, because `tomato test` builds each file as an ordinary program and a test file run by hand should be able to see its seeds.
  3. **`tomato init [NAME] [--seed]`** writes `tomato.toml` (`[project]` with `name` (default: the folder's name), `version = "0.1.0"` and `tomato` = the major and minor of the compiler that wrote it; for `--seed`, `[seed]` with a placeholder owner, description and license), `src/main.tom` (hello world with `@main`), `tests/example.tom` (each file in `tests/` is a test: a program that passes when it runs without raising; it uses `assert`) and a `.gitignore` with `builds/`. It writes nothing and names the files when any of them exists. Agent: a seed has no `main`, so `--seed` writes `src/STEM.tom` (the module rule of design section 5, `stem.tom`, would reject `main.tom`) and a test that imports it; a pre-release compiler writes its whole version (`1.1.0-rc.1`) because `1.1` is newer than it and the project would not build.
  4. **`tomato run`.** No word runs the project's `src/main.tom` (the nearest `tomato.toml` upward gives the project's folder). `tomato run X ...`: X is a FILE when it ends in `.tom` or has a `/`, otherwise it is an ENTRY of the project's main file (dotted names allowed) and the words after it are its INPUTS. `tomato run FILE [ENTRY [INPUTS...]]` is the full form. A `main` that takes inputs gives each one a default, so `tomato run` with no words always works; when it does not, the program stops with the runtime's message ("the entry point takes 1 input(s), got 0 (an input can be left out only if it has a default, ...)"). The check is when the program starts, not when it compiles, because programs with `main(n: int)` and a number given on the command line (the benchmarks) are fine.
  5. **`tomato build [FILE]`** builds one file, `src/main.tom` by default, into `builds/NAME`: NAME is the project's `name` for its `src/main.tom` and the file's name without `.tom` otherwise, and `builds/` is in the project's folder (the current folder when there is no project). `[build.ENTRY] output` and `mode` (D92c) and the flags `-o` and `--mode` still apply, the flags winning. **The built program takes `[ENTRY [INPUTS...]]`**: no word runs `main`, a first word that names an entry runs that entry with the words after it as its inputs, and any other first word is the error E591 that lists the entries. Every entry marker (and every dotted beginning of one, `greet` for `@greet.loud`, which runs all `@greet.*` as `tomato run FILE greet` does) is in the program, except the tests; a name that would run several functions is an entry only when none takes inputs. `sys.argv()` is the program's name followed by the inputs, as it was. `tomato build --entry NAME` still makes a program with only that entry, taking just its inputs (what `tomato test` builds; behavior kept). `tomato run` compiles for the one entry and runs it, which is the same behavior without the other entries. The old rule (the built program's words are the inputs of `main`) is gone: `./fib 30` is now `./fib main 30`.
  6. **Inputs are raw.** An entry takes all its inputs as text with `*args: tuple[str]` (and `sys.argv()` has them too). Everything after the FILE or the ENTRY on the command line is input: `tomato` reads no flag there, in `run` and in the built program. Its own options go before the entry (`tomato run --mode release f.tom greet bob`; between a FILE and its ENTRY is still fine), and `--` ends the options earlier (`tomato run f.tom -- main -o` runs `main` with the input `-o`).
  7. **`File` inputs.** An entry parameter typed `File` (`def main(data: File)`) gets a file the runner opens for reading as text from the input word (`-` is standard input) before the entry runs, and closes when the entry returns. A file that cannot be opened stops the program, before the entry runs, with a message that names the input (E592: `input 1 must be a file that can be read, but 'x.txt' cannot be opened: No such file or directory`). Text mode only. Agent: a `File` input has **no default** (a compile error says so) and `File | None` is not an input type: there is no file to fall back on, and a missing file is a mistake to stop on; so a `main` with a `File` input cannot run with no words. `tomato test FILE ENTRY INPUTS...` (D97a) builds the file with `--entry ENTRY` and runs it with the words after the entry, so a test entry with a `File` input gets the file the same way. Interplay (settled in a later change): `tomato test` reads its own options (`--filter`, `--mode`, `--timeout`, `-v`, `--color`, `--no-warnings`) only before the first FILE; after the file or its entry every word, even one that starts with `--`, is a raw input, and `--` ends the options earlier (so `tomato test x.tom e --filter` passes `--filter` to `e`); an option-like word right after a file with no entry is an error.
  8. **Local path seeds follow the layout.** A seed's modules are `src/*.tom` (they already were: `seed_modules`, the module map). A program looks for `import m` next to itself, then in the project's `src/` folder, then in the seeds, then in `std/`, so a test in `tests/` imports the modules of `src/` by name. `main` is the program's own module name, so it cannot be imported (a compile error says so): shared code goes in another file.
  9. **Foreign declarations in seeds (a default Davis leaned to, "like in Python you can have C"; easy to change).** A seed's modules **may** use `!name(...)` foreign declarations; the manifest code never rejected them, and the design's "no foreign declarations" check is dropped. `tomato seed add` will list the symbols a seed declares and ask once; the answer is recorded in `tomato.lock`, so a new symbol in an update asks again. A seed still cannot ship C or run build scripts in 1.0 (open: C sources or objects in a seed, after 1.0). Nothing of this is built (there is no `seed add` yet); only the design text and the TODO.md item changed.
- Alternatives: keep `[seeds.ENTRY]` tables (D92b; one table for every entry name, with rules for which apply, for a use that is almost only tests); a `[dev-seeds]` (the name D92b removed); a project name in the build's output chosen from the folder (the manifest's name is the one the user wrote); the first word of a built program as `main`'s input when it is no entry (kept the old convention, but a mistyped entry would run `main` with a wrong input and say nothing).
- Cost: a built program's command line changed (every use of `./prog INPUT` for a `main` with inputs, in the benchmarks and the tests, is now `./prog main INPUT`); `tomato run NAME` for a file with no `.tom` and no `/` now means an entry; options after an entry are inputs; a program holds all its entries, so it is a little larger and every entry's inputs are checked when the program is built; a file with `main` and a `File` input has no default way to run. `tomato test` is not changed beyond `--test-seeds` and the order of the options (D97a).
- Supersedes / superseded by: supersedes D92b; changes D92a item 3 (already replaced by D92b), D92c item 3 (`tomato build ENTRY`) and the lookup, the lock and the entry tables of D95a (items 1, 3 and 4, choice e).

### D95a — The manifest in code: strict reader, `[build]`, local path seeds and `tomato.lock` (stage 1, no registry): built (the shape is D92a to D92c; the choices below are the task's, to be confirmed by Davis)

- Evidence: the lead agent's request of 2026-11-26 ("implement the `tomato.toml` manifest in code, the stage 1, no registry part") over D92a, D92b and sections 2 to 6 of `docs/package-registry-design.md`. The sheet is `docs/manifest.md`. D92c (the `[build]` tables) was implemented as described in the request: `mode` and `output` only, `target` reserved, flags override the file, `[build]` read only in a project.
- Built:
  1. **Reader.** `compiler/src/manifest.tom` reads `tomato.toml` with `std/toml` and checks everything but `[limits]` (still `config.tom`): `[project]` or `[seed]` (never both), `[seeds]` and `[seeds.ENTRY]` (the keys with a slash are seeds, the others are entry tables, so the follow-up "allow `seeds.*` sub-tables" is done), `[build]` and `[build.ENTRY]`, `[seed-store]`. Unknown tables and keys are errors (`ConfigError`, E555 to E568, with the file's path first). `tomatoc` reads it whenever a manifest is found, so `run`, `build`, `compile`, `test`, `config` and `seed` all stop on a mistake.
  2. **The `tomato` key** is a minimum checked against `tomato version` (the text of the `VERSION` file, handed to the compiler in `TOMATO_VERSION` for the command only; the browser and a bare compiler use `sys.version()`): E562. Versions are `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` with an optional SemVer pre-release and no `+build` (E560); the `tomato` key may be `MAJOR.MINOR`. The order is SemVer's, in Tomato (`compare_versions`), checked against `semver_cmp` of `native/tomato` on every pair of a list.
  3. **`[build]` and `[build.ENTRY]`** give `mode` and `output`; the flags `--mode` and `-o` win. `[build.T]` applies to a run of entry `x` when `T` is `x` or a prefix of it, the longer table winning (the tables below `x` do not apply to a build). `output` is relative to the manifest's folder and its folder is made; `run`, `test` and `compile` ignore it. The `[build]` of a `[seed]` is read, checked and ignored; a used seed's is never read. `target` is E566. `native/tomato` gets the settled mode and output from `;% mode` and `;% output` lines at the end of the IR (so one compiler run reads the manifest once); `tomato compile` removes them.
  4. **Local seeds.** `tomato seed install [--locked]`, `tomato seed check` and `tomato seed hash [FOLDER]` (the compiler does the work through the entry `--seed`, as it does `--config`). `install` resolves the `{ path = ... }` seeds of every table (the union, D92b item 5) into `tomato.lock` (`compiler/src/seeds.tom`): the seed's own `[seed]` name, version and modules, the content hash of section 6 (seed hash v1, a `.git` folder left out, a symlink refused), `source = "path"`, `path`, and `entries` for a seed `[seeds]` does not list. `git` seeds and registry versions stop with E570 and E571 ("not supported yet"): no fetch code exists, and the request ruled out new infrastructure. A build reads the lock, checks it against the manifest (E569 "run: tomato seed install") and gives the compiler the module map; `read_module` looks in the program's folder, then the module map, then `std/`; a module in a seed and in `std/` is a compile error naming both (E581); a seed's code is named in errors (`acme.example/thing 0.3.0: src/thing.tom:2:5`) and its warnings are not shown.
- Choices to confirm (Davis):
  - (a) **A settings-only file** (neither `[project]` nor `[seed]`, the D77g shape) stays valid and is read as a project's, so `[build]` and `[limits]` work without a `[project]`; `[seeds]` needs one of the two.
  - (b) **A `[seed]`'s `[build]` is ignored** (read and checked, `tomato config` says so), following "`[build]` read only in a project", even when `tomato test` runs inside the seed's own folder.
  - (c) **A path seed is not re-hashed by a build** (it is a working folder; a changed file would otherwise need `tomato seed install` before every run). The hash is checked by `install --locked`. A seed from a store would be re-checked.
  - (d) **The key under `[seeds]` must equal the seed's own `[seed] name`**, and a path seed that itself uses seeds is refused until there is a registry (E573, E574), so a graph never needs resolving.
  - (e) **Entry tables below the entry** (`[seeds.test.parse]` for `test`) are read, as D92b proposed (not yet confirmed).
  - (f) **Names**: an owner is lowercase letters, digits, dots and hyphens (`local/thing` is fine for a path seed); `tomato seed check` additionally wants a domain with a dot or `k-` and a key hash (E580). The module rule of section 5 (`stem.tom` and `stem_*.tom`) is enforced at install and check time.
  - (g) The lock has no `index` field and a seed's `seeds` is always `[]` until a registry exists; the `[seed-store] vendor` setting is read and checked but nothing uses it.
- Alternatives: a separate `tomato-seed` helper program (design section 14; more install and release machinery for work that needs no process, and the compiler already has `std/toml` and `std/hashlib`); a second `tomatoc` run to read the settings (one more process start per command); the module map as a file (section 5; the compiler gets the lines as an argument list instead, nothing is written); recomputing the hash at every build.
- Cost: `tomatoc` grows by `std/toml`, `std/hashlib`, `std/os` and the two new files; a stray `tomato.toml` with other tables in a parent folder now stops builds (it already did for a bad `[limits]`); the compiler's `mode` argument can be empty (the manifest or `debug` decides).
- Supersedes / superseded by: implements D92a, D92b and the D92c shape; D77g's "other tables are ignored" (choice d of D77g) is replaced by the check here for the project's file (the user's `config.toml` is still not checked for other tables). The follow-ups "allow `seeds.*` sub-tables" and the manifest part of "Answer the seed registry open questions and start stage 1" are done. **Changed by D94b (27 November 2026):** the entry tables `[seeds.ENTRY]` (items 1, 3 and 4, choice e) are replaced by `[test-seeds]`, the lock's `entries` by `test_only`, the flat lookup by the `src/` folder, and a seed's content hash leaves out a top-level `builds/`.

### D96a — An entry marker marks exactly one function, and entry names may be dotted (`@test.parse_empty`, `@greet.loud`): accepted (Davis, 26 November 2026)

Changes D81a (`@test` may mark any number of functions: the one carve-out of the rule that an entry name is written once) and P21 of `docs/provisional.md`. D16a settles the idea that `@name` makes an entry point; D28a that `tomato run FILE ENTRY` chooses it by name. This keeps both and makes the name a path.

- **Rule.** An `@` entry marker marks **exactly one** function, and its name may have dots: `@x`, `@x.y`, `@x.y.z` (a dot must be followed by a name; `@greet.` is a syntax error). The same full marker on two functions of the main file is a compile error, **E583** (`'one' and 'two' are both marked @test`, at the second function), whichever entry is run. Before, two functions with one marker silently ran the last one (the `@test` carve-out of D81a aside). A bare repeated `@test` is no longer allowed: a program that has one gets E583 and fixes it by naming each test (`@test.adds`, `@test.parses`).
- **Selecting by entry name.** The entry name `N` (`tomato run FILE N`, `tomato build --entry N`, the browser's entry name) selects the functions whose marker is `N` or starts with `N.`, in the order they are written in the file. So `greet` selects `@greet`, `@greet.loud` and `@greet.loud.twice`, `greet.loud` selects `@greet.loud` and `@greet.loud.twice`, and neither selects `@greetx` or `@greet_all` (the dot decides, not a prefix of letters). Nothing selected is the old error (E228, `no function is marked @greet.whisper`).
- **A bare `@x` together with `@x.y`: allowed.** Running `x` runs `@x` and every `@x.*` (in the order written); running `x.y` runs only that one. Chosen over making it an error (a family `@test` plus `@test.slow` is natural to write, and the rule above gives it one meaning with no special case) and over `x` running only the bare one (then there would be no way to run the family).
- **What a selection runs.** One function selected: it is the entry point as before, so it may take inputs (`tomato run FILE calc 5`) and may be `async`. Several selected: the compiler appends a driver as source (as it does for tests, so no IR is written by hand) that calls each in the order written, one after another; an error that nothing catches ends the program, as in any program. Each must take no inputs and cannot be async (**E584**: `the function 'loud' is marked @greet.loud, and the entry 'greet' runs several functions, so it must take no inputs and cannot be async`), and returns nothing (E175, as for any entry point). Run one with its own name to give it inputs.
- **Tests.** (Changed by D97a: the selector is now the word after the file, `tomato test FILE test.NAME`, and `tomato test` has no `--entry`.) The entry `test` selects `@test` and `@test.*`, and `test.NAME` selects one test (or a family, `test.parser` for `@test.parser.empty` and `@test.parser.nested`). `tomato test FILE... [--entry test.NAME] [--filter TEXT] [--mode MODE]`: `--entry` is the selector, as it is for `tomato build` and `tomato compile`, because the files are the positional words of `tomato test` and a name such as `test.parse_empty` could also be a file; `tomato run FILE test.parse_empty` is the same selection. Everything else of D81a stays: the printed name is the **function's** name (`ok   parse_empty`), `--filter` matches that name, the tests run in the order written, E520 (async), E521 (parameters or result) and E522 (`no function is marked @test`, for the entry `test`) keep their ids; E520 and E521 now name the marker (`is marked @test.adds`) because the text of the message has the marker in it (`std/errors.txt` lines 520 and 521, the same ids). `--entry` must be `test` or start with `test.`; `tomato test FILE --entry test.nothing` is E228.
- **Parsing.** `@` is followed by a name and then any number of `.name`; the marker is stored as `entry:x.y`. At the top level `@add`, `@init` and the other method markers stay an error (E96) when bare; with a dot (`@add.fast`) they were an ordinary entry name (**changed by D113a: now E613**). After `import m` the lexer already joins `m.x` into one name, and the result is the same marker.
- **Chosen over** keeping `@test` repeatable and adding dotted names (two rules for one marker, and a file could not name a test to run alone), over `@test('name')` (a call form no other marker has; the entry name on the command line would still need a path), over a separate `@suite` marker (a second kind of marker to document for what a path expresses), and over discovering tests by function name (no `@` at all; the language marks things explicitly). The dot is one new token in a marker, the command line stays `FILE ENTRY`, and a name can be nested as deep as a project wants.
- **Cost.** A marker is no longer one name: the syntax highlighter (`compiler/src/hltomato.tom`) colors the whole dotted name. Nothing is discovered: the compiler looks only at the main file, as for D81a, so `@test.x` in an imported module is not run. Marking a function with `@greet.loud` still reserves `greet` as an entry name, as D16a does for `@greet`.
- **Migration.** Every repeated `@test` in the repository (the `tests/*_logic.tom` files, the printf'd programs of `tests/run.sh`, the negative tests, the comments of `std/testing.tom`, the docs) is now `@test.NAME` with NAME the function's name. No example, std file or playground sample used a repeated `@test`. No release had D81a (1.0.0 is a draft), so the changelog has no Breaking change for it.
- Built with a compiler change (`entry_driver` in `compiler/src/compiler.tom` replaces `test_driver`; the parser; the highlighter), so `compiler/bootstrap/tomatoc.ll` and `site/wasm/*.wasm` are regenerated. Tests: `tests/negative/` `test_bare_repeated`, `entry_main_repeated`, `entry_dotted_repeated`, `entry_group_with_input`, `entry_group_async`, `entry_group_result`, `entry_dotted_unmatched`, `entry_dotted_unmatched_test`, `entry_marker_dot_end` (all in the rejected-programs section of `tests/run.sh`); the `tomato run` and `tomato test` checks of command line part 4 (a family, one member, `greetx` not selected, a lone function with inputs, release mode, `tomato build --entry`, `--entry`, `--filter` inside an entry, bare `@test` with `@test.*`, an unknown or non-test `--entry`); the browser's `test.adds` entry; the highlighter's dotted decorator in `tests/hltomato_logic.tom`.

### D97a — `tomato test` for 1.0: a file is a test, a process for each file, `FILE ENTRY INPUTS...`, a line for CI; better `assert_equal` diffs: accepted (Davis's direction of 27 November 2026, relayed by his lead agent; the choices marked "agent" are not yet confirmed)

D81a built `tomato test FILE...` as a runner of functions marked `@test` and the `testing` module. This makes them ready for seeds and CI. Davis's direction replaced the first plan of the task (discovery of files that have `@test` functions, `@setup` and `@teardown`, selection of one function): the unit of a test is a **file**. `docs/testing.md` is the guide. No language feature is added; the work is the shell command and Tomato code in `std/testing.tom` (no compiler or runtime change, so the bootstrap IR and the wasm files are not regenerated).

- **A file in `tests/` is one test.** It is a program (a function marked `@main`) that passes when it ends with status 0, which is when nothing was raised and nothing called `sys.exit` with another status; it fails with any error, exit status, signal, stack overflow, time-out, compile error or a file with no `@main`; it is skipped when it ends by an uncaught `skip()` (the command reads the error id E523 in the program's error output; the reason is shown in debug mode, not in release mode, whose errors carry no text). No `@test` marker is needed. Set-up and clean-up are code in the file (**no `@setup` or `@teardown`**, the first plan's choice, dropped: a file already is the unit, and a function value cannot carry state to a hook, D63a).
- **The command.** `tomato test` runs every `tests/*.tom` of the current folder; `tomato test FILE...` the files named; `tomato test FILE ENTRY [INPUTS...]` one file with an entry and the inputs of that entry's function, built and run as `tomato run FILE ENTRY INPUTS...` is (the entry may be `test`, which runs the file's `@test` functions, or a dotted `test.NAME`, D96a). **The argument rule:** a word that ends in `.tom` or contains `/` is a file (a folder stands for its `*.tom` files); a word that is not a file is accepted only right after a file, as its ENTRY, and every word after the entry is an input; any other bare word is an error that suggests `x.tom` (`tomato test x` does not look in `tests/`). With no ENTRY a file runs as a whole and takes no inputs, so **a test's `main` may take inputs only if each has a default**: a `main` with an input that has none fails with the runtime's message. A file whose name starts with `_` is a helper module and is left out of a folder or of the no-argument run (a file named by hand is always run; **agent**: the first name that is not a test needs a rule, and this one needs no list). Chosen over `tests/x` for a bare name (Davis: it must be an error), a selector word for a function (Davis: no selector form) and a `--entry` option.
- **One process for each file.** Each file is built once (`tomato build --entry ENTRY`, its warnings and errors captured) and the program is run with its output captured; a `sys.exit`, a crash or a hang in one file cannot hide another. The tests of one file with `@test` functions share its process, as in D81a. Chosen over a process for each function (a start for every test, and the driver would need a selector).
- **Output and status.** One line for each file (`ok   FILE`, `skip FILE  (reason)`, `FAIL FILE  (why)`), the output of a failed file indented under its line (and of every file with `-v`), and the last line `tomato test: N files, P passed, F failed, S skipped: ok` or `: FAILED` (a line for CI, always printed). The status is 0 when no file failed, else 1 (also for an unreadable file, an unknown option or a bare word; 2 stays the usage message of an option with no value). `--filter TEXT` keeps the files whose name contains TEXT (no file kept: a warning and status 0, as D81a left a filter that matched nothing). **`--timeout N`** (optional, off by default) stops a file that has run for N whole seconds (TERM, then KILL a second later) and fails it; the compile is not counted. A file with `@test` functions reports what ended its run: `the program ended in NAME with status 0 (a test must not call sys.exit)`, through a small progress file the driver rewrites before each test (`--progress FILE`; the program's `--filter` and `--progress` are the only words its driver takes, and any other is an error with status 2).
- **Files with `@test` functions.** A file with functions marked `@test`/`@test.NAME` is run by the driver of the entry `test` (D81a/D92a), even if it has a `@main` too (which stays for `tomato run`; the manifest's `[build.test]` and `[seeds.test]` tables apply because the entry is `test`, D95a), and counts as one test: it passes when all its functions do, and its line says `(P passed, S skipped)`; a failure shows one `ok`/`skip`/`FAIL` line for each function under it. A file run as a program (`@main`, no `@test`) reads the tables of the entry `main`; test-only seeds for those files are for the later `[test-seeds]` task. `_run_all(names, tests, args)` is internal; D81a's output of the driver is unchanged. Nothing in the command line selects one function (the `--filter` on function names of the driver remains in `tomato run FILE test --filter TEXT`).
- **Diffs for `assert_equal` (agent).** `expected X, got Y` for short values; for text of more than 12 characters (or with a newline) the two texts under each other in a window of 60 characters with a caret under the first differing character (`values differ at character N (expected M characters, got K)`); for a list the first differing item, going down into nested lists and dicts (`lists differ at [1][2]: expected 5, got 4`; an extra or missing item); for a dict every missing, unexpected or changed key (up to 6, `dicts differ: key 'port' is missing (expected 80)`). The whole values follow when each is at most 60 characters. A `message` is added to the first line; the runner indents the other lines under the test name. `assert_equal[T]` cannot know `T` (the language has no type test and cannot print a list of `str | None`), so it works from `str()` of the values: a text at the top is not in quotes and a list or dict is recognized by its brackets (a text written like a list is read as one, and only shows a plainer difference); items inside a container are as a list prints them, found by a scanner that follows quotes and brackets.
- **Other changes to `testing` on the way (agent).** `assert_equal` and `assert_not_equal` raised "None where a value is needed" (E10) when a value that may be `None` was `None`; now it is a failed assertion (`expected tom, got None`), and two `None` are equal. Two dicts have no `==` in the language, so a second overload of both names takes `dict[K, V]` (same keys, equal values, any order; declared first so that a call with a dict finds it: overloads are by the number of type parameters, D62b). Sets and tuples have no `==` either and stay unsupported. `assert_in`, `assert_not_in` and the order checks show values the way a list does (`'a' is not in ['b']`, which also makes an empty text visible). New: `assert_less`, `assert_less_equal`, `assert_greater`, `assert_greater_equal` (D81a had left them out because `<` on a type parameter was unverified; it works). `skip()` ends a file as skipped when nothing catches it.
- **Cost.** A file has a process and a build of its own, so a folder of many small tests is slower than one file of many `@test` functions (the build is most of the time). Text is not quoted at the top of a message. The time limit is for a whole file. A test that ends the process with output still in a C buffer loses that output (the driver flushes after every test line). `tomato test` reads only the current folder's `tests/`.
- **Tests.** `tests/programs/testing_diff` (every message above; debug, release, ASan, WebAssembly), `testing_lib` (quoting in `assert_in`/`assert_not_equal`), and the `tomato test` checks of `tests/run.sh` (`t_cli_test`, four parts: every form of the command line, the report, the status, the errors, `--filter`, `-v`, `--mode release`, a skipped file, a file that does not compile, a helper module, inputs and defaults, `sys.exit` in a test function, a stack overflow, a hang with and without `--timeout`).
- **Follow-ups (not done here).** The interplay of a `main` and `@test.*` functions in one file beyond "the `@test` functions win" (the `@main` of such a file is then only for `tomato run`), and test-only seeds for the files run as programs (`[seeds.test]` applies to the files with `@test` functions, the entry being `test`); a seed's own `tests/`, `src/`, `tomato init` and `[test-seeds]` (a later task); `--fail-fast`; `--list`; a per-test time limit inside a file; machine-readable output (JUnit/TAP); whether a `--filter` matching no file should exit 1; running files in parallel; async tests and `with assert_raises(...)` (need closures or async function values).

### D98a — `sys.run`: start a program from Tomato, with no shell: built (the choices below are the implementer's defaults, to be confirmed by Davis, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: Davis ("build is an entry in our code; also add `sys.run`"), relayed by his lead agent on 2026-11-27, so that the `tomato` command's own build steps (compiling C, calling `clang`, linking) can later be written as Tomato entries instead of shell scripts. Until now the standard library could not start a process (`docs/package-registry-design.md` section 14 and open question 4; `docs/next-features.md` item 15, whose `Popen` and async parts stay open).
- Decision (the defaults of the request, then what the implementation settled):
  1. **Home: `std/sys.tom`**, as `sys.run(args)`. `sys` is already "the process" (argv, exit, the standard streams), and the request names it; a new module `process` or `subprocess` was the alternative (no module-level values are involved either way, D49b). The cost is that every program that imports `os` (which imports `sys`) now compiles the few lines of `run` too.
  2. **Signature:** `run(args: list[str], capture: bool = True, cwd: str | None = None, env: dict[str, str] | None = None, stdin: str = '', check: bool = False, timeout: float | None = None) -> RunResult`. It starts the program `args[0]` with the arguments `args[1:]` **directly: there is no shell**, so every word reaches the program as it is (spaces, quotes, `$`, `*` and an empty word are not special) and no quoting is ever needed. A program name without a `/` is looked for in the `PATH` of **the environment the program gets** (as `execvp` does, `/usr/bin:/bin` when there is none); with `env={'PATH': ...}` that `PATH` is the one searched (Python's rule). The call waits for the program and blocks the whole Tomato program while it runs, as the `os` calls do (other tasks do not run; an `async` form belongs with `Popen`, see the follow-ups).
