  3. **`stdin` is the name, not `input`** (the request said `input`): `input` hides the builtin `input()` and the compiler warns about that (the warning would show in every program that imports `sys`). `stdin` also matches the `stdout` and `stderr` of the result.
  4. **The result is `RunResult` with `status`, `signal`, `stdout` and `stderr`.** `status` is the exit status of a program that exited, and **128 plus the signal number when a signal ended it** (the shell's rule, so `status == 0` is the one test for success, and `status != 0` catches a crash too); `signal` is that number, or 0 for a program that exited by itself. Chosen over Python's negative `returncode` (a negative status is not a valid `sys.exit` code and `status != 0` would still work, but a Tomato program that passes `status` on to `sys.exit` must not need a case) and over a separate field only (a caller would need to read two things to know that the run failed). The text is the program's bytes as UTF-8, **invalid bytes becoming U+FFFD** (the rule of `requests`); there is no `bytes` variant yet.
  5. **`capture=True` (the default)** reads the standard output and error into the result, and the program's standard input is empty (end of file at once, `/dev/null`) unless `stdin` is given, so a program that reads its input cannot wait for the keyboard. **`capture=False`** gives the program this program's own standard streams: the result's `stdout` and `stderr` are `''`, this program's buffered output (standard output and error and every open `File`) is flushed first, so the order on the screen is the order of the calls, and the program's standard input is this program's own unless `stdin` is given. With `stdin` given (in either mode) the text is written to the program's standard input and closed; a program that does not read it, or ends first, does not stop this program and does not kill it with `SIGPIPE` (a broken pipe is ignored).
  6. **Both streams are read with `poll`** while the input is written, so a program that fills both pipes (the output of a compiler, megabytes on each) cannot make either side wait for ever; nothing is limited except memory.
  7. **`cwd`** is the folder the program runs in (this program's own folder is not changed), **`env`** is a dict of variables **added to** the environment (this program's, with a name that is already set replaced; nothing is removed, and this program's own environment is not changed). A name that is empty or has `=`, a NUL anywhere, or an empty `cwd` or program is a `ValueError` (E512, E511, E586, E585).
  8. **`check=True`** raises **`ProcessError`** (a `RuntimeError`) when the status is not 0; its `message` is `'clang' exited with status 1: ` followed by the end of the standard error (the last 2000 characters) or `'x' was ended by signal 11`, and its fields hold `program`, `status`, `signal`, `stdout` and `stderr`. E588.
  9. **`timeout` (seconds, a `float`, more than 0, else E587)** is supported: poll waits at most until the deadline, then the program is killed with `SIGKILL`, waited for, and **`ProcessTimeout`** (a `ProcessError`, E589) is raised with `stdout` and `stderr` as far as they were read and `status` 137, `signal` 9. A timeout always raises, with or without `check` (there is no result to give). Only the program is killed, not what it started (killing a process group would stop the program from using the terminal when it inherits the streams); the pipes are not waited on past the deadline, so a grandchild that holds them cannot make the call overrun. Without a timeout, a grandchild that keeps a pipe open keeps `run()` waiting, as it does in Python.
  10. **A program that cannot be started raises the `os` classes**: `FileNotFoundError` (E505, `could not run 'name': No such file or directory`), `PermissionError` (E507: the file is not executable, or is a directory), `NotADirectoryError`, other `IOError` (E515) for the rest, and for a `cwd` that cannot be entered the same classes with `could not change to the directory 'DIR' to run 'name'`. Nothing has run when they are raised, and the program is **always waited for**: no child is left as a zombie, in debug and release mode, after a failed start, a timeout or an error. The reason is told to the parent through a close-on-exec pipe, so a missing program is a Tomato error and not a child that exits with status 127.
  11. **Not available in the browser:** `run` raises `RuntimeError` E514 (`run is not available in the browser playground (it cannot start a process)`), checked before anything else, as `os.putenv` does; the C function is a stub there. `site/wasm/*.wasm` and `site/wasm/std` were regenerated.
- Implementation: `tomato_run` in `runtime/tomato_rt.c` (`fork`, `execve` of the file found by this runtime's own `PATH` search so that the child's `PATH` decides, two or three close-on-exec pipes moved above descriptor 2, `poll`, `waitpid` retried on `EINTR`; in the child only `dup2`, `chdir`, `sigaction`, `sigprocmask`, `execve`, `write` and `_exit`; `SIGPIPE` is reset to the default in the child and ignored in this process during the call; the signal mask is cleared in the child). No compiler change. `native/tomato` is unchanged: replacing its shell steps is what this makes possible.
- Tests: `tests/programs/sys_run.tom` (debug, AddressSanitizer with leak checking, and release AddressSanitizer): success, a non-zero status, `check`, signals, both streams, 300 KB on each stream in both orders and 3,000 interleaved lines, 1 MB of input read and not read, `cwd`, `env` (added, replaced, `PATH`), `stdin`, inherit mode (output order, input), a missing program and folder, a file that cannot be run, every `ValueError`, timeouts (output kept, closed streams, inherit mode, unread input), and that no zombie and no descriptor is left over after 60 more runs; `tests/programs/os_browser.tom` for the browser's error.
- Alternatives: `os.run` (the name of open question 4: the process belongs with `argv` and `exit` in `sys`, and the request names `sys.run`); `posix_spawn` (the `chdir` of the child needs `posix_spawn_file_actions_addchdir_np`, an extension that is not on every system, and a failed `chdir` is not reported well); running `/bin/sh -c` (the quoting problems the request rules out); a `Popen`-style object with pipes (built as `Process`, D98b); returning the output as `list[int]` bytes (a second function later); raising for a non-zero status by default (Python does not; the build steps want to look at the status).
- Cost: `std/sys.tom` grows by about 100 lines that every `import os` carries; a program that starts programs is not portable to the browser; the child inherits this program's other open descriptors (files and sockets that are not close-on-exec: Python closes them; D98b closes them), and its stack guard and signal handlers are reset by `exec`.
- Follow-ups (not built here; the first two are D98b): an `async` run and `Popen` with streams (item 15 of `docs/next-features.md`; they need the scheduler's descriptor waits); closing the inherited descriptors in the child; `bytes` output; killing the child's whole process group on a timeout; `sys.getenv` was not added (`os.getenv` and `os.environ` already exist, D80a).
- Supersedes / superseded by: answers open question 4 of `docs/package-registry-design.md` (a run in `std` exists; the `tomato seed` split of section 14 is unchanged until Davis decides); refines D80a (`sys`).

### D99a — The user's `config.toml` may hold `[build] mode`; how `run`, `test` and `build` use the build tables; `seed check` warns about an empty `[build.ENTRY]`: built (the choices below are the implementer's, to be confirmed by Davis; easy to change, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: three open items of D92c and D95a (TODO.md, 2026-11-27) answered by the task "Build tables: usage, config defaults, seed check" under the lead agent's request. Built in `compiler/src/manifest.tom` (`user_build_mode`, `entry_markers_of`), `compiler/src/ctool.tom`, `compiler/src/seeds.tom`; tests in `tests/run.sh` (`t_cli_build_tables`, `t_cli_seeds`) and `tests/manifest_logic.tom`. The text is in `docs/manifest.md`.
- Decision:
  1. **Use of the tables is confirmed, and no gap was found.** `run`, `build`, `compile` and `test` read `[build]` and `[build.ENTRY]` for the entry they compile (`main` for a plain `run` or `build`, which holds every entry; `--entry X`; `tomato test` builds each file for `test` or the entry it was given). `mode` applies to all of them; `output` only to `build`, and only when there is no `-o`. A flag wins; the most specific table that is the entry or a prefix of it wins key by key (`[build.grp]` covers `grp.one`; `[build.grp.one]` does not cover `grp`; `[build.grp.o]` covers nothing); a seed's own `[build]` is ignored in its folder by all three. Each is now a test.
  2. **The user's `~/.tomato/config.toml` may hold `[build] mode`**, the lowest default: flag, then `[build.ENTRY]`, then the project's `[build]`, then the user's `mode`, then `debug`. It applies with no project, in a project with no `[build]` and in a seed's folder. It holds nothing else: `output` and `[build.ENTRY]` are E558 there (a path needs a project folder to be relative to, and an entry table is about one program), a bad mode is E565. `tomato config` shows it. Other tables of the user's file are still not checked (D95a).
  3. **`tomato seed check` warns** (a `warning:` line, status unchanged) for each `[build.ENTRY]` whose name is not an `@` marker, or the start of one, in a `.tom` file of the seed's `src/` or `tests/`.
- Alternatives: keep `[build]` only in a project (D92c item 5: simpler, but a developer who always wants release builds on a machine must repeat it in every project or pass `--mode`); allow `output` or entry tables in the user's file (no meaning without a project); make the empty `[build.ENTRY]` an error (a table for an entry added later, or a typo, should not stop a publish; the warning matches the plan for `[seeds.ENTRY]`).
- Cost: one more place that can set a mode (`tomato config` says where); the warning only looks at lines that begin with `@` in `src/` and `tests/` (a marker inside a generated file is not seen); a project's `tomato.toml` is still not warned about, since its entries live in files the build chooses.
- Supersedes / superseded by: changes D92c item 5 (the user's file does not hold `[build]`) to allow `mode`; fills the "warning for a `[build.ENTRY]`" of D95a's not-built list; D92c and D95a are otherwise unchanged.

### D100a — Seeds: short (bare) names are granted by a reviewed pull request, probably accepted; use and listing stay open and automatic: accepted direction (Davis, 2026-11-27); handles and host shortcuts proposed, pending his yes; the design is `docs/package-registry-design.md`, nothing is built

- Evidence: Davis (2026-11-27, relayed by his lead agent), in three steps. First "Named packages, no accounts, are invite only", with use staying open. Then a correction: it is a **pull request**, Homebrew-like, "you put in a pull request and it probably will be accepted", not a web of signed invitations and not inviter keys. Then a second correction: the pull request is **only for short names** (bare names such as `json5`, and handles if adopted); **listing in the default index is not by pull request and not invite only: it stays automatic** under the structural checks and the `policy.toml` ratchet of D89a, to which a star requirement or a similar signal may be added later. Davis also asked for `username/x` names and for git hosts other than GitHub (items 7 and 8, proposed).
- Accepted direction (D89a's items 1, 2, 4, 5 and 7 stand; its item 3 and 6 keep the automatic ramp):
  1. **Use stays open.** `owner/name` works with no approval, no accounts, any git host, no registry needed (section 18.2 of the design).
  2. **Listing stays automatic.** The default index lists a seed by the written rule of `policy.toml` (launch level 0, raised over time with a notice period, for new listings only, grandfathered). Nobody lists or delists one seed; a maintainer cannot either. Nothing in this decision changes D89a items 2 to 6.
  3. **A short name is granted by a reviewed pull request.** A **pull request** is any request to merge a change into the index repository, on whatever host the index lives (a forge's pull or merge request, a pushed branch, a patch); the design does not require GitHub or any account. The author runs `tomato seed request-name OWNER/NAME`, which writes a `[[request]]` signed by the **owner's** key (the only identity of the asker); sends it; automatic checks run (`tomato seed index-check`: the signature, that the seed is **listed**, the name rules, no lookalike short name, not held, a per-owner cap, and a future `[short_name]` floor in the policy); a **maintainer reviews lightly and usually accepts**, then signs a `[[name]]` statement in `names.toml`; a decline is a closed pull request with a reason, not a blacklist.
  4. **One seed per name, never first come and never by popularity.** The short name of a seed is its own name (`json5`); two seeds that want it are decided by the maintainer, guided by `POLICY.md` (the original over a fork, a maintained seed over an abandoned one). Renames: the name stays with the old seed until a maintainer moves it to the successor. A released name is held for 90 days before it can go to a different owner's seed.
  5. **Who maintains the index and what they can and cannot do.** A small named set of **maintainers**, at first Davis alone, each an ed25519 key listed in the index's `config.toml`; a client takes the keys from **its own configuration** for that registry, never from the index it reads. They can accept, decline, release or move a short name, change `policy.toml` and the maintainer set (by a signed pull request, with the notice rule), and put a notice on a seed. They **cannot** decide use, list or delist one named seed, delete or edit any statement (the index is append only), touch a lock, or give a name to an owner who did not ask. This answers open question 1 **in part**; whether maintainers may also add a narrow `malware` or `legal` yank is still open.
  6. **Resolution of a bare name.** `tomato seed add json5` is, in order: the user's alias; the default index's short name; else the **candidates list** with **listed seeds first** (score and signals) and the unlisted after, exit status 2. The manifest and the lock always hold the full `owner/name`, so releasing or moving a short name changes only what a later `add` means. Unlisting a seed (the policy's demotion) or releasing a name **never touches `owner/name` use or locked builds** (section 18.1 stands); a seed that is not listed keeps its short name but the name does not resolve until it is listed again.
  7. **Stars, later (not set now).** `policy.toml` gets `min_stars` and `min_endorsements` for `[listing]` and for a `[short_name]` floor, all 0 (off). A star count comes from the seed's own host as a signed `[[host]]` signal appended by the runner, is **read only when a seed is evaluated for listing or a short name**, and is no part of use or of a locked build. Setting a number is a tightening: the 60-day notice and grandfathering apply, and **raising the bar never unlists an existing seed without a notice period**.
  8. **Proposed, pending Davis's yes: handles** (`dave/thing`). A handle is an owner name with no dot, granted to **one key** by the same reviewed pull request (`request-handle`, a `[[handle]]` signed by a maintainer), lookalike-checked, held for 90 days after a release; the index entry for `dave/thing` holds the git URL on **any host**, pinned by commit and content hash. **Domain and key owners still work with no pull request**, and no one needs a handle. It changes open question 8 from "no handles" to "handles granted by pull request".
  9. **Proposed, pending Davis's yes: non-GitHub hosts and host shortcuts.** A seed's git URL is plain data and any host works (GitHub, GitLab, Codeberg, Sourcehut, self-hosted, a local path). The forms: the index's full or handle name; `tomato seed add --git URL --rev TAG|COMMIT` (unregistered); and shortcuts `gh:USER/REPO`, `gitlab:USER/REPO`, `codeberg:USER/REPO`, `srht:~USER/REPO` that `add` expands to the full https URL before anything else, with `[git-hosts]` in the user's configuration for more. **The manifest and the lock store the real URL and the content hash, never the shortcut or the handle.**
- Alternatives: signed invitations by existing inviter keys (the first form of this decision; dropped by Davis for a pull request, which needs no key web and matches Homebrew); a pull request for **all** listing (dropped by Davis's second correction: listing stays automatic); first-come short names (brings back squatting); short names by score or popularity (the original open question 7 recommendation; replaced by a person's decision, since the ramp's score is cheap to inflate); an account system (rejected earlier by Davis); trusting the maintainer keys the index itself publishes (rejected: a lying mirror could then forge a name, so the keys come from the user's configuration); an invitee getting power to invite others (irrelevant after the correction: there are maintainers, not inviters).
- Cost, stated plainly: a person is back in the loop for short names, so a maintainer's slowness or mistake costs an author a typing shortcut (never use or listing) and one maintainer is a single point of failure for names until a second exists; short names are a shared namespace with the squatting and lookalike risk of any such namespace, bounded by the automatic checks, the hold, and public, append-only decisions; a short name tells users "a maintainer looked", not "safe"; handles, if adopted, add a second shared namespace with the same costs; host shortcuts expand to fixed hosts, so a user who types the wrong shortcut gets an unregistered seed that `add` says is unregistered.
- Updated open questions of `docs/package-registry-design.md` section 19: 1 (partly answered), 6, 7 (decided: yes, by reviewed pull request, with details of mine to confirm), 8 (handles proposed), 9 (maintainers edit the policy), 10 (the ramp stays; stars may be added later), 11 (smaller stakes). Questions 2 to 5 are unchanged. No language rule changes and nothing is built.
- Supersedes / superseded by: supersedes D89a's alternative "a bare-name namespace from the listing is deferred, open question 7" (bare names now exist, by reviewed pull request) and its "no human decides" for short names only; D89a items on listing stand. Refines `docs/package-registry-design.md` sections 1.5, 1.7, 18.8 and 19.

### D98b — `sys.run` follow-ups: the child closes the descriptors it inherits, `sys.run_async`, `sys.spawn` and `Process`; and `inline_merge` leaves string constants alone: built (the choices below are the implementer's defaults, to be confirmed by Davis, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: three items left in `TODO.md` by D98a and by the debug link of `native/tomato` (the request of Davis's lead agent on 2026-11-27): the child of `sys.run` inherits this program's open files and sockets; `run` blocks every other task while a child runs, so the parallel test runner and long builds written in Tomato could not overlap child programs; and building the compiler itself in debug mode printed a clang `constant expression type mismatch` and fell back to the slow link.
- Decision, the child's descriptors:
  1. **The child gets its standard streams and nothing else.** After the `dup2`s and the `chdir` and before `execve`, every descriptor above 2 is closed except the pipe that reports a failed start (which is close-on-exec anyway). The parent lists its open descriptors **before** the fork (`opendir` of `/dev/fd`, or `/proc/self/fd`; with neither, every number up to the limit, at most 65,536), because the child may only call functions that are safe after a fork in a program that has threads; the child then calls only `close` for each (the same code on macOS and Linux). On Linux the child also calls `close_range(3, ~0)` (around the pipe it keeps), which takes a descriptor that another thread opened after the list was made and costs one system call; where `close_range` is missing or refused (a kernel before 5.9, a seccomp filter) the list does it. Rejected: a bounded loop of `close` over `sysconf(_SC_OPEN_MAX)` (a container's limit of a million makes every start cost tens of milliseconds); `closefrom` (not on macOS); marking every descriptor of the runtime close-on-exec (misses what a program's own foreign calls open). The reason it matters beyond hygiene: a program started while another one is running would otherwise hold the first one's input pipe open, so the first would not see the end of its input until the second ended.
- Decision, the async form and the `Process` object (the minimum for a parallel runner and long builds):
  2. **`await sys.run_async(args, capture=True, cwd=None, env=None, stdin='', check=False, timeout=None) -> RunResult`**: the same arguments, result and errors as `run` (`ProcessError`, `ProcessTimeout`, the `os` classes, the same ids), but the calling task waits without blocking the others, so `asyncio.gather([start sys.run_async(a), start sys.run_async(b)])` runs both programs at once. It is made of the pieces below (a task reads each stream, a task writes the input, a task for the timeout). **Cancelling it kills the program** (SIGKILL) and waits for it, so `asyncio.wait_for(start sys.run_async(...), 5)` leaves nothing running and no zombie; every helper task is finished and its error taken before the call returns or raises.
  3. **`sys.spawn(args, capture=True, cwd=None, env=None, stdin=False) -> Process`** starts a program and returns at once (it needs no wait, so it is a plain function). `stdin=True` makes the program's input a pipe; `capture=True` makes its output and error pipes (`capture=False` and `stdin=False` give the program this program's own streams, as in `run`; with `capture=True` and `stdin=False` its input is empty). `Process` has `pid`, `stdout` and `stderr` (`PipeReader`s), `status` and `signal` (set when it has ended), and `await write(text)`, `close_stdin()`, `await wait() -> int`, `done() -> bool`, `kill(signal=9)`, `stop()` (kill, wait for it without giving other tasks a turn, close everything: for clean-up code) and `close()` (give up the pipes; it does not stop the program). A `PipeReader` has `await read_line()` (the line with its `\n`, `''` at the end, as `File.readline` and `Socket.recv_line` do), `await read()` (everything to the end), `read_available()` (what has come, without waiting; a character that is not complete stays for the next call), `pull()`, `close()` and `piped`. Text is UTF-8 with invalid bytes becoming U+FFFD, as in `run`.
  4. **How it waits.** A pipe is waited on with the scheduler's descriptor wait (`_fd_wait`, the one the sockets use), so a task waiting for output costs nothing. The end of the program has no descriptor that macOS and Linux share (a Linux `pidfd` is not on macOS), so `wait()` looks with `waitpid(WNOHANG)` and sleeps between looks, 0.5 ms at first and then twice as long up to 20 ms; a program that has ended is noticed within 20 ms. Rejected: a `SIGCHLD` handler (a signal handler in a program with other users of signals, and a wake-up that needs the scheduler anyway); `pidfd_open` where it exists with the poll as the fallback (a second path to test; left as a possible speed-up). The reads are non-blocking, and what has been read stays in a buffer of the runtime until it is read (`PipeReader`), so a stream's pipe is emptied whenever its reader runs.
  5. **Only the stream that is being read is emptied** (the Python rule): a program that writes more than a pipe holds to a stream nobody is reading waits until someone reads it, so a program that writes much to both streams needs two tasks (`t = start p.stderr.read()`) or `run_async`. A first version of the tests found that this is not hidden; the comments say it. When the program has ended, `done()` and `wait()` close its input pipe and empty both streams into this program, so the descriptors go back at once and the text is still there to read; a stream is closed when it is read to its end, and the buffer of one that is never read stays until `close()`.
  6. **Errors:** a stream of a program started with `capture=False` raises `ValueError` E595; `write` without `stdin=True` or after `close_stdin()` is E596; a signal outside 1 to 64 is E597; a program that has closed its input makes `write` raise `IOError` (E515, `could not write to the program: it has closed its standard input`; a broken pipe never kills this program). In the browser `run_async` and `spawn` raise `RuntimeError` E514 like `run`.
  7. **Not built:** a `timeout` for `wait()` (`run_async` has it), `communicate`, `stdout` and `stderr` merged into one stream, `stdin` from a file, `bytes`, a process group, `pidfd`. A `Process` that is never waited for stays a zombie until this program ends, and one that is not killed runs on when this program ends (as in Python).
- Decision, `inline_merge`:
  8. **Only symbol references change.** The `awk` pass that renames the calls of `@tomato_X(` to `@tomato_inl_X(` now counts the double quotes before a match and leaves it alone inside a quoted string, so the constant `c"call void @tomato_retain(ptr\00"` of a program that prints such text stays what it is (before, its length changed, clang refused the merged file with `constant expression type mismatch`, and the build fell back to the link without the inline copies; the program was right, only slower; it showed when the compiler built itself in debug mode). Rejected: skipping the lines that start with `@` (a string can also be in an instruction or metadata, and the test is the quote). `t_cli_debug_link` builds a program with such strings in debug mode, checks that the output is right, that there is no warning, and that the merged IR keeps the strings and renames the calls.
- Implementation: `run_spawn` in `runtime/tomato_rt.c` (the fork and the exec, shared by `tomato_run` and `tomato_proc_spawn`), `PStream` (a read end, its buffer and whether it has ended), `tomato_proc_*` and `tomato_pstream_*` (wasm: stubs); `std/sys.tom` (`PipeReader`, `Process`, `spawn`, `run_async`; `run` and these share `_prepare` for the checks). Errors E595 to E597 were added to `std/errors.txt`. `compiler/bootstrap/tomatoc.ll`, `site/wasm/*.wasm` and `site/wasm/std` were regenerated.
- Tests: `tests/programs/sys_async.tom` (debug, AddressSanitizer with leak checking, release AddressSanitizer): everything `sys_run.tom` checks for `run_async` (status, signal, `check`, both streams of 300 KB, 1 MB of input, `cwd`, `env`, a missing program, every `ValueError`, timeouts), other tasks running while a program runs (it waits for a file that a task makes), three at once with `gather`, cancelling (the program is gone), `spawn` with a conversation with `cat`, `read`, `read_available` with a character cut in two, `kill` with 9 and 15, a closed input, every error, and that a started program does not hold another one's pipes (a program that ends only when the other has) and sees no descriptor above 2; no zombie and no descriptor is left after 60 runs. `tests/programs/sys_run.tom` gained the open-files check (it fails without the closing, and passes with `close_range` alone and with the list alone); `tests/programs/os_browser.tom` the browser's error for `spawn`.
- Alternatives: `Popen` as the name and a class with keyword arguments (a constructor takes no keyword arguments, and a function that returns the object does); one `Process` with `stdout` as a file-like `File` (a `File` is a `FILE*` and blocks); a single `communicate()` (what `run_async` is); making `spawn` async to start a reader task for each stream (it would remove the rule of 5, but every `Process` would carry two tasks that keep a program alive).
- Cost: `std/sys.tom` grows by about 300 lines that every `import os` compiles; the exit of a program is noticed by looking (within 20 ms); a stream nobody reads can stop the program that writes to it; a started program is not killed when this one ends.
- Follow-ups: see the overview of the task (`pidfd` on Linux, a `timeout` for `wait()`, `communicate`).
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes the follow-ups of D98a (the `async` form, `Popen` with pipes, closing inherited descriptors); the `inline_merge` fix refines the debug link of `docs/performance.md` ("What changed").

### D101a — Seeds from git and the seed store, `tomato seed add/remove/vendor`, and the question about foreign symbols: built (the choices below are the implementer's defaults, to be confirmed by Davis, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: the lead agent's request of 2026-11-27 (three TODO items: `seed add/remove/vendor` for local seeds, git seeds in a content-addressed store, and listing the foreign symbols a seed declares), over `docs/package-registry-design.md` sections 2, 3, 6, 12 and 15 (stage 3), D92a to D94b and D98a (`sys.run`). The sheet is `docs/manifest.md`. No registry, no network beyond `git`, no signatures.
- Built:
  1. **Commands.** `tomato seed add --path DIR | --git URL --rev COMMIT [--test] [--yes] [--offline]`, `seed remove NAME`, `seed vendor [--offline]`, and `seed install [--locked] [--offline] [--yes]`. The compiler does the work (the `--seed` entry of `ctool.tom`, as for D95a); `native/tomato` only reads the words and finds the manifest (one `case` arm, because `seed` was already its own arm). `add` takes the seed's name from the seed's own manifest, so `NAME[@VERSION]` (the registry form) is not accepted.
  2. **Editing the manifest** is a text edit (`compiler/src/manedit.tom`): lines of `[seeds]` and `[test-seeds]` are added, replaced or dropped and everything else (comments, blank lines, order, an existing line's spacing and trailing comment, CRLF) stays byte for byte. std/toml's `dumps` cannot do that, and a comment-preserving writer in std/toml was the alternative (a much larger change to a library for one caller). What is lost is not data but reach: a seed written with a dotted key or a multi-line value cannot be edited (`remove` refuses and changes nothing; `add` puts the file back and prints the line to write by hand, E603). The check is generic: the edited text is read again with the real reader and must equal the old requirements with exactly the intended change.
  3. **The fetch** is in Tomato (`compiler/src/seedstore.tom`) and starts `git` with `sys.run`, not in the shell as section 6 and 14 planned: the shell front end is to be replaced by Tomato (`docs/shell-to-tomato-plan.md`), `sys.run` exists for exactly this, and the code then has no second implementation. The steps are those of section 6 (scratch bare repository, `fetch --depth 1 URL COMMIT`, `ls-tree -r -z --long`, `cat-file blob`, no checkout, `GIT_ALLOW_PROTOCOL=https:ssh`, no hooks, `fsckObjects`, no prompt, `ext::` and a leading hyphen refused). The URL schemes are https, ssh, `file://`, `host:path` and an absolute folder (a local URL also allows the `file` protocol, so the tests and a user's own `--git /path` work; a relative folder is refused because it would depend on where the command runs). **The rev must be a full commit id** (the manifest names a commit; a tag moves). Limits chosen: 10 MB a file, 64 MB and 5,000 files a seed. A blob is written by `sh -c 'git cat-file blob "$1" > "$2"'` with the words passed as arguments, because `sys.run` returns text and would change other bytes.
  4. **The store** is `~/.tomato/seeds/store/ab/<hex>/` under `${TOMATO_HOME:-$HOME/.tomato}`; the scratch folders are `seeds/tmp/PID-N` and are always removed. A fetched tree is hashed with seed hash v1 and put in place only if the lock's hash (when it has one) matches; a mismatch is E601 and nothing is stored. The bare-clone cache `seeds/git/` of section 6 is not built (every fetch is `--depth 1` of one commit).
  5. **The lock** gains `source = "git"` with `git` and `commit`, and `symbols`. A build never fetches; it re-hashes the stored copy of a git seed (and every vendored copy) each time (a path seed is still not re-hashed, D95a choice c). `install` takes the lock's seeds from the store or a matching vendor copy before using git, so it works offline when they are there; `--offline` refuses to run git (E605).
  6. **Vendoring.** `seed vendor` needs a current lock, writes `VENDOR/owner/name` (beside, hash again, rename), and `[seed-store] vendor` (read-only until now) makes the module map use the vendor folder for every seed, path seeds included, so a vendored project builds with nothing else present. The default folder when the setting is absent is `vendor/` and the command says to set it (it does not edit `[seed-store]`). The setting must be a folder below the project's.
  7. **Foreign symbols (D94b item 9).** `add` and `install` parse every module of the seed and collect the `LlvmDecl` (`!name(...)`) names. Names that the old lock does not hold for that seed are listed with their module and line, and the command asks `[y/N]` on standard input (the shell tells the compiler whether it is a terminal; `input()` is async-only, so the answer is read from `/dev/stdin`). `--yes` accepts, no terminal without `--yes` refuses (E602) and changes nothing, `install --locked` refuses. Accepted names are `symbols` in the lock. A version that only drops names asks nothing. The answer is per seed and per name: an update that adds one name asks again for that name only.
- Choices to confirm (Davis):
  - (a) The fetch lives in the compiler, not the shell (item 3).
  - (b) Only a full commit id is accepted as `rev` (a tag is refused with the command that resolves it), and `add --git` has no `--tag`/`--name`.
  - (c) The store and a vendor copy are re-hashed by every build (cost: hashing a seed's files on each run; the files are small); a path seed is not.
  - (d) `add` for a seed already listed replaces its line (that is the update); there is no `seed update` yet.
  - (e) `--yes` is the only way to answer without a terminal (no environment variable), and a pipe is not a terminal.
  - (f) A seed's `symbols` are names only; the declared types are not recorded, so an update that changes a signature but not a name does not ask again.
  - (g) A module that does not parse stops `add` and `install` (the symbols cannot be found), with the parse error.
- Alternatives: a comment-preserving writer in std/toml; fetching in the shell (as the design said); `git archive` or `git clone` and a checkout (filters, attributes and hooks could change files; `archive` honours `export-ignore`); a global per-user list of accepted symbols (one answer would cover every project); accepting tags as `rev` and resolving them (a second source of truth that moves).
- Cost: `tomatoc` grows by two files (`manedit.tom`, `seedstore.tom`); a build with a git or vendored seed hashes its files every time; git must be installed to fetch (the error says so); ids E598 to E605 and E570 retired.
- Supersedes / superseded by: implements the stage 3 part of `docs/package-registry-design.md` (not `verify`, the bare-clone cache and a repository path) and D94b item 9's "nothing of this is built"; refines D95a choice (g) (`[seed-store] vendor` is used now) and E570.

### D103a — `tomato seed verify`, `tomato seed update` and the `path` of a git seed: built (the request is Davis's TODO item, relayed by his lead agent; the choices marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed by Davis, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: the TODO item "Add `tomato seed verify` and `tomato seed update`, and the `[[source]]` `path`", over `docs/package-registry-design.md` sections 3, 4, 6 and 12 and the work of D101a (add, remove, vendor, install, the store, the question about foreign symbols). No registry, no network beyond `git`, no signatures. The sheet is `docs/manifest.md`.
- Built:
  1. **`tomato seed verify`** hashes every copy of every locked seed again with seed hash v1 and compares it with the lock: a git seed's store folder, the vendor folder's copy of every seed when `[seed-store] vendor` is set, and the folder of a path seed. One line each (`ok`, `CHANGED`, `MISSING`, `skipped`), then a summary, or E606 and exit status 1 after all the lines when anything is missing or different (also a module the lock lists that the copy lacks). It never fetches or writes. Not built: `--inputs` and the revoked keys (no signatures).
  2. **`tomato seed update [NAME...] [--dry-run] [--yes]`** locks the named seeds, or all, again. What moves is a git seed's commit: a line may now carry `tag = "v1"` or `branch = "main"` beside its `rev` (`seed add --git URL --tag T|--branch B` looks the name up once and writes the commit as `rev`), and `update` asks git where that name is now, rewrites the `rev` of the line (both tables when it is in both) with the text editor of D101a, fetches and hashes the seed, and writes the new commit and hash into the lock. It shows version, commit and hash before and after (a `warning:` when the version went down, a `note:` when it left the compatible line, never a refusal), asks nothing for a version bump and asks again, by the existing consent rule, only for foreign symbols the lock does not hold; one refusal changes nothing in any seed. A git seed with no tag or branch is reported as pinned; a path seed is read again. `--dry-run` writes neither file.
  3. **The `path` of a git seed** (the design's `[[source]]` `path`, for a repository that holds several seeds): `{ git = "URL", rev = "COMMIT", path = "seeds/a" }` (`seed add --subdir`). The fetch keeps only the files below the folder and treats it as the seed's root; the rest of the repository is not checked; the hash is that of those files alone. The lock holds `path` beside `git` and `commit`.
  4. **The lock's format version.** A git `path` is the first thing a version 1 reader would misread (it would hash another tree for the same URL and commit), so the lock is written as `version = 2` when, and only when, an entry has one; every other lock stays version 1 byte for byte, the reader accepts 1 and 2, a version 1 lock with a git `path` is a damaged lock, and any other version is E578. (`symbols` of D101a was optional and harmless to an old reader, which is why it did not need a bump.)
- Choices to confirm (agent):
  - (a) A moving name is recorded in the manifest as `tag`/`branch` next to the commit, not as a rev that is a tag: the manifest and every build stay exact, and `update` is the only thing that follows a name (D101a (b) kept: `rev` is always a full commit id).
  - (b) `verify` also checks the working folder of a path seed and counts a change as a problem (the build does not check it, `install --locked` does); a folder that is gone while a vendor copy exists is only `skipped`.
  - (c) `update` of a seed with no tag or branch does nothing but say so (it does not move to the remote's HEAD); a version that goes down or leaves the compatible line is said, not refused (design section 4's "newest in their line" needs an index to mean anything).
  - (d) `--dry-run` may fetch a seed into the store (a cache named by hash that no lock uses) so that its version, hash and symbols can be shown; the manifest and the lock are never written.
  - (e) The flag for the folder is `--subdir` (`--path DIR` is the local-folder form of `add`); the key in the manifest and the lock is `path`, as in the design.
  - (f) The lock's version is 2 only when needed, rather than a bump of every lock; the alternative was an optional field read as `path` by version 1 (a wrong hash, found only by a build's E601).
- Alternatives: record `tag` only in the lock (the lock is not edited by hand and does not say what to follow); make `rev` accept tags (the manifest would not name a commit); a `[[source]]` table in tomato.lock (a second place for what `git`, `commit` and `path` already say); `update` that follows the remote HEAD; a separate command to move a tag.
- Cost: `tomatoc` grows by the two commands and the `path`, `tag` and `branch` keys; a lock with a git `path` needs this version or a later one; ids E606 (E598 widened).
- Tests: `tests/run.sh` `t_cli_seed_update` (local bare git repositories only: a moved annotated tag and a branch, `--dry-run`, the question about symbols and its rollback, one seed named, pinned seeds, two seeds in one repository, the unsafe forms of a path, the lock versions, `verify` on a changed, missing, edited, and vendored copy).
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes D101a's "not built" list (`verify`, a repository `path`; `update` as `add --rev`, which still works) and refines D101a (b) and (d).

### D102a — Optionals are narrowed by `is None` and `is not None` (locals and parameters), for as long as the test is known to hold: built (the request is Davis's, relayed by his lead agent, 2026-11-27; the choices marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: the TODO item "Flow narrowing of optionals by `is None` / `is not None`", opened with D94a (`int | None`, `float | None` and `bool | None`) and the `assert` note of D81a: `s + '!'` after `if s is None: return` was E190 (an optional is not a `str`), and `abs(x)` or `x + 1` on an `int | None` took the value out of its box and tested for `None` at every use, even right after the test. Python's type checkers (mypy, pyright) narrow the same way, and D55a already does for unions tested with `x in T`. Davis's lead agent asked for narrowing of locals and parameters of every optional type, with `and`/`or`/`not` and `while`, no narrowing of fields, and a rule that stays sound when the variable is assigned.
- Decision (the rule):
  1. **What can be narrowed.** A **local variable or parameter** whose type is `T | None`, `T` being any type that can be `None` (text, lists, dicts, sets, tuples, objects, interface values, `int`, `float` and `bool`). Never a field (`self.x`, `p.next`), a list or dict item, the result of a call, or a module-level name: another call, another name for the same object or another task may change them, so each use is checked as before.
  2. **What narrows.** The tests `x is None`, `x is not None`, `None is x` and `None is not x`. What a condition `C` shows when it is true, or when it is false: `x is not None` shows `x` is not `None` when true (and `x is None` when false); `not C` swaps true and false; `A and B` shows what both show when it is true and nothing when it is false; `A or B` shows what both show when it is false and nothing when it is true. `x == None` and `x != None` do not narrow (agent: the TODO item names `is`; they work as before).
  3. **Where it holds.** The variable has type `T` (the non-`None` type) in exactly these places: the body of an `if` (or `elif`) whose condition shows it when true; the `else` and the later `elif` conditions and bodies of an `if` whose earlier conditions show it when false; the right side of `A and B` when `A` shows it when true, and of `A or B` when `A` shows it when false; the true side of `V if C else W` when `C` shows it when true, the other side when false; the body of `while C:` when `C` shows it when true (the test runs again at every turn, so the loop head needs nothing more); and **the rest of the block after an `if`**, when every branch of the `if` that does not leave (by `return`, `raise`, `break` or `continue`) knows it at its end, an `if` with no `else` counting as a branch that knows what all its conditions being false shows. So `if x is None: return`, `if x is None: continue`, `if x is None: raise ...` and `assert x is not None` (the parser makes `assert` an `if` that raises) leave `x` narrowed to the end of the block. A branch that assigns the variable does not know it at its end.
  4. **Where it ends: at any statement that can assign the variable.** A statement that contains, at any depth, an assignment to the variable (`x = ...`, a loop variable, a `catch ... as x` name, a list comprehension's loop variable) is not run with the variable narrowed, except for a plain `x = value`: the value is worked out with `x` still narrowed (so `node = node.next` and `x = x + 1` are fine), and after the assignment `x` is `T | None` again, **whatever was assigned** (agent: assigning a value that is not `None` does not narrow: `if x is None: x = default` leaves `x` an optional afterwards; a later `if x is not None` or `assert` narrows it again. **Changed by D102b (2026-11-27):** an assignment of a value that is not `None` narrows from there, and the `if` statement no longer ends a narrowing at its start.) A narrowing that has ended is not restored by the end of the statement: the rest of the region sees the full optional. This is a rule about statements, not about the order of execution, so it holds in loops (a loop that assigns the variable anywhere in its body is never run with the variable narrowed from before the loop) and with `try`, `catch` and `finally`.
  5. **A narrowed variable is a `T`.** Operators, methods, arguments and `return` see `T`, so no unwrap is compiled and nothing can stop with "None where a value is needed" there. A test of it, `x is None`, `x is not None` or `x == None`, is still allowed inside the region (it is `False`, `True` and `False`), so code that tested again before still compiles. A variable made from it (`y = x`) has type `T`, as it would from any `T` (agent: Python's checkers do the same; before, `y` was `T | None`, so `y = None` later in the function now fails to compile with E324).
  6. **Representation.** An optional that is a text, list, dict, set, tuple, object or interface value is the same pointer as its `T` (D23b), so narrowing it changes only the type the compiler uses. An `int | None`, `float | None` or `bool | None` is a box (D94a): where the variable is narrowed its number is taken out once into a slot of its own (a copy of a big integer or a float of any size, as for a union member), and the uses read that.
- What is not narrowed (the list for later; the first item is done by D102b): by assignment of a non-`None` value (`if x is None: x = f()`), by `x == None`, by the end of a `while x is None:` loop, by `match`, by a conditional expression or `and`/`or` beyond the shapes of rule 2 (`(x is None) == flag`), and any field (rule 1). Each is sound to leave: the variable is then the optional it was declared as, and the old checks apply.
- Alternatives: (a) D55a's rule for unions, an assignment to a narrowed variable is a compile error: rejected, because the main use is `while node is not None: ... node = node.next`, and a rule that makes every loop over a linked list a compile error would not be used. (b) A full flow analysis with joins at every merge point and a fixpoint for loops (the variable is known to be non-`None` wherever every path says so, assignments of non-`None` values included): it narrows more (`if x is None: x = default`), but the compiler's slot types are fixed while a function is compiled, a join would need the value-box slots of `int | None` copied in and out of a slot of the narrowed type at every merge, and a mistake there is an unsafe read; the statement rule above is simple enough to read in a few lines and cannot give a wrong type. It can be extended later by making more places "know it at the end" (rule 3) without changing what already compiles. (c) Narrowing fields as well, as Python's checkers do while nothing intervenes: rejected, because any call can change a field, so the rule would need an analysis of calls (D12 and the borrow rules show how far that goes). (d) Narrowing `x == None` too: left out; it costs nothing to add.
- Cost: a program that copied a narrowed variable and then assigned `None` to the copy fails to compile (rule 5), and `if x is None: x = default` still needs the old unwrap (or a second variable; D102b removed this). Every use of a narrowed `int`, `float` or `bool` optional is cheaper and a use of the others needs no `None` test, but a narrowing of an `int | None` makes one copy of its number where the test is. The statement rule re-scans each statement of a narrowed region for assignments, which is cheap. In `std/` the changes are only in the generated IR (no source change was needed).
- Tests: `tests/programs/optional_narrowing.tom` (every optional type, every place of rule 3, `and`/`or`/`not`, `assert`, loops, conditional expressions, a linked list), `tests/programs/optional_narrowing_flow.tom` (assignment, nested assignments, loops that assign, a field, redundant tests, `try`, loop nests in release mode, a generator and an async function, a copy of a narrowed variable) in debug, AddressSanitizer, release mode and WebAssembly; `tests/negative/optional_narrow_*.tom` (a field, after an assignment, a nested assignment, no early exit, after the `if`, the right of `or`, after the loop, a loop that assigns, another variable, a copy) and `tests/runtime/optional_narrow_ended.tom` (the check comes back after an assignment).
- Supersedes / superseded by: changes D94a item 3 ("variables are not narrowed by `is None`"), the "Not covered" line of D55a for optionals (unions are as before), the `assert` remark of D81a, and P30 of `docs/provisional.md` ("a variable copied from an optional stays optional": a copy of a narrowed variable is a `T`); completes D23b and D23g's open "how a program narrows an optional to `T`".

### D108a — A module's own definitions shadow the built-ins; the names of the built-in types stay reserved: built (decided by Davis, 2026-11-27, language design review 1.4; the exact rules below are the implementer's, to be confirmed)

- Evidence: `def len(x: int) -> int` in a program was silently ignored and the built-in `len` still won (`len(3)` was E324, "the argument of len has type int, but str is expected"). After 1.0 every built-in added in 1.x (`zip` as a value, `bytes`, `isinstance`) would have taken over, without a word, the functions of that name that compiled before. The review (`docs/language-design-review.md` 1.4) offered two rules, shadowing (Python's) or a compile error for defining a built-in's name, and recommended shadowing; Davis decided on shadowing on 2026-11-27.
- Decision, the rules:
  1. **What shadows.** A top-level function (also a generic or an `async` one), class, interface or constant that the module defines (`export` or not), and a name the module brings in with `from m import name`, wins over a built-in of the same name **inside that module**. Another module that does not define or import the name still sees the built-in (a library's own `sum` is not seen by the program that imports the library's other names), and `import m` with `m.len(x)` is unaffected, as before. The program that defines `print` has no built-in `print` left in that module (write with `sys.write_stdout`); `len(3)` of the example above is a call of the module's `len` and compiles.
  2. **What can be shadowed.** Every built-in function and class: `print`, `open`, `input`, `clock`, `sleep` and `_`-names the compiler knows, `len`, `range` (the function and the class of `std/builtin/range.tom`), `hash`, `abs`, `chr`, `ord`, `round`, `divmod`, `any`, `all`, `sorted`, `reversed`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `map`, `filter`, `enumerate` and `zip` (see 6), the error classes (`Error`, `ValueError`, `AssertionError`, ...), `bitarray` and `frozenbitarray`, `Iterable` and `Sized`. Before this decision some of them already gave way in name lookup (`min`, `max`, `sum`, `sorted`, `hash`, `abs`, `input`, `sleep`, `clock`) and some did not (`print`, `open`, `len`, `range(...)` as a call and a loop, `str`, `int`, `float`); the error classes and `range` as a class gave way but shared a program-wide key with the built-in (rule 8). They now all do, except the types of rule 3.
  3. **The built-in types are reserved: defining one is a compile error** (E608, "'str' is the name of a built-in type, so it cannot be defined here", at the name in the `def`, `class`, `interface` or `const`): `int`, `uint`, `float`, `bool`, `str`, `File`, `list`, `dict`, `set`, `tuple`. A type name is written in annotations (`x: str`, `list[int]`, `x in list`), in conversions (`int(x)`, `str(x)`, `float(x)`) and is the type of every literal and of every f-string, so a module's own `str` could not replace it for the whole module: `x: str` would name the module's class while `'abc'` is still the built-in text, and the two would stop agreeing in the middle of a program. Reserving the names is also what keeps a conversion such as `int(x)` and an f-string meaning what they mean everywhere. Before, such a definition was silently ignored, like `len`'s; it is now an error that names the clash. The list is fixed: a built-in type added after 1.0 should be looked up after the module's own definitions, as `Iterable` and `Sized` are, so that adding it breaks nothing; only a name that the compiler hard-wires everywhere is added to the reserved list, and that is a breaking change of its minor version.
  4. **Variables.** (Changed by D125a, decided by Davis later on 2026-11-27: a local variable or parameter shadows a built-in, a function or a class of its name everywhere, calls included, and a variable named like a built-in type is E623.) Written for D108a: a local variable or a parameter named like a built-in was allowed, with the `shadow` warning ("the variable 'len' has the same name as a function or class, which it hides here"), and hid nothing in a call: after `len = 3`, `len([1, 2])` was still the built-in `len`.
  5. **Not names, so not affected.** Methods and fields (`x.len()`, `self.print`, a method called `max` in a class): a dot never looks at the names of the module. The markers of operators (`@len`, `@iter`, `@str`, `@lt`, ...) and of entry points (`@main`, `@test.x`) are words after an `@`, not names: a class with an `@len` method is measured by the built-in `len` where the module has none of its own, and by the module's `len` where it has. A constant that takes a built-in's name hides the built-in too, and calling it is E609 ("'len' is a constant, not a function, so it cannot be called"; it was "the function 'len' does not exist" for any constant).
  6. **What the compiler writes itself keeps the built-ins.** f-strings (`str` and `_format_spec`), `assert` (`AssertionError`), the length check of `a, b = xs` and of a `for` over tuples (`len`, `ValueError`, `str`), the loops `for i, x in enumerate(xs)` and `for a, b in zip(xs, ys)` (`range`, `len`) and comprehensions that use them are written by the lexer and the parser as names marked "core" (`Token.num` is 1 for the lexer's `str`; `Node.core` for the parser's), and `Gen.call` looks a core name up in the module `builtin` only, whatever the module defines (`Mod.resolve_core`). The helpers the compiler makes for printing, hashing, sorting, `sum`, `min` and `max`, `range` and the formats (`std/builtin/`, `helper_source`) are compiled in the module `builtin`, which never sees a program's names. The loops over `enumerate(xs)` and `zip(xs, ys)` are the one place where the parser, not the compiler, decides, because it writes them out as it reads them: a module that defines or imports `enumerate` or `zip` (found by looking through its tokens first, `top_level_names` in `compiler/src/parser.tom`) has its own, and `for a, b in zip(xs, ys)` then loops over the list of tuples that the module's `zip` returns, taken apart like any `for a, b in pairs`. `for i in range(n)`, `for line in open(path)` and the fast paths for `range` loops, `len` and loop nests are used only where the name is the built-in (`Gen.is_builtin`); a module that defines `range` or `len` gets no loop nest in release mode, which is slower, not different. The echo of a bare expression in `tomato repl` and the website playground is written `_core_print(expr)`: the lexer reads the name `_core_print` as the built-in `print`, marked core, so a session that defines its own `print` still shows values with the built-in (`native/tomato-repl`, `site/play.html`). The spelling exists for those two tools; in an ordinary program `_core_print` is just that.
  7. **The standard library.** Each `std/` module is a module of its own and sees its own definitions and the built-ins, as a program does, so a program's `len` is never seen by `json` or `re`. A std module that defines a built-in's name sees its own inside itself: `math` has `abs`, `min` and `max` (they already gave way: `gcd` calls `math.abs`) and `terminal` has `open` (nothing in it calls the built-in). `tests/programs/shadow_std.tom` defines 16 built-in names and runs 13 std modules.
  8. **Keys.** A main-program definition used to have the program-wide key of its plain name, the same key as a definition of `std/builtin/` of that name, so a program's `class range`, `class ValueError` or `def _range1` collided with the built-in one (E114 "has no field", E128 "'range' takes 3 arguments", an LLVM "invalid redefinition"). It now has the key `main.NAME` when the built-ins have that name (`Mod.key_for`); the type shows as `main.range` in a message. Every other key is as before, so no compiled program changes.
- Not done (follow-ups): the repl and the website's playground show the value of a bare expression by running `print(expr)`, written by a shell script and by JavaScript, so a session that defines its own `print` shows through it (a `print` that takes one text refuses a number); a local variable or parameter does not shadow a call (4), unlike Python's rule, so the warning's "which it hides here" is not true for calls of built-ins and the question is whether locals should shadow too (it could break a program that has a local `len` and calls `len(x)`).
- Alternatives: (a) Defining a built-in's name is a compile error (the other option of 1.4): then each built-in added in 1.x has to be checked against published programs, and the error only shows the clash; rejected for the reason in the review. (b) Shadowing for the types too: rejected, rule 3. (c) Shadowing only for the names the compiler looked up by `resolve` already: rejected, `len`, `print` and `open` are the examples of 1.4. (d) Marking a program's definition instead of the compiler's own names, by renaming it (`len` becomes `main.len` in the tree): rejected, the other modules reach a name as `m.len` and `from m import len` and an error message would show the renamed name. (e) The parser asks the compiler for the module's names (a two-pass parse) instead of looking through the tokens for `enumerate` and `zip`: rejected for the cost; the token scan finds the same names (the top level of a module has only definitions and imports).
- Cost: a program that defined `print`, `open`, `len` or `range` and was surprised that it was ignored now has it used (and its signature checked: E324, E128 for its calls); a program that defined a built-in type's name (`def str`, `class list`) now fails with E608 instead of being silently ignored. Programs that define none of these compile to the same code as before (the fixed point and every expected output are unchanged). A module that defines `range` or `len` loses the release-mode loop nest (rule 6). The compiler itself is unchanged in its source: it defines none of the built-in names.
- Tests: `tests/programs/shadow_functions.tom` (a recursive `len`, `max`, `zip`, `range`, `abs` and `sum` in one module that also uses f-strings with and without a format, `assert`, unpacking, comprehensions, `for ... in range` over the module's `range`, `enumerate`, the module's `zip` as a function and in a `for`, and the built-ins it does not define), `shadow_print.tom` (a recursive `print`, written with `sys.write_stdout`), `shadow_library.tom` with `shadow_library_lib.tom` (`from m import len, open`, a private `sum` that the program does not see, `max` that the library sees as the built-in), `shadow_classes.tom` (a class `range`, `AssertionError` and `ValueError`, an interface `sum`, constants `open` and `hash`, methods named like built-ins, `@len` and `@iter`, `assert` and unpacking still raising the built-in errors), `shadow_std.tom` (16 names and 13 std modules); they run in debug mode (`tests/programs.txt`), in release mode with AddressSanitizer (`shadow_functions`, `shadow_classes`) and through WebAssembly (all five); `tests/negative/shadow_type_function`, `shadow_type_class`, `shadow_type_const`, `shadow_type_interface` (E608), `shadow_len_own_signature` and `shadow_print_own_signature` (the module's own signature wins, E324 and E128) and `shadow_const_called` (E609), the first and the fifth also through WebAssembly.
- Supersedes / superseded by: settles `docs/language-design-review.md` 1.4 and the third item of its ranked list; it makes the rule of D24c ("each module has its own namespace") hold for the built-ins as well.
### D104a — The bare-clone cache for seed fetches, and four small `tomato` polish items: built (the request is Davis's TODO list, relayed by his lead agent, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: `seed add` and `seed update` fetched the tag and then the commit with `--depth 1` into a fresh scratch repository each time, so nothing was reused and a seed that was gone from the store needed the network again. Found while testing the CLI contract suite: `tomato init 'a\b'` wrote a backslash that TOML reads as an escape (the shell's `echo` unescaped `\\`); `tomato config` printed the report up to a wrong file's error on standard error; `--locked` after an edit of a path seed said only that the lock did not match the manifest; `--mode ""` was read as no flag.
- Decision:
  1. **The cache** is `${TOMATO_HOME:-$HOME/.tomato}/seeds/git/<first 40 hex of sha256 of the URL text>/`, one bare repository for each URL as written. Every fetch goes into it (`git fetch --depth 1`, the same options and checks as before), so a second fetch adds only new objects. A commit it already holds (kept under `refs/tomato/c-<commit>`) is read with no network, so `seed install` works with the repository gone and no store. A tag or branch is fetched into a ref of its own (`refs/tomato/<scratch name>`, removed after) and not FETCH_HEAD, so two commands at once do not read each other's answer. Files are still read from git's objects, never checked out, and the caller still hashes them against the lock, so the content-hash rules are unchanged: a damaged or edited clone can make a fetch fail or fall back, not change a locked seed.
  2. **Fallback.** When the kept clone cannot be made or used (a read-only store, a damaged folder), the fetch is done again in the scratch folder; if that works, the damaged clone is removed. A network error fails both and says the same as before. `gc.auto=0` for every git call, so no background gc runs in the store.
  3. **`tomato init`** writes the name with `printf`, so a backslash reaches the manifest as `\\`.
  4. **`tomato config`** prints a settings error alone: the compiler's report goes to standard output and the error to standard error, and the script shows only the error.
  5. **`--locked`** names the path seed and both hashes (short form) when the files of a path seed changed since the lock; any other mismatch has the old text. A build still does not check a path seed (a working folder, D95a (c)): intended.
  6. **`--mode ""`** is E282 in `run`, `build` and `compile` (`tomato test --mode ""` still means no flag).
- Alternatives: keep a full (not shallow) clone (much larger, and the commit-by-id fetch of a host that allows it is the same); a cache keyed by a readable name (URLs can hold anything); delete a damaged clone on any error (a network error would throw the cache away).
- Cost: the store folder grows by one shallow clone for each repository fetched (safe to delete).
- Tests: `tests/run.sh` (the seed fetch checks in `t_cli_seed_update`, and the changed `--locked` text), `tests/cli_contract/cases/{init,config,args,seed}.sh`.

### D105a — `progress_bar` writes through the standard error stream, never by opening `/dev/stderr`: built (2026-11-27) (numbered D101a until the third audit, which found that id used twice; D103a and D104a went to other work that landed meanwhile)
- Request: `from itertools import progress_bar` and a loop over it in `tomato repl` on macOS gave `error[E25]: could not open '/dev/stderr': No such file or directory`.
- Cause: the bar opened the path `/dev/stderr` for every redraw. That fails whenever the path is not there for this process (a closed or redirected descriptor 2, a system without `/dev/stderr`), and an uncaught error then ended the program for a progress display. The repl also runs the session with standard error captured in a file (it shows the session's errors itself), so even a bar that worked was never seen.
- Decision: the bar writes with the runtime's standard-error primitive (the one behind `sys.write_stderr`), which cannot fail. When standard error is a terminal the bar is one line redrawn with `\r`. When it is not (a pipe, a file, closed), it writes a plain line at the first update, then at each new tenth, and the finished bar once at the end, with no `\r`. The repl sets `TOMATO_PROGRESS_TTY=1` when its own standard error is a terminal, and a bar whose standard error is not a terminal then draws on `/dev/tty` through the terminal primitives (the stubs of the browser make it fall back to plain lines there). Standard output is never written.
- Other uses: `open('/dev/stdout')` and `open('/dev/stderr')` in a program that asks for them (`tests/programs/std_streams.tom`) stay; the runtime already gives a copy of the descriptor. No other file in `std/`, `examples/` or `tests/` opened `/dev/std*` or `/dev/tty` except `std/terminal.tom` (its job).
- Tests: `tests/run.sh` `t_progress_streams` (stdout clean, stderr to a pipe, a file, closed, and `/dev/full`) and `tests/repl_progress.exp` (the repl on a pseudo-terminal).
- Alternatives: `sys.write_stderr` from Tomato (needs `import sys` in `itertools` and still cannot reach the terminal in the repl); a new runtime primitive for a progress descriptor (needs a rebuilt browser runtime).
- Cost / limit: the repl runs the whole session again for each new piece, so an earlier bar is drawn again then (follow-up: skip progress for the part of the session already shown).

### D102b — An optional is narrowed by assigning it a value that is not `None`, and the end of an `if` joins what its branches know: built (the request is Davis's TODO item, relayed by his lead agent, 2026-11-27; the choices marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: the TODO item "Narrow an optional by assignment of a non-None value", the first item of D102a's "what is not narrowed" list. `if x is None: x = default` left `x` a `T | None` for the rest of the block, so `x + '!'` on a `str | None` was still E190 and an `int | None` was still unwrapped (and checked) at each use. Python's checkers narrow on assignment, and an `if` that gives a default is the commonest way to write one. Davis's lead agent asked for the narrowing to start at an assignment of a value that is certainly not `None`, to be kept at the end of an `if` only when every branch that stays keeps it (a branch that leaves counts as keeping it), and to stay conservative for loops, proved in tests under AddressSanitizer and in release mode.
- Decision (the rule, which adds to D102a and replaces its rule 4 where it says "after the assignment `x` is `T | None` again, whatever was assigned"; everything else of D102a stands):
  1. **What narrows.** A plain assignment statement `x = value` (`x += 1` is `x = x + 1`) to a local variable or parameter whose type is `T | None`, when the type of `value` is certainly not `None`: any type but `None`, an optional, a union that has `None` in it and a type that is not known (a call that gives no value). So `x = 'a'`, `x = f()` where `f` gives a `str`, `x = x + 1`, `x = y` where `y` is itself narrowed and `x = Node(1, x)` narrow; `x = None`, `x = t` where `t` is a `str | None`, `x = d.get(k)` and `x = a if c else None` do not, and the variable is the optional again as in D102a. The value is worked out as before, with `x` still narrowed when it was (`node = node.next` is unchanged). Declarations are not assignments for this rule (agent: `x: str | None = 'a'` does not narrow: it is the place where a program says that `x` will hold `None` later, and the copies `y = x` of a narrowed variable are `T`, so `y = None` later would stop compiling in programs that declare an optional with a first value; only the plain `x = value` the TODO item names narrows).
  2. **Where it holds.** From the assignment to the end of the block, until a statement that can assign the variable (D102a rule 4, unchanged: such a statement is not run with the variable narrowed, `x = value` apart, and what it leaves is the optional unless it narrows it again by this rule).
  3. **Joins.** At the end of an `if` the variable is narrowed to the end of the block when it is narrowed at the end of every branch that does not leave (by `return`, `raise`, `break` or `continue`), and in the branch that is not there (an `if` with no `else`, or the `else` that no condition reaches) as far as it was before the `if` or its conditions being false shows it. It is narrowed at the end of a branch when it was narrowed at the start of the branch (before the `if`, or by the conditions that lead to it, D102a) and no statement of the branch ended it, or when the branch assigned a value that is not `None` after the last such statement. So `if x is None: x = f()` (with `f` giving a `T`) keeps `x` a `T`; `if c: x = 1 else: x = 2` does; `if c: x = 1` with `x` an optional before does not; a `T` that a branch only changes (`if c: s = s + '+'`) stays a `T` after the `if`; a branch that assigns `None` or an optional makes it the optional. D102a's statement rule is relaxed for the `if` statement only, to make this exact: an `if` that assigns the variable in a branch no longer ends a narrowing from before the `if` before its first branch. Each branch starts from what was narrowed before the `if` (plus what its condition shows), whatever an earlier branch assigned, and what the branches know is joined at the end. Only what a condition itself assigns (a list comprehension's loop variable) ends a narrowing at the start of the `if`. Any other compound statement (`while`, `for`, `try`) still ends it at its start.
  3a. **Loops stay as D102a left them, which is what is implemented** (agent). A `while` or `for` that assigns the variable anywhere is never run with the variable narrowed from before it, and what the body narrows, by an assignment or by a join, ends with the body: it is not carried to the next pass, to the loop head or to the code after the loop (the loop may not have run, and `break` and `continue` leave from any point). Inside the body, narrowing by assignment works as anywhere else (`for it in items: cur = it; if cur is None: cur = '?'; use(cur + ',')`). The refinement "a loop that assigns a value that is not `None` at the end of every path keeps the variable narrowed in the loop and after it" is not done: the head of a loop joins the entry and the end of the body, so using the variable narrowed in the body needs the end of the body to be known before the body is compiled (the compiler would compile the body twice, or run an analysis that gives the type of every expression before code is made), and after the loop the variable is narrowed only when the loop is entered from a state where it was already (a `while` whose test fails at once) as well as at every `continue`, `break` and end of the body. The compiler's slot types are fixed while a function is compiled and a wrong join is an unsafe read. What it gains is small: `while x is None: x = f()` and the loops of D102a (which test at the head) already work, and the body of a loop sees its own assignments.
  4. **Representation and cost.** As D102a rule 6. A narrowing that starts at an assignment is made after the store: the number of an `int`, `float` or `bool` optional is taken out of the box that the assignment has just made into a slot of its own (a copy of a big integer or a float of any size, freed with the function), a text, list, dict, set, tuple, object or interface value only changes the type the compiler uses. A branch that follows one that assigned starts with the variable narrowed again (one unboxing for an `int`, `float` or `bool` optional), and the end of an `if` with such a join does the same. In release mode a loop nest has two copies of its code that jump into each other (the fast copy of a loop nest; D77e for its float variables), and the slot of a narrowed `int`, `float` or `bool` optional that was made inside the nest is filled by the copy that narrowed it: where the other copy can come in (a loop head of the fast copy, the start and end of each statement of the general copy) the slot is filled again from the variable, which holds the same value. Before this the join of D102a (`if x is None: continue` in a nest) could leave the slot empty after a bail-out, and the general copy then read a 0 for the number (found while testing this decision, in `tests/programs/optional_narrowing_assign.tom`, and fixed there). The same problem existed for the slots of a union narrowed by `x in T` (D55a); D106a fixes it.
- What is not narrowed (the list for later): `x: T | None = value` (rule 1), `x == None`, the end of a `while x is None:` loop, `match`, a conditional expression or `and`/`or` beyond D102a's shapes, a loop that assigns a `T` at the end of every path (rule 3a), any field. Each is sound to leave.
- Alternatives: (a) A full flow analysis with a fixpoint for loops: rejected for the reason given in D102a (b) and rule 3a: it needs types before code, and a mistake is an unsafe read. (b) The statement rule of D102a, ending a narrowing at the start of any statement that contains an assignment, kept for the `if` too, and the join taken only from the branches' own assignments: simpler, and it gives the TODO example, but `x` narrowed before an `if` would be lost when a branch assigns it (`if c: s = s + '+'`), and each branch's code would depend on the branches before it only by luck; rejected for being surprising. (c) Per-branch narrowing without the join (the rule of D55a, an assignment to a narrowed variable is an error): rejected in D102a. (d) Narrowing a declaration with a value (rule 1): rejected for the copy cost above; it is a one-line change in the compiler if Davis wants it.
- Cost: a program that copies a variable that is narrowed by an assignment and later assigns `None` to the copy fails to compile (D102a rule 5, now reached by more programs: `x = Node(); y = x; y = None`). A narrowed `int`, `float` or `bool` makes one copy of its number where the narrowing starts (and once more where a branch after an assigning one starts, and at a join). `tests/programs/optional_narrowing_flow.tom`'s `reassign` and `incr` keep their output; no source in `std/` changed.
- Tests: `tests/programs/optional_narrowing_assign.tom` (every optional type; the default, plain assignment, a copy, joins of `if`/`elif`/`else`, a branch that leaves, a narrowing from before the `if` that a branch assigns or leaves alone, nested `if`, loops that assign (run, with the check at every use), `try`, big integers and floats in the narrowed slot, a generator, an async function, and five release-mode loop nests) in debug, AddressSanitizer (with leak checks), release mode and WebAssembly; `tests/negative/optional_assign_*.tom` (an optional or `None` assigned, one branch that does not assign, an `elif` chain without `else`, a call that gives an optional, after a loop, the top of a loop that assigns, `try`, an `int` before and after a join, after `x = None`, a copy); a fuzz run, not part of the suite, of 320 random functions of two optional `int` parameters (nested `if`/`elif`/`else`, `while`, `for`, `try`, `break`, `continue`, `return`, assignments of numbers, big integers, `None` and the other variable, tests, uses) with seven inputs each, whose results (a number or the `None` error) equal Python's in debug and release builds under AddressSanitizer with leak checks. It also found, with the checked-in compiler, an unrelated use after free: `t += a` with a big `t` and an `int | None` `a` that is `None` inside a `try` (the operation takes `t`'s number before the check of `a`); listed as a follow-up.
- Supersedes / superseded by: replaces D102a rule 4's "whatever was assigned" and the first item of its "what is not narrowed" list; the rest of D102a stands.

### D104a — The repl replaces a redeclared variable where it stood; `datetime.replace` takes `int | None`: built (the request is Davis's TODO items, relayed by his lead agent, 2026-11-27; the rule is the implementer's, to be confirmed)

- Evidence: in `tomato repl` a variable declared again (`x: int = 2`) was removed and added at the end of the session, so an earlier line that used it (`print(x)` between `x: int = 1` and `x: int = 2`) failed with "name x is not defined". Separately, `datetime.replace` still took its parts as `int | bool = False` although D94a made `int | None = None` possible (`offset_minutes` already was).
- Decision (repl): **one rule for functions, classes, interfaces and variables.** A declaration with the name of an earlier one replaces it in the session, where the old one stood (a variable: its place among the statements, not the end), and the session runs again from the top, so every earlier line that used the name uses the new one and the old value is dropped. Any type may be given (`x: int = 1` then `x: float = 2.5`). If an earlier line does not work with the new declaration (the type changed and `x + 1` came before), or the new line is wrong itself, it is refused with that error, at that line (`session:N` for an earlier line), with a hint, and the old declaration and its value stay. A replacement shows no output of its own, as for a redefined function (what the earlier lines printed was shown when they ran); in a piece with more statements the declaration is run first, alone, and then only the output of the rest is shown. The history, the editor and what Up recalls are untouched (every entry stays, as typed). When the in-place declaration is refused, the repl tries the old way (the old declaration removed, the new one added at the end of the session); this is what makes `x: int = y * 10` work when `y` was declared after `x`, and it shows the in-place error only when that fails too. Only a declaration with an annotation (`x: T = ...`) on one line of the session's top level is replaced; plain `x = 1` after `x = 1` is an assignment as before.
- Decision (datetime): `year`, `month`, `day`, `hour`, `minute`, `second` and `microsecond` of `datetime.replace` are `int | None = None` (left out or `None` keeps the part), like `offset_minutes`. A caller that passed `False` for a part now gets E324 (as for `offset_minutes` in D94a); no caller in the repository did. The body uses `is not None`, narrowed by D102a.
- Alternatives: (a) for the repl, keep the old order and refuse earlier lines that used the variable: rejected, it is the bug. (b) Keep the old variable and refuse any change of type: rejected, it differs from functions and classes. (c) For datetime, accept `False` as well: rejected, a union `int | bool | None` keeps the old confusion.
- Cost: the output of a piece that redeclares a variable and has a side effect in the new initial value (`x: int = int(input())`) is not shown for that line, as for a redefinition; a declaration that spans several lines is not recognized as a redeclaration (its first line is replaced and the continuation lines stay, as before).
