- Tests: `tests/run.sh` (`t_cli_repl_redef`: in place, new type, refused with the hint, a loaded piece with statements), `tests/repl_history.exp` (a pty session: replace, refuse, history), `tests/programs/datetime_extra.tom` (`None` and omitted parts).

### D107a — Polish of the manifest and seed commands, `itertools`, `progress_bar` and `sys.run`: built (the request is Davis's TODO items from the third audit (docs/third-audit.md M2 to M8, M10, M13, M14, S3 to S5), relayed by his lead agent, 2026-11-27; the choices marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: the repros in `docs/third-audit.md`.
- Decision:
  1. **Lines in manifest errors.** E555 to E560, E565, E568, E590 and E562 print `FILE:LINE:` before the message. The TOML reader keeps no positions, so `manifest.tom` finds the line from the file's text (`line_of`: the key written as `key = ...`, a dotted key, a quoted seed name, or the header of the table; the line of the seed for a mistake inside its inline table); when it cannot (a key in a multi-line value) the message has the file only, as before. A guess, never wrong about the file.
  2. **The user's `[build] mode`** is read before `--mode` and the project's `[build]` are looked at, so a bad one is E565 in every build (agent: the alternative, to leave an unused file unread, hides the mistake until the day the flag is dropped).
  3. **Missing option values** are one line, status 2 (`error: -o needs a value`; `--color` lists its values; `seed remove` asks for a name). The other usage errors (a second file, extra words of `seed check`) still print the help.
  4. **`tomato test` with no word** takes the `tests/` of the folder with the nearest `tomato.toml` when the current folder has none (agent: file names are then printed with the project's path, not relative to where you are).
  5. **`[seed-store] vendor`** may not be empty, `.`, or begin with `src`, `tests`, `builds` or `.git` (E604, in the manifest reader); a vendor path that is a file is E604 from `seed vendor`.
  6. **A foreign symbol added to a locked path seed** is looked for on every build of a path seed (its modules are parsed: the same as `seed install` does) and refused as `--locked` refuses it (E602, "run `tomato seed install` and answer, or add --yes"); a module that does not parse is left to the compile. Cost: each build parses the modules of its path seeds once more.
  7. **A project module and a seed module with one name** (a file next to the program or in `src/`, and a module of a seed the run uses) is E607 at the import, naming both files (agent: an error, like E581, rather than a rule that one wins).
  8. **`tomato init NAME`** refuses a name with a `.` or `..` part (`../x`, `a/../b`); `a/b` and a backslash stay (they are made into `-` in `builds/`); after `--` every word is a name.
  9. **`seed check`** prints a `warning:` for a `description` or `license` that starts with `TODO:` and for the owner `yourdomain.example` (warnings, status 0: the command has no failing check besides the owner rule E580).
  10. **A damaged store copy** is fetched again and replaced by `seed install` (not with `--offline`, which has no git: E601); the E601 text says to run `install`.
  11. **`itertools`**: a negative count is `ValueError` (E39) in `combinations`, `combinations_with_replacement`, `product_repeat` and `repeat` (Python's `repeat(x, -1)` is empty; the item says to raise, as `batched` does), and in `permutations` below -1 (-1 stays "all"). `for x in progress_bar(...)` is compiled as the file loop is (D82a): the bar in a hidden variable and a `try ... finally bar.close()`, and `close()` does nothing the second time. A bar kept in a variable is still closed by hand. `progress_bar([], 'x')` stays E184 (an empty list literal has no type: write the list in a variable with its type); the comment said otherwise and is corrected.
  12. **`sys.run` and `spawn`** read the first line of a script that cannot be started (kind "not found" and a `/` in the name) and say `its interpreter 'X' was not found` when it starts with `#!`.
- Alternatives: keep positions in the TOML reader (a larger change of `std/toml.tom` for one message); make `seed check` fail on placeholders (publishing has no registry yet to check against); close a `progress_bar` with a generator `finally` (`yield` inside `try` is E108).
- Cost: the two compiler changes (the `for` rewrite, `read_module`) and the manifest reader change the bootstrap IR; the E607 line is new in `std/errors.txt`.
- Tests: `tests/cli_contract/cases/{init,seed,build_tables,config,project,args,help,env_status}.sh` (the golden text of the usage errors changed on purpose: one line instead of the help), `tests/programs/{itertools_lists,sys_run}.tom`, `t_progress_break` in `tests/run.sh`, and the store checks of `t_cli_seed_store`.
### D106a — Two memory-safety fixes found with the narrowing work: the slot of a union narrowed in a release-mode loop nest, and `t += a` with an optional `a`: built (the request is Davis's TODO items, relayed by his lead agent, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: two TODO items that D102a/D102b's tests and fuzzing found, both reproduced with the checked-in compiler (`compiler/bootstrap/tomatoc.ll` of 60a3dea0) before the fix. (1) `def f(xs: list[int], k: int | str) -> int:` with `for i: for j: if k in str: continue; t += xs[i] * xs[j] + k`: debug mode gave ...980 and release mode ...975 (for `k = 5` and a big number in `xs`), `k: float | str` gave a wrong float, and a text member crashed with a segmentation fault. (2) `t = <big int>; try: t += a; catch RuntimeError: pass` with `a: int | None` that is `None`: AddressSanitizer reports a heap-use-after-free in debug and in release mode.
- Decision (no rule of the language changes: both are the compiler doing what the rules already said):
  1. **The slots of a union narrowed to one member in a loop nest.** A union narrowed by `x in T` to one member `T` keeps `T` in a slot of its own, which replaces the variable's slot while the narrowing lasts (D55a). A release-mode loop nest is compiled twice (D77e, D102b rule 4): each copy makes the narrowing with a slot of its own, the fast copy goes to the start of a statement of the general copy when a number is big, and the general copy's slot was then empty. The compiler now records each narrowing that has a slot of its own (`UnionNarrow`, beside `OptNarrow`) and `opt_rederive` fills the slot again from the variable's box wherever the other copy can come in (a loop head of the fast copy, the start and end of each statement of the general copy), exactly as for a narrowed `int`, `float` or `bool` optional. The box holds the same value for as long as the narrowing lasts (a narrowed union cannot be assigned), so this is always right; the cost is one load, a type-offset load and, for a text or object, a retain and a release, at those points, in release-mode nests that narrow a union.
  2. **When a statement hands the old number to the operation.** `x = x + 1` (a `+ - *` tree, or `//` and `%` by a constant, with `x` read once) lets the operation free the old big number itself, so the store frees nothing (`self_update`, `docs/performance.md`). That is only right when nothing can stop the statement after the operation has been given the number: reading a variable of type `int | None` unwraps it and raises 'None where a value is needed' when it is `None`, and the error path then freed the number that the variable still held. The old number is now handed over only when every other variable the right side reads is an `int` or a fixed-width integer (`plain_leaves`); any other leaf (an optional, a union, an object, a float, a name the compiler does not know the type of) keeps the ordinary store, which frees the old number after the new one is made. The fused release-mode expressions (`fuse_update`) only have `int` leaves of simple shapes and were checked, not changed.
- Siblings checked with the old compiler and fixed or found fine (programs in `tests/programs/optional_compound_assign.tom` and `union_narrowing_nests.tom`; two throw-away fuzzers, not part of the suite, found nothing more): the use after free was in `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `t = a + t`, `t = a - t`, `t = 2 * t + a`, `t = (t + a) * 3` and `t = t // 3 + a` alike, and is fixed for all of them; `+=` on a text or list with an optional right side, `+=` and `*=` on a float, and `t + xs[i] * 2` with an index out of range were never affected; the union slot problem affected every member type (int, float of any size, text, object) and the `while` and `for` forms; nothing else in the nest code skips a narrowed variable (the nest's own small-int and float tests leave narrowed variables out and the fast copy reads them with a small-number or finite-float check that leaves it). A fuzz run of 200 random nests of two `if ... in ...: continue|break` chains, a `c is None` test and uses of the narrowed values, with five union values (small, big, float, huge float, text) and five tables, gave the same results in debug and release mode under AddressSanitizer with leak checks; 500 random one-statement updates of a big `t` by trees over an optional, an int and a constant gave Python's results, with `None` leaving `t` unchanged, in both modes.
- Alternatives: (a) For the nest, one slot shared by both copies instead of filling again: the fast copy keeps its own slot so that it never has to look at the general copy's code, and the nest code already works by refilling (D102b); a shared slot would need the narrowing made before the nest, and the narrowing is often made inside it (the example). (b) For the update, test the optional before taking the number (a second path that unwraps first): it keeps the optimisation for `int | None` leaves, but a program that reads an optional in the update is rare and the check of the ordinary store is cheap; not worth a second code path.
- Tests: `tests/programs/union_narrowing_nests.tom` (twelve nest shapes: the example, three members, text, float (also of a size no double holds), object, branches, a narrowing in the outer loop, used twice, `while` loops, with an optional, narrowed twice; each with small tables and with a big number at each position, big and negative big members) and `tests/programs/optional_compound_assign.tom` (the operators and tree shapes above, in a `try`, in a loop, uncaught, a narrowed optional, a fixed-width operand, and the text, list and float cases), each in debug, AddressSanitizer with leak checks, release mode (AddressSanitizer with leak checks) and WebAssembly. Before the fix `union_narrowing_nests` crashed or gave wrong numbers in release mode and `optional_compound_assign` was a heap-use-after-free in every build.
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes D102b rule 4 ("the same problem exists for the slots of a union ... a follow-up") and D55a's implementation; no change to what programs mean.

### D111a — `@str` is found through the object's own class, and the optional and union compile errors of the third audit are smoothed: built (the request is Davis's TODO items from the third audit (S2, O3 to O5, D9) and one more, relayed by his lead agent, 2026-11-27; the choices marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: the repros in `docs/third-audit.md` and the three TODO items, each reproduced with the checked-in compiler first. `class Own(Error)` with `@str def show(self)`, then `catch Error as e: print(e)` printed `x`, not `own:x`; `p: P = Q()` printed with `P`'s `@str`; `self.s + 'x'` on a field `s: str | None` inside `if self.s is not None:` said only "cannot use '+' with str | None and str"; `xs = [1, None]` gave E324 with no way out; E458 and E313 put the caret under the whole `def`; E340 said a function that is not `export`ed was "not defined"; `(t * 2) + o.n * 3` with `o: Box | None` was E339 "the class 'Box?' does not exist".
- Decision:
  1. **`@str` is virtual (agent: the first of the two ways the TODO item offered).** `print`, `str()`, f-strings and the text of a list or union item look the `@str` method up in the object's own class, whatever the method is called and whatever the static type is, so a subclass's `@str` under another name (`class Own(Error)` with `@str def show`) is used for a value typed `Error` or the parent class; a class with no `@str` of its own uses its nearest parent's, as before. The compiler keeps the direct call (which can be inlined) when no class below the static one has a method marked `@str`, so a program without such a subclass compiles to what it did. The method table has one more slot (`@str`, which is no method name) only in a program that needs it. Alternative: a compile error for a second `@str` under a new name in a subclass, asking for the parent's name and `override`; rejected because the rule in `docs/errors.md` ("a class may mark any one method `@str`") already allowed it and it would break classes that were fine.
  2. **A field that can be None in an operator** (`self.s + 'x'`, `'a' + self.s`, also in a longer `+` chain) keeps its message (E190) and gains two help lines: the field is not narrowed by an `is not None` test (D102a), and to copy it to a variable and use that.
  3. **`[1, None]`** (a list whose type came from its first item, and a later item is `None`) keeps E324 and gains a help line: write the type of the list, `xs: list[int | None] = ...`.
  4. **The caret of E458, E313 and a type that is not supported** is under the type that is wrong (the start of the union, or `None`), not the whole `def`: the parser notes the column of a union and of `None`, and the errors of a parameter, a return type, a field and a declaration use it. `x: None` still says E313 (this parameter type is not supported) with the help "None alone is not a type; write the type that can be None, such as int | None". `int | None | None` and `None | None` are no longer called "two or more other types": they are the new E611 "None is written more than once in this union".
  5. **A module function that exists but is not `export`ed** (`import cache` then `cache.part(3)`) says E225, "module 'cache' does not export 'part'", with the help to put `export` before its definition, the same as `from cache import part` does (it said E340, "not defined").
  6. **A field of an optional object in a longer arithmetic expression** (`(t * 2) + o.n * 3` with `o: Box | None`) compiles, and a `None` there stops the program with "None where a value is needed" (E10) as `t + o.n` does; the compiler had asked for a class named `Box?` while deciding whether the number could be lent.
- Alternatives: for 6, a type error at compile time (rejected: `t + o.n` and every other use of an optional object's field is a run-time error, and a compile error only in the fused form would be one more rule); for 4, keep the line-only caret and quote the type in the message (rejected: it changes the first line of E458 and E313).
- Cost: the bootstrap IR changes (parser columns, `str_varies`); E610 is new in `std/errors.txt`; a program that has a subclass with an `@str` loads the object's method table to print a parent-typed value.
- Tests: `tests/programs/str_dispatch.tom` (names, a generic subclass, a parent-typed list, an optional, an error caught as `Error`), `tests/programs/optional_field_fused.tom`, and `tests/errors/{optional_field_not_narrowed,list_none_item_hint,union_none_with_two_types,union_none_twice,param_type_none,field_type_union_none,return_type_none_twice,module_function_not_exported}` with the whole text.
- Supersedes / superseded by: changes the "uses that, but only when the static type is that class" behaviour that D93b left in (a class's own `@str`, any name); the caret rule of `docs/errors.md`.

### D23i — `x is T` is the type test and `a is b` is identity; `x in T` is the old spelling, kept with a warning: decided (Davis, language design review 2.2, 2026-11-27); the rules marked "default" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed

- Evidence: in the language design review (`docs/language-design-review.md`, 2.2, "`x in Type` for type tests", which was ranked "Blocks 1.0"), Davis decided on 2026-11-27 that the type test is `x is T`, replacing `x in T` (D23f) and generalising `is None` (D23c; `None` is a type, D23d). He added the meaning of `is`: it checks equivalence at the pointer (identity) level **and** checks whether something is a type, so `a is b` on two reference values is identity (the same object), `x is T` where `T` is a type name is the type test, and `x is None` stays as it is (the type test for `None`). The request reached this task through his lead agent. The problem it solves, from the review: `if v in Angle:` is a type test while `if v in angles:` searches a list, and the two read alike.
- Decision:
  1. **The type test.** `x is T` is true when the value's type is `T`, and `x is not T` when it is not; what matches what is exactly D23f and P44 (a class name matches its subclasses, an interface name the classes that implement it, `int` plain integers only, an optional by its contents, `tuple[str, int]`, `list[int]` and the other forms the compiler accepted after `in`). The test is compiled by the code that compiled `x in T`, with the result negated for `is not`; `x is None` and `x is not None` are not changed at all: they stay the pointer test of D23c, with its error for a value that is never `None` (E169).
  2. **Identity (the exact rule; default).** `a is b` and `a is not b`, where neither side is a type, are true when both sides are the same object. They work on **objects of classes, interface values, lists, dicts and sets, and an optional of one of these** (`Shape | None`), and the two sides may have different types when one is assignable to the other (`Circle` and `Shape`, a class and its interface, `Shape | None` and `Shape`); two types that can never hold the same object (`A` and `B`, `list[int]` and `list[str]`) are a compile error. Everything else is a compile error (E190), with the help to compare with `==`: `int`, `float`, `bool` and the fixed-width integers (values: a number has no identity, and a big integer is a heap number that each variable owns a copy of), **text and tuples** (immutable, and whether two equal texts are one heap value depends on how literals and results are shared, which differs between modes and would make the answer something no program should rely on), unions and the optional `int`, `float` and `bool` (boxes). The compiler compares the two pointers, so `l is m` is false for two lists with the same items and true after `m = l`. Being strict first is the compatible direction: allowing text later changes no program, forbidding it would.
  3. **Type or variable (default).** The right side is a type when it is a bare type name (`None`, `int`, `str`, `float`, `bool`, a class or an interface of the program) or `tuple[...]`, `list[...]`, `set[...]` or `dict[...]` with type arguments, **and** it is not the name of a local variable or parameter. A local named like a type is a variable: `x is str` then compares identity with it (and, for text, is an error). The resolution order is the one `in` had (locals, then `None`, then the built-in type names, then the classes and interfaces the module can see); the rule "a module's own definitions shadow builtins" (language design review 1.4, the sibling task "Module definitions shadow builtins") had not merged when this was built, so the order is not changed here: when it lands, a module-level class named like a builtin type is decided by that rule, and this decision follows it.
  4. **Narrowing is the same for `is`.** A union variable (D55a) is narrowed by `x is T` in the branch, in `else`, in later `elif` bodies and after an early exit, exactly as by `x in T`; **new:** `x is not T`, `x not in T` and `not (x is T)` narrow the other way (the branch is the other members, which are a smaller union or one type; `else`, the later branches and the rest of the block after `if x is not T: return` are `T`). The rule of D55a that a narrowed union cannot be assigned is unchanged. An optional (D102a) is narrowed by `x is T` where `T` is not `None` and the optional's own type converts to `T`: the variable is not `None` there, so it is a `T` in the true branch, after `if x is not T: return`, on the right of `x is T and ...`, as `x is not None` narrows it, with `and`/`or`/`not`, conditional expressions and `while` as for `is None`. Still not narrowed (D55a's list, unchanged): a union through `and`/`or`/`not` conditions or `while`, fields, and anything but a plain variable.
  5. **Transition (default; Davis has not decided this part, and it is easy to change).** `x in T` (a type on the right) keeps working in 1.0.x, with the same meaning and narrowing as `x is T`, and the compiler prints a warning, kind `type-test`, for it: `'in' with a type is the old spelling of the type test`, help `write x is T, and x is not T for the opposite`. It is shown once per line, only for code the compiler compiles (a function that nothing calls is not looked at), never for a seed's code, and `# allow: type-test` silences it, like the other warnings (`docs/errors.md`). It is non-fatal. To change the choice: remove the `in` case in `Gen.binop`, `narrow_target` and `opt_names` (an error instead of a warning, `warn_old_type_test` becomes the message) or delete `warn_old_type_test` and its call (silent). The design review proposed "keep `x in T` for 1.0.x only if programs need a transition, with a warning"; the programs that need it are the ones outside this repository, which the compiler cannot see.
  6. **Moved to the new spelling:** every `x in Type` in `std/` (`asyncio`, `bitarray`, `cache`, `json`, `math`, `re`, `requests`, `time`; `site/wasm/std` is a copy), the tests, the examples' guide text and the documents. The highlighters (`hltomato`, `site/site.js`, `editors/vscode`) need no change: `in` and `is` are keywords everywhere and none treated `in Type` specially.
- Alternatives: (a) Remove `x in T` at once (a compile error that names `is`): the cleanest, and what the review says to do once no program needs the transition; rejected as the default only because the choice is Davis's and a warning can be made an error by deleting two lines, but an error cannot be made kinder after programs break. (b) Keep `in` silently: rejected, since the point of the review is that the two spellings read alike. (c) Identity for every type: rejected for text, tuples and numbers (rule 2). (d) A different word for identity (`same`, `===`): not what Davis said; `is` already means identity in Python. (e) `x is T` as a separate node in the parser: the parser cannot tell a type from a variable, so `is` stays one binary operator and the compiler decides by the right side (rule 3), as it did for `in`.
- Cost: no new runtime code (the type test is the old code, identity is one pointer comparison, `is not` one `xor`). Existing programs that write `x in T` get a warning; a program that wrote `a is b` on two objects, lists, dicts or sets, which was E190, now compiles. `asks_type` (the check that stops `std/math`'s functions of `int | float` from getting a float-only copy) now also looks for `is` and `is not` with a right side other than `None`.
- Found while testing, not changed (follow-up): the type test on a class-typed value is decided from its **static** type, as `x in Type` was: with `v: Shape` holding a `Circle`, `v is Circle` is false and `v is Shape` true, so `is` cannot be used to downcast; only unions, optionals and interface values are tested by their contents. D23f's "a class name also matches subclasses" holds in the direction `Circle is Shape`. **Settled by D109a (2026-11-27): the test now reads the object's own class, so `v is Circle` is True for a `Circle` held by a `Shape`, and `x is Dog` narrows a base-typed variable to a `Dog`.**
- Tests: `tests/programs/type_is.tom` (the type test on unions of numbers, text, classes, `list[int]` and `tuple[str, int]`, `is not` with `if`/`elif`/`else`, `not`, early `return`/`raise`/`continue`, three members, optionals with `and`/`or`/`not`/`while`, plain values and literals; identity for objects, lists, dicts, sets, interface values, optionals, a subclass and its base, temporaries and copies), `tests/programs/type_in_old.tom` (the old spelling, silenced with `# allow: type-test`, and `in` for membership), `tests/programs/type_is_nests.tom` (`is not` in release-mode loop nests, as `union_narrowing_nests`), each in debug, AddressSanitizer with leak checks, release mode and WebAssembly (the first two; the nests also in release), `tests/negative/is_*.tom` (thirteen rejected programs: numbers, text, tuples, unrelated types, boxes, assignment to a narrowed union after `is not`, an optional not narrowed on the other side) and `tests/errors/in_type_warning` and `is_value_help` (the whole warning and error text). The existing tests that used `in Type` were moved to `is`.
- Supersedes / superseded by: changes the spelling of D23e and D23f (their meaning is kept, P44); settles D23c's open "`is` with other operands"; completes language design review 2.2; D55a, D102a and D102b are extended, not changed.

### D34c — Release mode prints the class and the message of an uncaught error, on one line: built (Davis, language design review 1.3, 2026-11-27; the exact punctuation is the implementer's, to be confirmed)

- Decision (Davis): in `--mode release` an uncaught error prints one line with the class and the message, like debug mode but without the call trace. Before, `raise ValueError("config file missing key port")` printed `error E11:3` (the message, the only information of an error the program made up, was lost); debug printed `error: ValueError: config file missing key port (raised on line 3)` and the trace. Text written to standard error is the kind of output D84a calls "prints something else", so the form had to be settled before 1.0.
- The form (agent): `error[ID]: Class: message (line N)`. It is debug's head with two changes: the id is always shown, since release has no short code any more and `E11` ("an error raised by the program") stands for an error that has no id of its own (debug leaves the id out for those); and `(line N)` replaces `(raised on line N)`, because no trace follows to say what the line is the start of. A failure the language raises itself (`ZeroDivisionError`, `IndexError`, ...) prints, as in debug, no class, because its message already says what happened, followed by the line: `error[E01]: division by zero (line 4)`; its text is the one in `std/errors.txt`, which a compiler-emitted check carries as the message of the error object in both modes. A failure of the runtime with no source location (out of memory E349, a stack overflow E350, bad startup input) prints `error[E349]: out of memory` with no line (there is none; before, `error E349:0`). A `Diagnostic` (the compiler's own error, only seen when the compiler is a release build) is printed as it is, as in debug. The `in a task nobody awaited` suffix of an unread task error is added in release too. A message is printed whole, however long or with however many lines.
- Binary size: the runtime already holds the messages, as the `std/errors.txt` texts of its own failures (they are string constants at the call sites, which release builds no longer discard) and the object carries the program's message. What release used to leave out is the class names: the compiler named the classes of a release program only when it imports `testing` (for `assert_raises`). It now names every `Error` subclass of the program (and, as before, every class when `testing` is imported), a short string for each; other classes stay unnamed.
- Alternatives: (a) keep the short ids and print the message only for the program's own errors (the proposal of the review, "the short form only for the runtime's own messages"): rejected, since the runtime's messages are in `std/errors.txt` and cost little, and two forms made the output harder to document and to match with tests. (b) Print the class for runtime failures too (`error[E01]: ZeroDivisionError: division by zero`): rejected, debug does not, and the message is complete without it; `tomato explain E01` still shows the class. (c) Keep `error E11:3` and add the message after it: rejected, it is neither debug's form nor readable.
- Cost: release builds hold the class names of their error classes and the message of each runtime check (a few bytes each); an error line is longer. `tomato explain` is unchanged: it still takes `E01:4` (the id and line a user reads from the new line) and no longer needs to, since the line now says what the error is; a program that read the old `error E01:4` text from a release program's standard error must read the new one (D84a; this is why it is settled before 1.0).
- Tests: `tests/runtime/release_long_message.tom` (a 10000-byte message, in release mode and under AddressSanitizer), `tests/runtime/divide_by_zero.tom` (a runtime failure, in release mode and under AddressSanitizer), and the release-mode checks of `tests/run.sh` and `tests/cli_contract` (run, build and explain, whose golden output changes only for the release error line).

### D109a — `docs/provisional.md` is triaged once before 1.0: promoted, on a short unstable list, or removed; the 1.0.x promise excepts only that list: Davis's decision to triage (2026-11-27); the outcome below is the agent's proposal, to be confirmed row by row

- Evidence: Davis decided on 2026-11-27 to triage the placeholder list once before 1.0, as `docs/language-design-review.md` 1.1 and 1.2 propose. D84a promised that 1.0.x releases are compatible "except the placeholders in `docs/provisional.md`", but the file had 91 rows, 25 of them marked superseded, and its live rows included the core syntax (P1, P5, P6, P7, P8, P10, P11, P22, P24) and "error wording is the prototype's own" (P20): read literally the promise excepted nearly every program.
- Decision (the process): every row has exactly one outcome. **Promoted** when the behaviour has been stable for many releases and a decision number or a test pins it (the row says which); **kept on the unstable list** when Davis may still change it; **removed** when superseded, when it describes the retired Python prototype or an implementation detail, or when its text no longer matches the compiler. Each non-trivial row was checked with a small program on the compiler built from `main`; a row whose text did not match was corrected, removed or listed, never promoted as written.
- *[Update, D116a (and D112a, D113a): P44, P33 and P21 left the list the same day, so 61 promoted, 4 on the unstable list, 26 removed; the text below is the original proposal. Update, D126a: Davis's later answers add two features (the marker names beyond `@add`, the async task order) to the list, which has 6 features; 61 promoted rows and 26 removed.]*
- Outcome (the agent's proposal, `docs/provisional-triage.md`): **58 promoted, 7 on the unstable list, 26 removed.** The list: error message wording (P20), the edges of the type test (P44: the old `x in T` spelling and the test on class-typed values; the spelling `x is T` itself is D23i), `!name` foreign functions (P33), entry markers other than `@main` (P21), the host input protocol (P84), the text `progress_bar` draws (P68), and `random.choice`, `shuffle` and `sample` (P91). `docs/provisional.md` now has the list (section 1), the promoted rows with the decision or test that pins each (section 2) and the removed rows with where their behaviour lives now (section 3); a new placeholder may be added only on the list.
- The promise: `docs/releasing.md` (Compatibility) now reads "all 1.0.x releases are compatible, except the 7 features on the unstable list in `docs/provisional.md` (section 1)". This replaces the exception clause of D84a; the rest of D84a stands.
- The error promise (proposed, marked so in `docs/releasing.md`; review 1.2): an error id never changes its meaning; the class an operation raises does not change within a minor version; message text may be reworded in a patch release, but the first line keeps its id; a decided rule the compiler does not enforce yet is enforced in a minor release. `docs/errors.md` ("Two branches that each add an error") says how two branches that each add a line to `std/errors.txt` resolve the id clash: the branch that merges second renumbers its own lines, highest id first, and an id on `main` or in a release never moves.
- Found while checking (not decided here): the type test on a variable declared as a class or interface type tests the declared type, not the object's class (`a: A = B(); a in B` is `False`), contrary to D23f's wording (D23i records the same finding as a follow-up); class conversion `Circle(s)` (D12az, D12bh) and the `@init` flow analysis (D12x) are not built; the old texts of P11, P14, P18, P25, P57 and P58 no longer matched. While this task ran, `main` decided `x is T` (D23i), builtin shadowing (D108a) and the release-mode error line (D34c); the triage was updated to them (P31, P44, P88 corrected, U2 narrowed to the edges of the type test).
- Alternatives: (a) promote every row not marked superseded: rejected, it would promise things nobody decided (P7, P8) and text that is wrong (P15, P44). (b) Remove the exception and promise everything: rejected, `!name` and the entry-marker spelling are open in the review, and the edges of the type test are defaults Davis has not confirmed, and the release would carry decisions made by default. (c) A compiler warning when a program uses an unstable feature: not built, a follow-up (it needs a lint kind, a bootstrap refresh and a wasm rebuild).
- Cost: the 13 numbered judgment calls of `docs/provisional-triage.md` need answers; a "no" to any one moves a row between the three outcomes and changes the number 7 in four places (listed there).
- Supersedes / superseded by: replaces the exception clause of D84a; the P-rows it removes are cited by older decisions and the specification, so the numbers are kept.

### D110a — `x is Class` checks the object's own class, and `x is Dog` makes a base-typed variable a Dog: built (decided by Davis, 2026-11-27; the rules marked "default" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: the TODO item "Decide whether `x is Subclass` should check the dynamic class", left open by D23i ("found while testing"): with `v: Shape` holding a `Circle`, `v is Circle` was False and `v is Shape` True, because a class-typed value was tested by its static type, so `is` could not downcast. Davis decided on 2026-11-27 that `x is Animal` is true when `x` is an `Animal` or any subclass of it, by the object's dynamic class, as Python's `isinstance`. Reproduced before the change, with `Animal`, `Dog(Animal)`, `Cat(Animal)` and `Tag(implements Named)`/`Tag2(Tag)`: `a: Animal = Dog()` gave `a is Dog` False; `n: Named = Tag()` gave `n is Tag` False (an interface value was tested by its static type too, though D23i says it is tested by its contents); `Animal | int` holding a `Dog` gave `u is Dog` False; `Animal | None` holding a `Dog` gave `a is Dog` False. `x is Animal` was already True for all of them.
- Decision:
  1. **The test looks at the object.** `x is T` (and `x is not T`, `not (x is T)`, and the old spelling `x in T`, which goes through the same code) is true when the object `x` holds is of the class `T` or of a class that descends from `T`, and, for an interface `T`, when its class implements `T` (declared on the class or inherited from a parent, as `assignable` says). It is read from the object's own header, never from the static type of the variable. It covers a class value, an interface value, an optional of either (`None` is not a `T` for any `T` but `None`) and a union (each member in turn: the box says which member it holds, and for a class or interface member the object's class is tested too, so `Animal | int` holding a `Dog` is a `Dog` and an `Animal`). A field, an item or a call result is tested the same way, only never narrowed. `int`, `str`, `float`, `bool`, tuples and lists are values of one type and are tested as before.
  2. **Decided at compile time when it can be.** When the static type is already a `T` (a `Dog` variable tested for `Animal`, `Dog` or `Named` when `Dog` implements it) the answer is True, with no read; when `T` is a class that is not a descendant of the static class (a sibling, an unrelated class) the answer is False, with no read, because no object can be both. Only a `T` that is a strict descendant of the static class (or an implementing class of a static interface) and an interface `T` that a static class does not already satisfy are tested at run time.
  3. **How (no new runtime code).** A class `T` is `tomato_is_subclass(code_of_object, code_of_T)`, the helper that `catch` already uses (a walk up the parent table; one header load, the class codes are in debug and release mode alike). An interface `T` is one generated function per tested interface, `@tomato.isiface.N(code) -> i1`, a `switch` over the type codes of the classes that implement it, written when every class is known (so a generic class copy made late is in it); a class that nothing was made from has no code and no objects. The optional and union forms read the object only after the `None` or box test (a branch and a `phi`), never before. The names of the classes are not used (they are not in release mode); this is the way `catch` tests, not the way `tomato_error_is` does for `assert_raises`.
  4. **Narrowing.** *Unions* (D55a) are unchanged: `x is T` narrows a union to `T` when `T` is one of its members, and the other members in the opposite branch. That stays sound with the new test (a `Dog` that a union holds as its `Animal` member is a `Dog`, and a variable narrowed to the member `Dog` has the object's pointer, which is a `Dog`; the other members of the false branch lose nothing that was true). A `T` that is a class below a member (`x: Animal | int` and `x is Dog`) is tested but **does not narrow** (default; the union stays one; the narrowing to a member's descendant is a follow-up). **New: a local variable or parameter of a class type, an interface type, or an optional of one, is narrowed to `Dog` by `x is Dog`** where `Dog` is a class that descends from the variable's class (or implements its interface): in the true branch of `if x is Dog:`, the later `elif`/`else` and the rest of the block after `if x is not Dog: return`, on the right of `x is Dog and ...` and `x is not Dog or ...`, in the sides of a conditional expression and in a `while` body, exactly the places and the rules of D102a (it ends at any statement that can assign the variable, `x = value` is worked out with it narrowed and ends it, it does not cross a loop that assigns the variable). The object is the same pointer, so only the type the compiler uses changes: no code, no copy, the borrow rules are unchanged. The variable is not `None` there. It stacks with the "not `None`" narrowing of an optional (`x is not None and x is Dog`, in any order), and an assignment ends all of them and checks the value against the declared type (`a: Animal | None`: `a = None` inside `if a is Dog:` is fine). **Not narrowed (default):** a field, a global or any expression that is not a plain local; a variable that a union narrowing (`if u is Animal:`) holds in its own slot (the compiler refuses to assign it there, so it is not narrowed again); a narrowing to an interface (`a is Named` on an `Animal`: it is tested, the variable stays an `Animal`); and the false branch (`x is not Dog` says nothing about the variable's type).
  5. **What stays as it was.** `a is b` (identity), `x is None` and the transition rules of D23i, the E169 and E190 errors, the `type-test` warning for `x in T`, and every program that tested a class value for its own class or a base of it (it was True and still is). A program that tested a base-typed variable for a subclass (`v is Circle`, always False before) now gets what it asks for; the compiler's source and `std/` had no such test.
- Alternatives: (a) Keep the static test and say it in the documents: not what Davis decided. (b) Compare class names, as `tomato_error_is` does: names are in the program only in debug mode (and in release only for `testing`), strings are compared one by one up the chain, and a class and an interface of one name in two modules would collide; the code walk is exact and cheaper. (c) Mark each class with the set of its interfaces in the class tables (a table or a bit set, read at the test): rejected, since it changes the layout the runtime reads and needs a table for every class that is used; the generated switch is one small function per tested interface. (d) Narrow a union to a descendant of a member (`Animal | int`, `x is Dog`): left out to keep one slot per narrowed union member; needs a narrowing of the narrowed slot and its re-fill in loop nests (D106a). (e) Narrow a base-typed variable only in `if` (no `and`/`or`/`while`): rejected, the D102a machinery gives all of them for the price of one more kind of entry in its lists. (f) A new cast spelling (`x as Dog`): not asked for.
- Cost: nothing for a test that is decided at compile time. A run-time test of a class is one header load and a parent walk (a call of a six-line C function that the optimiser may inline); of an interface, one call of a function made of a `switch`; of an optional or a union, a branch before. A narrowing costs nothing at run time; the compiler does a short scan of the lists of narrowed names, which now also hold a type per downcast. The bootstrap IR grew by the new code only. `x in T` with a class `T` got the new meaning with `x is T`.
- Found while testing, not changed (follow-up): an optional of an interface (`Named | None`) cannot be given a `Named` value or a class that implements it (E324 "the value has type Tag, but Named | None is expected"), with or without this change; `x is T` on one could not be tried beyond the parameter.
- Tests: `tests/programs/type_is_class.tom` (subclass, base and sibling instances, a deeper subclass, `is not`, `not (x is T)`, `Dog` and `Poodle` variables, `Animal | None`, unions with `Animal`, `Dog | Cat | str` and an interface member, interface values for a class and for another interface, a class value tested for an interface that only a subclass implements, the narrowing in `if`/`elif`/`else`, early `return` and `raise`, `and`, `or`, a conditional expression, `while`, assignment inside the narrowing, narrowing twice, a field, a list of objects, the old `in` spelling and `catch Error as e` with `e is KeyError`), `tests/programs/type_is_class_nests.tom` (the same in release-mode loop nests, with small and big numbers), four rejected programs `tests/negative/is_class_*.tom` (a union not assignable after `is Dog` inside `is Animal`, a field of the narrowed class that does not exist, the false branch not narrowed, a field not narrowed); each program in debug, AddressSanitizer with leak checks, release mode and WebAssembly.
- Supersedes / superseded by: settles the TODO item and the "found while testing" paragraph of D23i; changes the reading of D23f and P44 ("a class name also matches subclasses": now in both directions, whatever the static type); extends D102a's narrowing (same rules) and leaves D55a's unchanged.

### D112a — `tomato_*` runtime functions are reserved: `!tomato_...` is a compile error outside `std/`: built (decided by Davis, 2026-11-27, language design review 1.6; the details marked "agent" are the implementer's, to be confirmed)

- Evidence: any program could write `!tomato_env_has(name: str) -> int` and call the runtime's private functions, so every `tomato_*` name and argument list became something a 1.0 program might rely on, and the runtime could not change after 1.0. `docs/package-registry-design.md` section 5 asked for a check and found none.
- Decision, the rules:
  1. **What is open.** `!name(...)` declares and calls any symbol that exists at link time: libc (`!abs`, `!strlen`), the program's own compiled C or LLVM IR (`--llvm`, `examples/mathlib.tom`), any LLVM function the program links, in seeds as in programs. The rules of D27b stay: `int` is `i64`, `int[length=N]` and `uint[length=N]` (up to 64) are `iN`, `bool` is `i1`, no `->` is `void`, and the symbol must exist when the program is linked (a missing one is a linker error, not a compile error).
  2. **`float` is a C `double`.** The argument is converted to the nearest double and a result is converted back. Since D77a a `float` can be larger or smaller than a double can hold: such an argument stops the program (the conversion `bf_to_f64` raises) and does not wrap or become infinity. The runtime's own `tomato_bf_*` functions, which take the float word itself, are reserved with the rest.
  3. **`tomato_*` is reserved.** Every function the runtime defines is named `tomato_*` (checked in this task: `nm` on the compiled `runtime/tomato_rt.c` lists no other global symbol, every `define` of `runtime/tomato_inline.ll` is `tomato_*`, and the compiler's own symbols are written `tomato.NAME`, with a dot, which `!name` cannot spell; the program's C `main` is the one name in the program that is not `tomato`). A `!tomato_...` declaration, or a `!tomato_...(...)` call, in a module that is not part of the standard library is E611 at the declaration or the call, with the help "tomato_* functions belong to the runtime and are not part of the language; use the std module that wraps it".
  4. **What counts as the standard library.** A module that `read_module` loaded from the `std/` folder of the toolchain (and the built-ins, `std/builtin/`), the same files in the browser's `site/wasm/std`. A module of the project or of a seed with the name of a std module does not count (it is found first and was not read from `std/`): `utf8.tom` next to a program that declares `!tomato_str_nbytes` is rejected. The compiler's own sources import `utf8.encode` instead of declaring the two functions themselves.
  5. **Seeds.** `tomato seed add` and `install` do not list a `!tomato_*` declaration for the user's consent: the seed is refused, E612, naming the module, the line and the symbol, and nothing is changed. Other foreign symbols are listed and accepted as before (D94b item 9, D101a).
  6. **Names that are not `tomato_*`** are not reserved (agent): the runtime defines none (3), and a name such as `main` of the program's own C entry or a libc function is the program's own business, as `!abs` is. If the runtime ever exports a name without the prefix, the check gets its name too.
- Alternatives: allow `!` only in `std/` (cuts the use of libc and the example `mathlib`, which Davis wanted kept); an opt-in flag such as `--allow-llvm` outside the compatibility promise (a flag every build of a C-using program would carry, and the runtime would still be reachable behind it).
- Cost: one check in `compiler.tom` (`check_reserved_llvm`, called for each declaration and each call) and one in `seeds.tom` (`find_symbols`); two lines in `std/errors.txt` (E611, E612); `compiler/bootstrap/tomatoc.ll` and `site/wasm/*.wasm` regenerated; the tests that declared `!tomato_json_quote`/`!tomato_getpid` outside `std/` use libc functions (`!fmax`, `!getpid`).
- Tests: `tests/negative/foreign_runtime_decl.tom` (native and wasm), `t_foreign_reserved` in `tests/run.sh` (declaration, call without declaration, a module named like a std one, std modules in debug and release, `!abs`, `examples/mathlib`, a seed with `!tomato_getpid`), the foreign-symbol seed tests with `!getpid`.

### D113a — Reserved marker names: a program's own entry may not use them: decided (Davis, language design review 2.1 problem 1, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: language design review 2.1: one `@name` namespace serves entry points (D16a), method markers (`@add`, `@hash`, D12j-l) and `@test` (D81a, D96a), so any marker the language adds after 1.0 (`@bench`, `@example`, `@setup`) could take a name a program already uses as its own entry, a breaking change that D84a forbids within 1.0.x. Marker names were already unique in a program (one function per marker, E583, D96a).
- Decision:
  1. **A reserved list.** `main`, `test`, `bench`, `example`, `setup`, `teardown`, `deprecated`, `init` and every operator and protocol marker the compiler knows (`add`, `sub`, `mul`, `div`, `floordiv`, `mod`, `pow`, `eq`, `lt`, `le`, `gt`, `ge`, `str`, `iter`, `len`, `get`, `slice`, `contains`, `reversed`, `static`, `bitarray`, `set`, `bitand`, `bitor`, `bitxor`, `invert`, `neg`, `rmul`, `hash`). It is published in `docs/specification.md` and `docs/releasing.md`; names are added to it only before 1.0 or in a minor release with a Breaking changes entry, and a new built-in marker after 1.0 takes only a name on the list.
  2. **The rule.** A top-level marker whose first word is reserved is a compile error (E613, a rename hint such as `@bench_sort`), unless the language defines that marker at top level: `@main`, `@test` and `@test.NAME` only. So the dotted forms under a reserved root (`@bench.sort`, `@add.fast`, `@main.extra`, `@init.x`) are errors too, because the language may later define them. A bare operator marker is still E96 (`marks a method`).
  3. **Not reserved:** names that merely start with a reserved word (`@bench_sort`, `@benchmark`, `@hash_it`, `@tests.one`), and any other root (`@greet`, `@calc.add`).
  4. **No decorators.** `@` never means a user-defined decorator: a marker is an entry name or one of the language's own.
- Alternatives: the review's Proposal 1, one fixed `@entry def serve():` form (a bigger break and more to type: rejected by Davis for the reserved list); a second sigil (`@@name`) for user markers (more syntax for a gap that a list closes).
- Cost: programs that used `@bench`-like names, or dotted operator names (`@add.fast`, allowed by D96a), must rename them; no program in the repository did (examples use `@greet`, `@prime`, `@greet.loud`, `@main`, `@test.*`). The check is in the parser (`is_reserved_marker`); the standard library and the repl are subject to it too.
- Tests: `tests/negative/entry_reserved_*` (bench, example, setup, teardown, deprecated, and `bench.sort`, `add.fast`, `main.extra`, `init.fast`, `hash.cache`), `t_reserved_all` (every reserved name dotted), `tests/programs/entry_reserved_ok.tom` (names that only start with a reserved one), the entry check in `tests/run.sh`, and the wasm rejections; debug and WebAssembly paths.
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes language design review 2.1 problem 1; replaces the sentence of D96a that a dotted operator name is an ordinary entry; D16a and D81a stand.
### D114a — One error class tree: `TimeoutError` and `ConnectionError` under `IOError`, and a bare `raise` in a `catch` raises the caught error again: built (decided by Davis, 2026-11-27, language design review 4.1; the exact rules below are the implementer's, to be confirmed)

- Evidence: `asyncio.TimeoutError` and `requests.TimeoutError` were two unrelated classes, so no one `catch` handled "a wait ran out of time"; `requests` had its own `ConnectionError` while `socket` had a `SocketError` family (`ConnectionRefusedError`, `ConnectionResetError`) under `IOError`; there was no way to run code on an error and then let it go on (`catch Error as e: log(e); raise e` makes a new trace that starts at the `raise`, and a `try`/`finally` cannot see the error). A `catch` names classes, so moving a class after 1.0 changes what a program catches: the tree is fixed now. Davis decided on 2026-11-27 (review 4.1): one tree, `IOError` keeps its name (no `OSError`), and a bare `raise` re-raises.
- Decision, the tree (everything not listed is as before):
  1. **Two built-in classes**, in `std/builtin/errors.tom`: `TimeoutError(IOError)` and `ConnectionError(IOError)`, with the inherited `@init` (`TimeoutError('message')`, `TimeoutError('message', 'E7')`). Any program can raise and catch them.
  2. **The libraries' classes extend them**: `asyncio.TimeoutError(TimeoutError)` (E438), `requests.TimeoutError(TimeoutError)` (E360), `requests.ConnectionError(ConnectionError)` (E364), and `socket.SocketError(ConnectionError)` with its subclasses `ConnectionRefusedError`, `ConnectionResetError` and `AddressError` (E441 to E446). `catch TimeoutError` handles both libraries' timeouts and `catch ConnectionError` a refused socket and a failed request alike; `catch asyncio.TimeoutError` still handles only asyncio's. A module class may have the name of the class it extends (`class TimeoutError(TimeoutError)` in `asyncio`): the base is then the built-in of that name (`Mod.parent_key`; before, D108a made the name mean the class itself and the compile ended in a stack overflow).
  3. **The classes whose parent changed** (pre-1.0, so no "Breaking changes" promise is broken; each is still a `RuntimeError`, so `catch RuntimeError` and `catch Error` are unaffected). A catch of a parent class now handles them; a catch of the old parent no longer does:
     | Class | Parent before | Parent now |
     | --- | --- | --- |
     | `asyncio.TimeoutError` | `RuntimeError` | `TimeoutError` (an `IOError`) |
     | `requests.TimeoutError` | `requests.RequestError` | `TimeoutError` (an `IOError`) |
     | `requests.ConnectionError` | `requests.RequestError` | `ConnectionError` (an `IOError`) |
     | `socket.SocketError` (and so `ConnectionRefusedError`, `ConnectionResetError`, `AddressError`) | `IOError` | `ConnectionError` (an `IOError`) |
     | `json.JSONDecodeError`, `JSONEncodeError`, `JSONTypeError`, `MsgpackError` | `RuntimeError` | `ValueError` |
     | `toml.TOMLDecodeError` | `RuntimeError` | `ValueError` |
     | `re.RegexError` | `RuntimeError` | `ValueError` |
     | `cache.CacheMiss` | `RuntimeError` | `KeyError` |
     So `catch IOError` now also handles `asyncio.TimeoutError` (it did not), `catch ValueError` handles a bad JSON, TOML or pattern text, and `catch KeyError` handles a `CacheMiss`; `catch requests.RequestError` no longer handles `requests.TimeoutError` or `requests.ConnectionError` (a class has one parent: catch `requests.RequestError`, `TimeoutError` and `ConnectionError`, or just `IOError`). Ids, messages and fields are unchanged.
  4. **The rule for the others**: an error that is about the world outside the program (files, the network, the terminal) is an `IOError`, with `TimeoutError` and `ConnectionError` for the two failures every library shares; text or data a function was given that is not acceptable is a `ValueError` (the class `struct.StructError` already was one); a lookup that finds nothing is a `KeyError`; anything else about the use of a library (`LockError`, `ProcessError` and `ProcessTimeout` of `sys`, whose program ran but did not succeed, and the `requests` errors that are neither) is a `RuntimeError`. **Unchanged on purpose:** the file errors stay under `IOError` (`FileNotFoundError`, `FileExistsError`, `PermissionError`, `IsADirectoryError`, `NotADirectoryError`, D80a); `AssertionError` is a direct subclass of `Error` (D81a); `TerminalError` is an `IOError`; `requests.RequestError`, `InvalidURL`, `TooManyRedirects` and `HTTPError` stay `IOError`s; `ProcessTimeout` stays a `ProcessError`, not a `TimeoutError` (`sys.run` raises it for a program that ran, and `ProcessError`s are `RuntimeError`s, not `IOError`s, D98a); `SkipTest` is an `Error`. `os`, `ospath`, `io`, `urlparse`, `struct`, `zlib`, `csv`, `datetime`, `hashlib`, `base64`, `hex`, `hmac`, `random` and the other modules raise only the language's classes (`ValueError`, `OverflowError`, `IOError` and its file subclasses, ...), so nothing moves for them.
- Decision, a bare `raise`:
  5. **Where.** `raise` followed by the end of the line is allowed only in the body of a `catch` clause (also inside an `if`, a loop or a nested `try` that is in that body, including the `try` body, `else` and `finally` of a nested statement). Anywhere else it is a compile error, **E614** (`'raise' with no error can only be used inside a 'catch' block`, with the help that it raises the caught error again and elsewhere to write `raise ErrorClass('message')`): outside any `catch`, in the body, `else` or `finally` of the `try` itself (the error is not caught yet, or is already gone), and in a function called from a `catch` (a function has no caught error; pass it the error). A `catch` block of an enclosing function does not count for a nested function, lambda or class method.
  6. **Which error.** The error of the **innermost** `catch` block the `raise` is in. In `catch A as a:` that contains `try: ... catch B as b: raise`, it is `b`; after that inner statement ends, a `raise` in the outer block is `a` again.
  7. **What it keeps.** The same error object (`e is saved` holds), so its class, id, message and fields are unchanged, and the line it was first raised on and, in debug mode, its call trace (`at f (line 4)` ... as it was when caught: the catch discards the trace, the bare `raise` puts it back, so an uncaught error that passed through a rethrow prints the same text as one that was never caught). In release mode the line is kept. Whether the error came from the language (`E01`) or from `raise` (`E11`) is kept too. `finally` blocks of the statement run as for any error leaving a `catch`, after the rethrow; an `await` inside the block does not lose the error; a task that re-raises fails with the original error for whoever awaits it.
  8. **Implementation.** The parser gives a bare `raise` a `Reraise` node and tells the `catch` handler (a fourth field) that it has one; only then does the compiler keep, in two hidden slots, the caught error (one reference more) and a small state object (`tomato_catch_save`: the line, who raised it, the trace). `tomato_catch_restore` puts the state back. A `catch` with no bare `raise` compiles exactly as before.
- Alternatives: (a) rename `IOError` to `OSError` as Python 3 did: Davis chose to keep `IOError`. (b) Multiple parents (`requests.TimeoutError` under both `RequestError` and `TimeoutError`): rejected, the language has single inheritance, and `catch IOError` already handles every `requests` error. (c) Keep the libraries' timeouts unrelated and add only a bare `raise`: rejected, that is the defect 4.1 names. (d) `raise e` as the rethrow: it makes a new trace and its line is the `raise`; it stays allowed and means that. (e) A bare `raise` in a called function re-raises "the error being handled" (Python's rule, a hidden global): rejected, a function that is also called outside a `catch` would be a run-time failure; a compile error is easier to read. (f) `catch ... as e: raise e` rewritten by the compiler: rejected, it would not keep the trace.
- Cost: the classes of the table above have new parents, listed in the changelog; programs that catch exactly `requests.RequestError` for a timeout or connection failure, or `IOError` and wanted not to handle `asyncio.TimeoutError`, change behavior. A program that wrote `class TimeoutError(RuntimeError)` of its own is unaffected (D108a: its own name wins in its module). `std/testing.tom`'s list of class names (`assert_raises`) is in the order of the new tree. The built-in names `TimeoutError` and `ConnectionError` are now taken in every module that does not define them (a program with its own `TimeoutError` class keeps it, D108a), and the highlighter lists carry them.
- Tests: `tests/programs/error_tree.tom` (every moved class is caught by `catch TimeoutError` / `ConnectionError` / `IOError` as it should be, ids and messages unchanged, a library's own catch does not catch the other library's, the JSON, TOML, regex, struct, cache and open errors' places), `bare_raise.tom` (class, id, message and object kept, with variable and without, loops, nested catches, after an inner catch, a `try` in a `catch`, conditional, `finally`), `bare_raise_async.tom` (a task re-raising after an `await`, two tasks, `wait_for`'s timeout), `tests/runtime/bare_raise_line.tom` and `bare_raise_line_rt.tom` (the first line and trace survive the rethrow, for a program's error and a runtime one), `tests/negative/bare_raise_outside`, `bare_raise_in_function`, `bare_raise_in_finally`, `bare_raise_in_try_body` and `tests/errors/bare_raise_outside` (E614); `bare_raise` and `bare_raise_async` also run in release mode with AddressSanitizer, `bare_raise` and `bare_raise_async` through WebAssembly.
- Supersedes / superseded by: settles `docs/language-design-review.md` 4.1 and open question 60; builds on D80a (file errors), D81a (`AssertionError`), D93b (fieldless subclasses inherit `@init`) and D108a (module names shadow built-ins).

### D115a — std's module names are reserved, a project file that shadows a std module warns, and `import x as y` and `from x import a as b` exist: built (decided by Davis, language design review 9.1, 2026-11-27; the rules marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: in the review (9.1) a file `json.tom` next to a program replaced `std/json.tom` silently, `import math as m` was E335, and every module std adds after 1.0 (`http`, `bytes`, `log`) could break a program that had a file or a seed of that name. Davis decided on 2026-11-27: reserve std's future module names, warn when a project file shadows a std module, and add `import x as y` and `from x import a as b`. This also settles what D24b and D24c left open about aliases (qualified names and re-exports stay open) and open question 3 of the seed design for the first two points (`docs/package-registry-design.md` section 19).
- Decision, `as`:
  1. `import m as n` binds only `n`: `n.name` is the exported name, and `m` is not bound in that file (`m.name` is E227, "name 'm' is not defined", with the help "'m' is imported as 'n' in this file, so write n"). `from m import a as b` binds only `b`, and `from m import a as b, c as d, e` mixes renamed and plain names; `a` is not bound (E340 with the same kind of help). The module is still found and compiled under its own name, so a program-wide key, a type name in a message (`math.Angle`) and a file name in an error show the real module, while a name the program wrote shows as written (`m.nope`). It works for std modules, the project's modules and seed modules.
  2. The new name is an ordinary binding of the importing module: it shadows a built-in of the same name in that file (`from math import sqrt as len` makes `len(36.0)` the square root, D108a), and a name the file defines twice or imports twice is still E120 ("'f' is defined twice in module 'main'"). Two `import ... as n` (or an `import n` and an `import x as n`) in one file are E616 ("the name 'n' is already used by the import of the module 'x' on line L", agent). `import m as m` is the plain import. `import a as x, b as y` on one line is not accepted (a plain `import` takes one module, as before).
  3. **A name a function binds wins over the alias** (agent): `import math as m` makes every `m.name` of the file the module's name, but in a function that binds `m` itself (a parameter, a variable it declares or assigns, a loop variable, a `catch ... as m`, a lambda parameter, in any depth of the body) `m.w` and `m.area()` are the variable's field and method, and the module's `m.sqrt` is not reachable there (rename one of them); a function that does not bind `m` is unaffected. It is the function as a whole, as in Python, not the lines after the binding; the parser splits the joined name back (`split_function_prefixes`). The same holds for a plain `import math` and a variable `math`, which was a bug before. A name the *module* defines at its top level (a constant `m`) does not win: `m.name` stays the module's.
  4. The unused-import warning names the renamed form (`the module 'math as m' is imported but never used`).
  5. For the website, the repl, the VS Code grammar and the highlighters nothing changes: `as` was a keyword already (`catch E as e`).
- Decision, reserved names: `std/reserved.txt` is the published list, one `name std` or `name planned` per line: every module std has now (35, `bitarray` included) and 55 names it may add: `http https log logging argparse cli subprocess process path pathlib string types typing enum dataclasses collections functools operator bytes bytearray array queue threading signal tempfile shutil glob fnmatch heapq bisect copy pickle sqlite xml yaml unittest inspect gc abc contextlib weakref decimal fractions numbers locale gettext codecs unicodedata ssl select ipaddress email smtplib` (the recommendation of the review's author, which Davis may trim; adding a name later costs nothing, removing one is a change to the promise). The compiler keeps a copy in `compiler/src/modnames.tom` (the manifest reader and `tomato seed` cannot read `std/` at run time) and `tests/run.sh` checks the two against each other, `std/*.tom` against the `std` names and that no `planned` name is a file under `tests/` or `examples/`.
- Decision, what a reserved name does: (a) a seed whose name has a planned module name as its stem (`acme.example/queue`) is refused when a manifest is read, whether it is the seed's own `tomato.toml` (`tomato seed check`, `seed add`, `install`) or a project's `[seeds]` (E561, the message names `std/reserved.txt` and suggests `queue_x`), and a seed module with any reserved name (a `stem_*` module) is refused by the module check (E579 for an existing std module, as before; E615 for a planned one); the seed naming rule of the design (`name` and `name_*` only) is unchanged. A seed named like an existing std module is still refused where its modules are read (E579) and, in a lock that has it, at compile time (E581). (b) A file of the project (next to the program or in `src/`) with the name of an existing std module is used, as before, and the compiler now warns, once for each name in a build, at the first `import` of it, naming both: "the file json.tom of this project shadows the standard module 'json'". `# allow: module-shadow` on the import line keeps it quiet (the kind is not `shadow`, which is the variable that hides a built-in), a seed's import never warns, and an import written by a std module (a file `time.tom` shadows what `datetime` imports) warns with no place. An alias does not hide it. (c) A file with a name that is reserved but not yet a module (`queue.tom`) is allowed and silent today. When std adds the module, that file keeps shadowing it, with the warning of (b); the program does not change meaning. This is the policy the registry design promised: std checks a new module's name against the index before adding it, and the project that maintains `std/` adds a name here first.
- Alternatives: (a) a prefix (`std.http` beside bare names): rejected, it needs dotted module names and subfolders, which the flat namespace does not have and the seed design rests on. (b) An error, not a warning, for the project file that shadows std: rejected by Davis's request (a warning), and a file may shadow on purpose, as the seed design keeps (`docs/package-registry-design.md` section 5). (c) Refusing a project file with a planned name: rejected, it would stop programs for a module that does not exist. (d) A single list file read by the compiler at run time: not possible for `tomato seed` (no `std` folder is passed to it), so the file is the published list and the compiler's copy is checked against it. (e) `import a, b as c` on one line: not asked for.
- Cost: `import x as y` costs one field on the parser's `Import` node (a list of the names each name is bound to, `''` for the same name) and a prefix instead of the module name in `build_scopes`. Programs that have a file with a std module's name get a new warning (no tests or examples did); no other program changes. `tomatoc` grows by one file (`modnames.tom`); `std_modules` moved there from `compiler.tom`.
- Tests: `tests/programs/import_as.tom` (std, project and builtin-named aliases, a type through an alias, `from ... as` mixed with plain names; debug, release, ASan and wasm), ten rejected programs `tests/negative/import_as_*.tom` and `from_import_as_*.tom`, whole-text messages in `tests/errors/import_as_unused` and `import_as_unknown_member`, `t_module_names` (the warning, `# allow: module-shadow`, an alias, once for each name, a planned name silent), `t_reserved_names` (the list against the compiler and the files) and `t_cli_reserved_seeds` (`tomato seed check` and a project manifest refuse `queue`, `queue-x` is fine).
- Not done (follow-ups): re-exports and `from m import *` (the other half of D24c's open point); `import a.b` and folders of modules; a warning at `tomato seed check` when a seed module shadows a *planned* name of a later std (it is refused outright); the registry's `index-check` still has to use `std/reserved.txt` (design section 1.2 says it copies `std_modules()`); the E616 message does not tell a repeated plain `import` (the old E120) from an alias clash.
- Supersedes / superseded by: settles `docs/language-design-review.md` 9.1 and the seventh item of its ranked list; completes the aliases of D24b and D24c; changes `docs/package-registry-design.md` sections 1.2 and 5 and open question 3.

### D116a — The 2026-11-27 answers on the type test, `!name` and entry markers take P44, P33 and P21 off the unstable list: decided (Davis, 2026-11-27, relayed by his lead agent)

- Evidence: Davis answered the open points of `docs/language-design-review.md` 2.2, 1.6 and 2.1 on 2026-11-27, and each is now recorded and built: the type test is `x is T` (D23i, D110a), `!name` is open for any symbol except the reserved `tomato_*` (D112a), and entry markers keep the `@name` spelling with a reserved list (D113a).
- Decision: the three rows of `docs/provisional.md` (P44, P33, P21, which were U2, U3 and U4 of the unstable list) are promoted to decided and cite those decisions. The unstable list has **4** features (error message wording, the host input protocol, the text `progress_bar` draws, `random.choice`/`shuffle`/`sample`); 61 rows are promoted and 26 removed. `releasing.md`, `changelog.md` and `provisional-triage.md` say "4". The old `x in T` spelling stays a warning through 1.0.x; making it an error or silent is a minor-release change (D23i rule 5).
- Not decided here: the error promise (triage judgment 1) is still an open judgment call for Davis, so error message wording (U1) stays on the list. `bytes` (review 8.1) has no decision yet.
- Also: P15 (class conversion, D12az and D12bh) is recorded as two programs in `tests/known_gaps` and in the Types row of the specification's status table.
- Supersedes / superseded by: narrows D109a's outcome ("7 on the unstable list" is now 4); changes nothing else in it.

### D117a — Seeds: handles and host shortcuts are in for 1.0, maintainers may yank (listing only), owners are git-host owners, `run`/`build` never fetch, a `.tom` file names seeds in a header line; the error promise is adopted: decided (Davis, 2026-11-27)

- Evidence: Davis's answers of 2026-11-27 (relayed by his lead agent) to the open questions of `docs/package-registry-design.md` section 19 and to the proposed error promise of `docs/releasing.md` (`docs/language-design-review.md` 1.2, `docs/provisional-triage.md` judgment call 1).
- Decision:
  1. **Handles (`dave/thing`) and host shortcuts (`gh:`, `gitlab:`, `codeberg:`, `srht:`) are both in for 1.0** (question 8; sections 1.10 and 6). Handles are granted by a reviewed pull request as D100a proposed; shortcuts are expanded at `add` time and never stored.
  2. **The error promise is adopted as proposed.** Error ids never change meaning; error classes are stable within a minor release; message text may be reworded in a patch release (the first line keeps its id). The "proposed" mark in `docs/releasing.md` is gone. The fourth bullet (a decided rule the compiler does not enforce yet is enforced only in a minor release) was part of the proposal and is adopted with it. U1 (error message wording) stays on the unstable list, as the promise says.
  3. **Maintainers may yank a published seed version in an emergency, listing-only** (question 1): the index hides the version, existing locks still build, and the yank is recorded publicly in the index (a signed `[[yank]]` with a reason). Nothing is deleted or edited.
  4. **Owners are git-host owners; no key-only owners** (question 2). Signing keys may come later as an addition. The `k-...` owner of sections 1.3 and 1.8 is not adopted for 1.0.
  5. **`tomato run` and `tomato build` never fetch from the network**; only `tomato seed add` and `tomato seed install` do. A missing seed is an error that tells you to install it (question 5).
  6. **A single `.tom` file names the seeds it needs in a header comment line**, for example `# seeds: dave/thing>=1.2`, read by `tomato run` and `tomato build` (question 3, the file half). New import syntax is still open.
  7. **`tomato seed` is usable from `sys.run` with nothing special** (question 4).
- Not decided, still proposed or open: 5 short names per owner, the 90-day hold after a release, requiring a seed to be listed before it asks for a short name (question 7); whether a client may decline maintainers' yanks; the details of the `# seeds:` line (several seeds, how it meets `tomato.toml`); questions 3 (syntax), 9, 10 and 11.
- Built: nothing. This task changed documents only. Implementation work is listed in the stages of the design (stage 4 for handles, shortcuts and the header; stage 5 for the emergency yank) and in `TODO.md`.
- Alternatives: none were offered beyond the questions' own recommendations; Davis's answers for 1, 2 and 3 differ from them (2 recommended accepting key owners; 1 recommended a signed `malware`/`legal` yank by security keys that may stop builds).
- Cost: the older sections of the design that describe key owners, the `malware` yank that stops a build and key-owner endorsement weights are not rewritten yet and say so at the top; stage 4 must reconcile them before it is built. **Done by D123a** (the design was rewritten the same day).
- Supersedes / superseded by: answers and narrows D89a and D100a; D109a's "error promise (proposed)" is now adopted.

### D118a — An optional of an interface (`Named | None`) takes `None`, a `Named` value or an instance of a class that implements `Named`: built (a bug fix, agent's reading; no new rule)

- Evidence: found while testing D110a (`x is Class` checks the dynamic class): `def f(n: Named | None)` called with a `Tag` (a class that implements `Named`) or a `Named` value, and `tag: Named | None = Tag()`, were E324 ("has type Tag, but Named | None is expected"), though `Tag | None` takes a `Tag` and `Named` takes a `Tag`. D23b already says an optional of an interface is the same pointer as the interface value (null for `None`), so no conversion was needed; the check compared the whole type text `%Named?` with the interface name and so refused everything but `None`.
- Decision: a value is assignable to `I | None` when it is `None` or assignable to `I`; and a `C | None` or `I2 | None` is assignable to `I | None` when `C` or `I2` is assignable to `I`. It is the rule every other optional already follows (`T` and `None` are assignable to `T | None`), now also for interfaces, in arguments, variables, returns, fields and list items. The other direction is unchanged: `Named | None` is not a `Tag | None` (E324), and a class that does not implement the interface is still refused, with the same message (`the value has type Plain, but Named | None is expected`). Narrowing by `is None` and `x is Class` already worked on such a parameter and is not changed.
- Alternatives: none considered; refusing was the bug.
- Cost: none at run time (no conversion code); the check in `assignable` gets one case.
- Also: the `unreachable` item of the same task turned out not to be a bug. After a `try` whose body always leaves and whose `catch` clauses all leave, the next statement can never run even when the catches do not cover every error class: an error that is not caught leaves the function too. The check is right; `tests/errors/unreachable_after_try_catch` pins it, and `try_catch_can_fall_through` pins the cases that must stay silent (a body that can finish, a catch that does not leave).
- Tests: `tests/programs/optional_interface.tom` (all the places a value can go, `is` tests, a field, ASan, wasm), `tests/errors/optional_interface_class_mismatch` and `optional_interface_variable_mismatch` (whole text of both refusals), `tests/errors/unreachable_after_try_catch` and `try_catch_can_fall_through`.
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes D23b for interfaces; changes nothing else.

### D121a — The compiler warns once per build when a program uses a feature on the unstable list: built (the list is Davis's, D109a; the warning is the agent's design, review 1.1's suggestion)

- Evidence: `docs/language-design-review.md` 1.1 suggested a compiler warning for the unstable features, as Rust gates unstable ones, and `docs/provisional-triage.md` left it as a follow-up. The TODO item asked for the warning, a way to silence it and the list kept in one place.
- Decision (agent): a program that uses a feature on the unstable list (`docs/provisional.md`, section 1) gets a warning of the kind `unstable`, once for each feature in a build, at the first use, naming the feature and what may change: `warning: 'progress_bar' is an unstable feature (U3, the text progress_bar draws)`. It is a warning (exit status and output are unchanged), shown before the program runs like every warning. It is given for U3 (`itertools.progress_bar`) and U4 (`random.choice`, `shuffle`, `sample`); U1 (error message wording) and U2 (the host input protocol) are not written in a program, so no text of a program can use them and they have no warning. Nothing promoted, decided or removed ever warns.
- Silencing follows the existing warnings: `# allow: unstable` on the line of the use hides that use (the next use on a line without it warns instead), and `--no-warnings` or `TOMATO_WARNINGS=off` hides every warning; the repl and a seed's code never show it, and neither does a standard library module.
- What counts as a use: `from module import name` (the import line, whatever the alias; one comment there silences every later use of the name) and a dotted `module.name` or `alias.name` of `import module` in a call, a value or a type. A project file with the name of the std module (which `module-shadow` already warns about) is its own code, not the unstable function.
- One list: `compiler/src/unstable.tom` (a module of data the compiler imports, like `modnames.tom`) has a row for each feature (id, words, the `module.name` names that mean a use, what may change). `tests/run.sh` (`t_unstable_features`) checks that its ids, the count in `docs/provisional.md` and the names in the feature column of the table agree, and that each name is exported by its std module. Adding a feature means one row there and one row in the table.
- Alternatives: (a) a data file `std/unstable.txt` the compiler reads at run time, like `std/errors.txt`: it would be the single file, but the native install, the release build, `site/wasm/build.sh` and the wasm file list each have to carry it, and a missing file would stop every compile. (b) A lint kind in `compiler/src/lint.tom`: the linter reads one file's syntax tree and has no resolved names, so `import random as r` and `from itertools import progress_bar as pb` would be missed or need a copy of the import logic. (c) A warning at every use: a loop over `progress_bar` in ten places would be ten warnings; once for each feature names it without noise.
