- Cost: a bootstrap IR refresh and a wasm rebuild (the compiler changed); the tests, examples and `tests/programs` that use the two features carry `# allow: unstable` so that they still compile with no warning; a local variable named like an imported unstable function is read as a use (rare, harmless: a warning that can be silenced).
- Tests: `tests/errors/unstable_use`, `unstable_dotted`, `unstable_allow` (the whole text) and `unstable_quiet` (no output: stable parts of `random` and `itertools`, and a project's own `choice` and `progress_bar`); `t_unstable_features` (the list against the docs, a project file, a project file named `random.tom`); `t_cli_unstable` (`tomato run`, `--no-warnings`, `TOMATO_WARNINGS=off`, the allow comment, the repl); `t_wasm_unstable` (the same through WebAssembly).
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes the follow-up of D109a; leaves the unstable list itself as D116a left it.

### D122a — The std names that would freeze at 1.0: `datetime` keeps its free functions, `math` drops `abs`, `min`, `max` and `pow`, `testing._run_all` is not exported, and `json` and `toml` read into and write from one `value` type: built (decided by Davis, 2026-11-27, language design review 2.3 and 10; the choices marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: the review's item 13 ("std names that would freeze (2.3, 10)"): after 1.0 a std name cannot be renamed. Davis decided on 2026-11-27 four things: (1) `datetime` has free functions only, with no `date.fromisoformat`-style methods, as one documented style; (2) `abs`, `min`, `max` and `pow` live in the built-ins only, and `math` keeps its float functions; (3) the test module's `_run_all` is not exported, and the runner reaches it another way; (4) `json` and `toml` each had a dynamic value type of their own, and become one shared type in a new std module.
- Decision:
  1. **`datetime`: free functions, one style (no code change in the module).** `date_fromisoformat`, `time_fromisoformat`, `datetime_fromisoformat`, `datetime_strptime`, `date_today`, `datetime_now` and `datetime_combine` stay as the final names, and no method of the same job is added (no `date.fromisoformat`, `datetime.now`, `datetime.combine`): a class method called on a class name would need the real `@static` the review's 2.3 describes, and one way of doing it is the promise. The module's head says so. *Agent:* a program that writes the Python spelling gets E115 as before (`'datetime.date' has no method 'fromisoformat'`) with a help line that names the free functions (`moved_method_hint` in `compiler/src/compiler.tom`), since that is the mistake a Python programmer makes.
  2. **`math` has no `abs`, `min`, `max` or `pow`.** The first three are removed from `std/math.tom` (`gcd` and the others now call the built-in `abs`): `abs`, `min` and `max` were built in already, and the `math` ones took ints only (`math.min(1.5, 2.5)` was a type error), so `from math import min` quietly gave the narrower function. `math.pow(base, exponent)` (ints only; a negative exponent raised E41) is removed too. **There is no built-in `pow`** (the compiler says "'pow' is not built in", E368), and none is added here (*agent*): `**` is the power operator, and a new built-in name is one more name to freeze. If Davis wants a built-in `pow(base, exponent[, modulus])` as Python has, it adds on top of this without breaking anything. A program that still imports or calls the old names gets the usual "module 'math' has no 'abs'" (E226, or "the function 'math.abs' is not defined", E340 for a qualified call) with a help line: for `abs`, `min` and `max` "is built in, and math no longer has its own: take it out of the import and call abs() as it is", for `pow` "math has no pow: write base ** exponent, such as 2 ** 10" (`moved_name_hint`). `math` keeps `gcd`, `lcm`, the primes, `isqrt`, `factorial`, `comb`, `perm`, `prod`, `fsum`, the angle classes and every float function.
  3. **`testing._run_all` is not exported.** The function stays, and the test driver the compiler writes for the entry `test` (D81a, D96a) still calls `testing._run_all(names, functions, args)`: when the entry is `test` the compiler marks that one function exported before it builds the scopes (`Mod.test_driver`, in `compile_with`; an `import testing` is expanded from those marks), so `tomato test`, the repl, the playground and the WebAssembly build, which all compile through `compile_with`, are unchanged. No other program can call it: in a run or a build `testing._run_all(...)` is "module 'testing' does not export '_run_all'" (E225, with the help line "a name that starts with an underscore is internal to testing.tom" instead of the advice to add `export`, which a user of std cannot follow). *Agent:* in a program compiled with the entry `test` the name is reachable by hand too, since the driver and the program are one module; that is a hole nobody can use by accident (the name starts with an underscore, is not listed in the module's exports, and is documented as internal). Chosen over (a) an exported `testing.run_all`, which would be a new public name to freeze, and (b) a language rule that a leading underscore hides an export (the review's alternative): that decides a rule for every module and every seed, which is a different decision.
  4. **One dynamic value type, in `std/value.tom`.** `json.Json` and its seven subclasses and `toml.Toml` are gone; both modules read into, and write from, the classes of `value`: a base class `Value` and the kinds `Null`, `Bool`, `Int`, `Float`, `Str`, `List` and `Dict` (what both formats have), `Bin`, `Ext` and `Timestamp` (MessagePack's, from `json`), and `Moment` (TOML's four date and time types, from `toml`, with its field `kind` renamed `form` because `kind()` is the method every value has). The name `value` was chosen because the class is `Value` and Rust's `serde_json::Value` and `toml::Value` are what a reader expects; no std module, seed name in the design, example or test used `value`, and it is a std module name like the others (D115a): it is listed as `value std` in `std/reserved.txt` and in `std_modules()` of `compiler/src/modnames.tom` (a seed may not provide a module of that name, and a project file called `value.tom` shadows it with D115a's warning), and in the website's and the highlighters' lists of module names. A program that has a variable or parameter named `value` is not affected: only `import value` binds the module's name, and `from value import Value` binds just the classes.
     - *The pattern is `json`'s, which `toml` now follows:* `v['key']` and `v[0]` (`@get`), `v.get('key')` (the value, or `None` for a missing key), `'key' in v`, `len(v)`, the `as_bool`, `as_int`, `as_float` (an `Int` is accepted), `as_str`, `as_list` and `as_dict` methods (the last two give the list and the dict the value holds, not copies, so `v.as_dict()['k'] = Str('x')` changes the value), `as_bytes`, `ext_type`, `seconds`, `nanos` and `as_moment` for the rarer kinds, and `kind()` (the strings `json` already used, and a Moment's form). Making a value is a constructor call (`Dict({'a': Int(1)})`, `List([])`). What `toml` had besides, and `json` did not, is gone: the builder functions `table()`, `array()`, `string()`, `integer()`, `real()`, `boolean()` and `moment()`, the methods `put`, `append`, `keys`, `lookup`, `at`, `is_table`, `is_array`, `describe`, `show` and the fields `kind`, `text`, `num`, `real`, `flag`, `items`, `order`, `fields` of a `Toml`; `Moment`'s `text()` is now `moment_text()` (`str(m)` still writes it).
     - *Errors.* A value used as the wrong kind, a missing key, a key of the wrong kind and an index out of range are a `ValueTypeError` (a `ValueError`, like `JSONTypeError` since D114a, in `value`; E71 to E75, E365, E366 and, for `as_float` on a TOML `nan` or infinity, E66): the ids that were `JSONTypeError`'s, now with the class of the shared type. `toml`'s own `ValueError` E64/E65/E69, `KeyError` E67 and `IndexError` E68 for the same mistakes are retired (their ids stay, as the table's other retired ones do). `catch JSONTypeError` and `catch KeyError` around a TOML lookup become `catch ValueTypeError`.
     - *Writers refuse what their format cannot hold* (new ids at the end of `std/errors.txt`): `json.dumps` of a `Moment` is a `JSONEncodeError` (E617), of a TOML `nan` or infinity E81, as for a float JSON cannot write; `json.dumps_msgpack` of a `Moment` is a `MsgpackError` (E618), of a TOML `nan` or infinity E619; `toml.dumps` and `toml.dumps_value` of a `Null`, `Bin`, `Ext` or `Timestamp` is a `ValueError` (E620, "a null value cannot be written as TOML"), next to the existing E70 (`dumps` needs a table) and E527. `Bin`, `Ext` and `Timestamp` keep their E77 to E79 for JSON text.
     - *Display and equality (agent).* `print(v)` and `str(v)` give compact JSON-style text for every kind: JSON's own text for the seven common kinds, a date or time as TOML writes it, a TOML `nan` or infinity as TOML writes it, and `<bin of 3 bytes>`, `<ext 5 of 1 bytes>`, `<timestamp 10 s 5 ns>` for the rest (a `Bin` used to raise `JSONEncodeError` when printed). A TOML value used to print as TOML (`print(doc['server'])` gave `{ port = 8080 }`); it now prints `{"port":8080}`. The shared type has `==`, which `Toml` had and `Json` did not: kind and content, a `Dict` ignoring key order, a `Float` by value and `special`; `Int(1) == Float(1.0)` is False.
     - *`toml.loads` keeps its parser.* The parser builds its private tree (the old class, now `_Node`, with the bookkeeping TOML's rules need for how a table came to be) and `loads` turns it into a `Value` at the end; so no case of `tests/programs/toml_valid.tom`, `toml_invalid.tom` or `toml_big.tom` changed its output, and a document is read exactly as before. `dumps` writes the same layout (plain values, then `[tables]`, then `[[arrays of tables]]`) and `json.dumps` and `dumps_msgpack` the same bytes.
- Compatibility note (for the release notes): a program that imports `Json`, `JsonInt` and the other `Json*` classes from `json` imports `Value`, `Int`, `Str`, `List`, `Dict`, `Bool`, `Float`, `Null`, `Bin`, `Ext` and `Timestamp` from `value` instead, and `JSONTypeError` becomes `ValueTypeError`; a program that imports `Toml`, `Moment` or the builders from `toml` uses `value` the same way; a program that imports `abs`, `min`, `max` or `pow` from `math` removes them (and writes `**` for `pow`). Nothing else in `json`, `toml`, `math` or `testing` changed: `loads`, `load`, `dumps`, `dump`, `dumps_value`, the MessagePack functions, the decode and encode errors and every other `math` function are as before.
- Alternatives: (1) Real `@static` and `date.fromisoformat`: not what Davis decided, and a compiler change (2.3). (2) Keep `math.abs` and friends and document that they are int-only: they would be a second function of one name, which is the problem. Renaming them (`ipow`): nobody asked for them. (3) For `pow`, add a built-in `pow`: see the *agent* note in point 2. (4) For the value type, keep two classes and share only an interface: both formats' readers, writers and accessors would still be written twice, and a document could not go from one format to the other without a copy. Put the MessagePack and date kinds in their format's module as subclasses of a smaller shared `Value`: it works for `Bin` (an accessor is enough), but `as_moment()` on the base class needs `Moment` there, and one place that lists every kind is simpler to read and to freeze. A single class with a `kind` string, as `Toml` was: no `x is Str` test and no per-kind methods, and it keeps a union-shaped record of unused fields.
- Cost: `json.tom` lost its class hierarchy's methods (`dump_to`, `pack_to`) to two functions each that dispatch on `kind()`; one more std module (about 460 lines, with every accessor in one place); a `Value` is about as large as a `Json` was (one object per node), and `toml.loads` builds its private tree and then the `Value` tree, so a large document takes a second pass and about twice the memory during the read (`tests/programs/toml_big.tom`, a megabyte-sized document, runs as before). The bootstrap IR changed because the compiler reads TOML (`config.tom`, `manifest.tom`, `seeds.tom` use `Value` now, with the same error texts, `value_word` and `toml_text` standing in for the old `describe()` and `show()`), and because of the three hints and the driver's access; the wasm files were rebuilt.
- Found while testing, not changed (follow-up): none that matter; the `testing._run_all` hole of point 3 is the one to know.
- Tests: `tests/programs/value_shared.tom` (one tree from both readers and `==` between them, each format writing what the other read, the same accessors on both, `str()`, the kinds only one format has, every kind through all three writers with its error ids, the errors from either reader, equality, key order), `toml_api.tom` (the accessors and the building of a document on `Value`, and the TOML writer's refusals), `toml_show.tom` and its three cases (output unchanged), `json_access.tom`, `msgpack_*` and `json_*` (renamed classes, output unchanged), `math_builtins.tom`; rejected programs `math_pow_moved`, `math_pow_aliased` (`import math as m`), `math_abs_moved`, `math_max_qualified`, `datetime_static_method`, `testing_run_all_private`, `value_toml_builder_gone` and `json_class_gone` (also through WebAssembly for the first five); the existing `tomato test` checks (`testing_lib`, `testing_diff`, the CLI contract's test cases and the WebAssembly `--entry=test` checks) cover the driver.
- Supersedes / superseded by: settles the review's item 13 (2.3 and 10: the `datetime` names, `math.pow` and friends, `testing._run_all`, the two dynamic value types); replaces D66a's `Json` class hierarchy and D62a's `Toml` class and `Moment` as the way these two libraries are read (their formats and parsers are as decided there); D49c's remark that "the integer-only helpers `abs`, `min`, `max` and `pow` already in `math` are unchanged" no longer holds; D108a's note that `math` defines `abs`, `min` and `max` and sees its own no longer applies (`tests/programs/shadow_std.tom` still defines built-in names in other modules).

### D123a — The registry design is rewritten to match D117a: no key owners, a maintainer's yank is listing-only and never stops a build: designed (agent's design from Davis's answers; documents only)

- Evidence: the TODO items "Rewrite sections 4, 17, 18.9 and the key-owner sections of the registry design to match D117a" and "Add the maintainers' emergency yank (listing-only) to the seed index plan". Nothing in the compiler, `tomato` or the tests changed.
- Decision (what the rewrite settles, all in `docs/package-registry-design.md`):
  1. **Owners are a domain or a handle.** The `k-...` key owner, its proof of work, its daily cap, its 0.25 endorsement weight and `claim --key` are removed or marked *not adopted for 1.0* (sections 1.1 to 1.3, 1.5, 1.6, 1.8, 17, 18.6, 18.7); a handle is how an author with no domain gets an owner. A key stays the signer of every statement, never an identity.
  2. **A yank never stops `run`, `build` or `install --locked`.** `run` and `build` never read the index (D117a, question 5), so a yank can only be seen where the local index is read: `install`, `verify`, `outdated`, `tree` warn; `add`, `update` and `search` skip the version. The lock records no yank status.
  3. **The maintainers' emergency yank** is a signed `[[yank]]` (and `[[unyank]]`) in a new file `yanks.toml` on the reviewed path, kind `security` or `malware`, with a public reason; `index-check` accepts it only with a maintainer's signature, for an existing version, as an appended statement. Section 18.9.4, stages 4 (client) and 5 (registry) of the plan.
  4. **Replaced rule:** the old "a `malware` yank is an error unless `--allow-yanked`" is gone. An *owner's* `malware` yank makes `install` and `add` refuse to fetch that version unless `--allow-yanked` is given (a fetch brings code in; a build does not); a maintainer's yank only warns. This is the one consequence of D117a that Davis did not state; it is **proposed**.
  5. **A `# seeds:` header** is described (section 5) as decided in principle, with its grammar left to the implementer.
- Not decided, still proposed (as in D117a): 5 short names per owner, the 90-day hold after a release, requiring a seed to be listed before it asks for a short name; also whether a client may decline maintainers' yanks (proposed: a user-file setting `honor_maintainer_yanks`, default true), a handle's endorsement weight (proposed 1), and the details of the header line.
- Alternatives: keep the key owner with a limit (rejected: Davis's answer); let a maintainer's `malware` yank stop builds as the first recommendation had it (rejected: Davis's answer); store a maintainer's yank in the seed's own file (rejected: that file is the owner's, on the open path, and a maintainer's statement there would mix the two paths).
- Cost: none to code. One more file in the index and one more setting; the owner's `malware` yank is weaker at the build than the first design said.
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes D117a's "older sections are not rewritten yet"; narrows D89a and D100a where they mention key owners.

### D124a — Seed host shortcuts, the handle grammar and the `# seeds:` line are built as far as they can be without a registry: built (the request is Davis's D117a; the choices marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed)

- Evidence: D117a decided that handles (`dave/thing`) and the host shortcuts `gh:`, `gitlab:`, `codeberg:` and `srht:` are in for 1.0, that a single `.tom` file names its seeds in a header comment line read by `tomato run` and `build`, and that these never fetch. The registry (stage 4 of `docs/package-registry-design.md`) is not built, so the parts that need a hosted index (looking a handle up, a handle's grant) cannot be. `run` and `build` already never touched the network (they read the lock and the store; the suite checks that `git` is not started).
- Decision (what is built; sheet in `docs/manifest.md`, "Host shortcuts, handles and the `# seeds:` line"):
  1. **Host shortcuts in `tomato seed add`.** `gh:USER/REPO`, `gitlab:USER/REPO`, `codeberg:USER/REPO` and `srht:~USER/REPO` become `https://github.com/USER/REPO.git`, `https://gitlab.com/USER/REPO.git`, `https://codeberg.org/USER/REPO.git` and `https://git.sr.ht/~USER/REPO` before anything else; only that URL is written to `tomato.toml` and `tomato.lock` (design section 6). The source is unregistered, like `--git`.
  2. **Handle grammar and the registry name.** A handle is `[a-z][a-z0-9-]{1,31}` with no dot, not `k-...`, not `local`, `std`, `tomato` or a name in `std/reserved.txt`. `tomato seed add dave/thing[@VERSION]` (or a domain owner's name) recognises a registry name and stops with E571 saying there is no registry and how to add the seed today; `tomato seed check` accepts a handle-shaped owner with a note that a registry has to grant it. Looking a handle up, the grant (`request-handle`, the pull request) and the lock's `index` field wait for stage 4.
  3. **The `# seeds:` line.** `# seeds: dave/thing>=1.2, acme.example/json5` in the leading comment block of the program (blank and `#` lines from the top, a `#!` line first allowed) is read by `tomato run`, `build` and `compile`; it is checked against the project's `tomato.toml` and `tomato.lock` and never fetches. A seed it names that the project does not list, a locked version below the minimum (the rule of `[seeds]`: the same or a later version of the same compatible line), a line that cannot be read, and a program with no project at all are E622; a listed seed that is not locked or not in the store is E569, `run: tomato seed install`, as for every build.
- Choices the implementer made where D117a and the design were silent (**agent**, to be confirmed by Davis):
  1. **The line names a seed and a minimum, never a source.** With no registry a name cannot be turned into a URL, so the project (`tomato.toml` and the lock) says where each seed comes from, and the line is the program's statement of what it needs. The consequence is that **a single `.tom` file outside any project cannot use the line yet** (E622, with the way to start a project); when the registry exists, `tomato seed install` for a lone program, resolved by the index and locked beside the file or in the user's folder, is the natural next step, and it is not designed here.
  2. **Grammar:** comma-separated `NAME` or `NAME>=VERSION` (`>=` is the only comparison; `1.2` means `1.2.0`; no host shortcut or URL in the line); several `# seeds:` lines add up; a name twice keeps the higher minimum; only the program's own file is read; the block ends at the first other line, so the line must be at the top.
  3. **Shortcut shape:** exactly USER/REPO of letters, digits, dots, underscores and hyphens (a deeper GitLab path is `--git` and `--subdir`), no `.git` suffix, and `add` still needs `--rev`, `--tag` or `--branch` (the default branch is not guessed). The user's `[git-hosts]` table (design section 13) is not built.
  4. **Reserved handle names** are the design's list plus everything in `std/reserved.txt` (a superset of "a std module name").
- Not decided here: the rest of question 3 (import syntax with the owner), a handle's grant, the 5-names limit and the 90-day hold, and where a lone program's lock lives.
- Built: `compiler/src/seeds.tom` (`expand_shortcut`, `handle_problem`, `add_spec_from_word`, `header_seeds`, `check_header`), `compiler/src/ctool.tom` (the `word` form of `seed add`, the header in `compile`), `native/tomato` (a word after `seed add`), E621 and E622 in `std/errors.txt`.
- Alternatives: (a) resolve a header name through a local index file or a `[registry]` table now: rejected, it invents the index format before stage 4. (b) Ignore the line with no project: rejected, the program would fail later with "cannot find the module", the opposite of a clear error. (c) A header that adds the seed to the project's `[seeds]`: rejected, a build must not edit the manifest and "run and build never change anything" is the point of the lock.
- Cost: the line is only a check today, so for a project it repeats what `[seeds]` says; and an unproject script cannot use it until the registry client exists. The browser reads the line as a comment.
- Tests: `tests/run.sh` `t_cli_seed_handles` (the four shortcuts through a `git` shim that serves a local repository, so no network; every refused form; the line in `run` and `build`: accepted forms, each error, `git` never started, a missing store copy, a program with no project) and the changed `seed add` messages in `t_cli_seed_store`; the `tomato help` golden of the CLI contract.
- Supersedes / superseded by: builds D117a items 1 and 6 (as far as the registry allows); the open parts stay in `TODO.md`.
### D125a — A local variable or parameter shadows a built-in, a function or a class of its name everywhere, calls included; the names of the built-in types stay reserved for variables too: built (decided by Davis, 2026-11-27; the details marked "agent" are the implementer's, to be confirmed)

- Evidence: D108a left locals as they were: `def f(len: int): return len + 1` worked, but after `len = 3` the call `len([1, 2])` still reached the built-in, so the `shadow` warning's "which it hides here" was true for reads and false for calls, and the TODO item "Decide whether local variables and parameters shadow builtins in calls" asked Davis. He decided on 2026-11-27: local variables and parameters named like a built-in shadow it everywhere, as in Python: the innermost name wins, in calls too (`max = 5` then `max` is the int; `def f(len: int): return len + 1`), and calling a local that is not callable is an ordinary type error.
- Decision, the rules:
  1. **Locals win.** Inside a function (a method, a lambda's body included) a call `name(...)` whose `name` is a parameter or a variable of that function is a call of that variable: when it holds a function value (`fn(int) -> int`) it is called as that value, otherwise it is E337 ("'len' is a variable, not a function, so it cannot be called"), with the extra line "the variable hides the function or class 'len' here; rename the variable to call it" when a built-in, function or class has that name. This holds for built-ins (`print`, `open`, `len`, `range`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `sorted`, `map`, `abs`, `hash`, ...), for a function or class of the module and for one imported. Before, `print`, `open`, `len`, `range`, `hash`, `round`, `abs` and the others that are compiled in place went to the built-in whatever the variable held (the type names are rule 3). The `shadow` warning is unchanged and now says what is true.
  2. **Which variables.** Every parameter and every variable the function binds: assignment and declaration targets, loop variables, a comprehension's variable (a variable of the function, D53c, so it stays after the comprehension as it does after a `for`), the names a `for a, b in ...` loop and `a, b = ...` bind, a `catch ... as e` name. A lambda's parameters are its own names (`key=len -> len(len)` is a call of the parameter); a lambda cannot reach the variables of its function (E151), so a variable `len` of the function is not seen by the lambda, which sees the built-in. A method or a field named `len` is a method: the dot never looks at variables (`x.len()`).
  3. **Type names stay reserved, for variables too (agent).** A parameter, variable, loop variable, comprehension variable, `catch` name or lambda parameter named `int`, `uint`, `float`, `bool`, `str`, `File`, `list`, `dict`, `set` or `tuple` is E623 ("'str' is the name of a built-in type, so a variable cannot have it"), the variable's twin of E608 (D108a). They were allowed before, with the warning. Reason: the same as D108a rule 3, a type name is written in annotations and means the type in every conversion (`int(x)`, `str(x)`, `list(x)`) and every f-string, so a variable `str` would either break the conversions in its function or make `str` mean two things in one function; Python allows it and pays with `str(x)` stopping to work after `str = 'abc'`. Keeping the type names out of reach is also what lets the compiler's own f-strings and unpacking checks use `str` safely. The alternative, Python's behaviour for these too, would make a call of `str(x)` a call of the variable. It is a breaking change only for a program that named a variable like a type, which the compiler warned about; it is easy to loosen (delete the check in `Gen.slot`).
  4. **Where the shadowing starts (agent).** A variable hides the name in the whole function, as in Python, for the calls and shortcuts that look at the function as a whole (the release-mode loop nests, borrowed `range` loops, `len` of a local list are not used when the function has a variable `len` or `range`); the order of the statements decides the rest, as it does for every variable in Tomato: a name read before its first assignment is not a variable yet (E227 "name is not defined"), so `total = total(xs) + 1` on the first assignment calls the module's `total` on the right (the variable is bound after the right side is computed) and `total(xs)` after it is the variable. Python would raise UnboundLocalError there; Tomato's order rule gives the useful program.
  5. **The compiler's own code stays immune.** What the lexer and the parser write (f-strings' `str` and `_format_spec`, `assert`'s `AssertionError`, the length check of unpacking and of `for a, b in pairs` (`len`, `ValueError`, `str`), `for i, x in enumerate(xs)` and `for a, b in zip(xs, ys)` (`range`, `len`)) is marked "core" (D108a rule 6) and is never the user's variable. A function that has a variable or parameter named `enumerate` or `zip` is not rewritten: `for a, b in zip(xs, ys)` there is an ordinary loop over what the variable holds (`pair_locals` in `compiler/src/parser.tom`). The repl and the website playground show a bare expression with `_core_print(expr)`, which the lexer reads as the built-in `print` marked core (it landed as its own change), so a session that has `print = 3` or `def print` still shows its values. The helpers of the built-ins are compiled in the module `builtin`, which sees no program name.
- Not decided here, because they do not exist: closures and inner functions (E149 stops a function declared in a block), and a scope narrower than the function (Tomato's variables are function-wide, D53c, which is why a comprehension variable is visible afterwards).
- Alternatives: (a) Locals shadow only after the point they are bound (Python's actual rule for reads is function-wide too, so this is not the Python rule) was rejected for the look-ahead shortcuts, which then needed ordering rules of their own; the order rule of rule 4 is only the existing one. (b) Locals do not shadow in calls (D108a's rule, unchanged): rejected by Davis, 2026-11-27. (c) A compile error for a local of a built-in's name: rejected by Davis (Python's rule). (d) Shadowing type names as well: rule 3. 
- Cost: a program that had a variable named like a built-in and called the built-in in the same function now gets E337 (it worked before; the compiler warned about the name). A program with a variable named like a type now gets E623. Nothing else changes: the compiled IR of every program in `tests/programs`, `tests/runtime` and `examples` is the same as before, except that a function with a variable `len` or `range` gets no loop nest in release mode (slower, not different). The compiler itself is unchanged in output (the fixed point holds).
- Tests: `tests/programs/shadow_locals.tom` (a parameter, an assigned variable, a declared one, a loop variable, a comprehension variable, a lambda parameter, a `catch` name, unpacked names, a method's parameter, each holding a function and called, with `len`, `max`, `print`, `range`, `sum`, `abs`, a module function and a class name; plain values named `max` and `sum` next to the built-ins used elsewhere; the module's own function before and after a variable takes its name), `tests/programs/shadow_core.tom` (variables named `range`, `len`, `print`, `iter`, `str`-using code: f-strings with and without formats, `assert` that holds and fails, unpacking, `enumerate` and `zip` loops and comprehensions over them, `range` loops, `@iter` and `@len`, comprehensions, lambdas), both in debug mode, in release mode with AddressSanitizer and through WebAssembly; `tests/negative/shadow_local_*.tom` (twelve: each binding form called, a function and a method, the order rule, a lambda reaching the variable of its function, a variable `zip` in a `for`) and `tests/negative/shadow_type_*.tom` (six: E623 for a parameter, variable, loop variable, `catch` name, lambda parameter and method parameter); the first of each also through WebAssembly; a repl check that `print = 3` does not stop the echo.
- Supersedes / superseded by: supersedes D108a's rule 4 and its follow-up about local variables (both edited to point here); settles the TODO item "Decide whether local variables and parameters shadow builtins in calls".
### D17e — `bytes`, an immutable built-in type for binary data, and every binary std API moved to it before 1.0: decided (Davis, language design review 8.1, 2026-11-27); the details marked "default" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed

- Evidence: in the language design review (`docs/language-design-review.md`, 8.1, "There is no `bytes` type", ranked "Blocks 1.0") Davis decided on 2026-11-27 that 1.0 gets a real immutable `bytes` and that the binary std APIs move to it before the compatibility promise starts, one way only (no old `list[int]` signatures kept beside the new ones), with the design below as the default; his lead agent relayed it.
- Decision:
  1. **The type.** `bytes` is an immutable, reference-counted, contiguous run of bytes, not a list of ints. It is laid out exactly as a text is (`{header, length, count, the bytes}`, type code 1) with the count equal to the length, so concatenation, comparison, hashing, `len` and dict and set keys are the text's code, which looks only at the bytes. Everything that makes new bytes keeps the count equal to the length (the text helpers that count UTF-8 characters are not used for bytes). `bytes` is a type name like `str`: it can be a parameter, field, list item, dict key, set element, optional (`bytes | None`) and union member (`x is bytes`), and a module cannot define the name (D108a).
  2. **Literals.** `b'...'` and `b"..."` with the escapes of a text (`\n \t \r \0 \\ \' \"`) and `\xHH` (two hex digits, either case). Only printable ASCII characters (and a tab) may be written as themselves: any other character is a compile error that says to write `\xHH` or to encode a text (`'é'.encode()`). No raw or multi-line form, no `f`/`b` combination.
  3. **Operations.** `len(b)`; `b[i]` is an `int` from 0 to 255 (a negative index counts from the end; outside the range is `IndexError`, as for lists); `b[i:j:k]` is `bytes` with Python's slice rules; `a + b` and `b * n`/`n * b`; `== != < <= > >=` compare the bytes in order (shorter first on a tie); `x in b` takes an `int` (a byte: anything outside 0 to 255 is `ValueError`, as Python) or `bytes` (a run of bytes, the empty one is in every `bytes`); `for x in b` gives the ints; `hash(b)`; `print(b)`, `str(b)` and an f-string show Python's repr, `b'...'` (the quote is `'` unless the bytes hold a `'` and no `"`; printable ASCII as itself, `\t \n \r`, a backslash and the quote escaped, every other byte `\xhh`), also inside a printed list, dict, set, tuple or union. Bytes and text never mix: `b'a' + 'a'` and `b'a' == 'a'` are compile errors whose hint is to encode or decode. `sum`, `min`, `max` and `sorted` take bytes (as their list of ints), and `sorted`/`sort()`, `min` and `max` take a list of bytes.
  4. **Making bytes.** `bytes()` is empty, `bytes(n)` is `n` zero bytes (`ValueError` for a negative `n`), `bytes(items)` takes a `list[int]` (or `tuple[int]`) whose items are all from 0 to 255 (`ValueError` E391 names the first item that is not: this is the clearly named conversion from the old `list[int]` data), `bytes(b)` is `b`, and `bytes('text')` is a compile error that says to encode. `list(b)` is the `list[int]` of the bytes. `bytes.fromhex(text)` (default: spelled as Python spells it, on the type name, rather than a new global `bytes_from_hex`; it is the only function attached to `bytes`) takes two hex digits for each byte, with ASCII whitespace allowed between the pairs.
  5. **Methods** (written in Tomato in `std/builtin/bytes.tom` over small runtime functions, so keywords and defaults work as for `str.zfill`): `decode(encoding='utf-8', errors='strict')` (the encodings are `'utf-8'` and `'ascii'`, spelled in any case with `-` or `_`; another is `ValueError` "unknown encoding"; `errors` is `'strict'`, which raises `ValueError` E498 "invalid UTF-8: the byte 0xff at position 1 cannot start a character" (or "starts a character that the next byte does not continue", or "... but the bytes end before it does": Python's three reasons, at the position Python gives), or `'replace'`, which puts U+FFFD for each maximal invalid sequence as Python does), `hex(sep='')` (`sep` is at most one character), `find`/`rfind`/`count(sub, start=0, end=...)` with Python's rules for the ends, `startswith`, `endswith`, `split(sep, maxsplit=-1)` (an empty `sep` is `ValueError`; there is no whitespace-splitting form, default), `join(items)`, `replace(old, new, count=-1)`, and `strip`/`lstrip`/`rstrip(chars=b' \t\n\r\x0b\x0c')`. `sub` and the like are `bytes` only (Python also takes an int there; default). Text has `s.encode(encoding='utf-8', errors='strict')`: UTF-8 copies the text's bytes (bytes in the text that are not UTF-8, from a file, are kept); `'ascii'` raises `ValueError` for a character above 127, or writes `?` with `errors='replace'`.
  6. **No `bytearray`.** A mutable form only adds, so it waits (follow-up; built later: D128a). Bytes are built by appending ints to a `list[int]` and calling `bytes(out)` once, by `b''.join(pieces)`, or with a `bytearray` (D128a).
  7. **The std migration (breaking, one way).** Every parameter that is binary data is `bytes` and every binary result is `bytes`; results that are text stay `str`:
     - `base64`: `b64encode(data: bytes) -> str`, `urlsafe_b64encode(data: bytes) -> str`, `b64decode(text: str) -> bytes`, `urlsafe_b64decode(text: str) -> bytes` (default: Python's encoders return bytes; Tomato's return text, because base64 text goes into JSON, URLs and headers and Tomato text is UTF-8, so nothing is gained by bytes there);
     - `hex.encode(data: bytes) -> str`, `hex.decode(text: str) -> bytes` (still strict: no whitespace, unlike `bytes.fromhex`);
     - `utf8.encode(text) -> bytes` and `utf8.decode(data: bytes, errors='strict') -> str` are kept (they are `s.encode()` and `b.decode(errors=...)`, with the same error ids as before);
     - `hashlib`: `update(data: bytes)`, `digest() -> bytes` (`hexdigest() -> str`), `new(name, data: bytes = b'')`, `sha256(data: bytes = b'')` and the others, `sha256_hex(data: bytes)` and the other one-shot hex helpers, `pbkdf2_hmac(name, password: bytes, salt: bytes, iterations, dklen=0) -> bytes`;
     - `hmac`: `HMAC(key: bytes, digestmod)`, `update(bytes)`, `digest() -> bytes`, `digest(key, msg, digestmod) -> bytes`, `hexdigest(...) -> str`, `compare_digest(a: bytes, b: bytes)`;
     - `zlib.crc32(data: bytes, value=0)`, `adler32(data: bytes, value=1)`;
     - `struct`: `int_to_bytes(...) -> bytes`, `int_from_bytes(data: bytes, ...)`, `pack_ints(fmt, values) -> bytes`, `unpack_ints(fmt, data: bytes) -> list[int]`, `Packer.raw(data: bytes)`, `Packer.bytes() -> bytes`, `Reader(data: bytes, byteorder)`, `read_raw(n) -> bytes`;
     - `uuid.UUID(data: bytes)` and its `data` field; `secrets.token_bytes(n) -> bytes`; `random.Random.randbytes(n) -> bytes` is new (four bytes of each 32-bit output, least significant first; the generator is not Python's, so the bytes are not Python's either);
     - `os.read_bytes(path) -> bytes`, `os.write_bytes(path, data: bytes, append=False)`;
     - `socket`: `Socket.send(data: bytes)`, `recv(n) -> bytes` (`b''` once the other side has closed), `DatagramSocket.sendto(data: bytes, host, port)`, `recvfrom(n) -> tuple[bytes, str, int]`;
     - `json` (MessagePack): `loads_msgpack(data: bytes)`, `dumps_msgpack(value) -> bytes`, `value.Bin(data: bytes)`, `value.Ext(code, data: bytes)`, `Value.as_bytes() -> bytes`;
     - `requests`: `Response.content` is `bytes`, and `data=` takes `str | bytes` (text is sent as its UTF-8, bytes as they are);
     - `terminal.KeyDecoder.feed(data: bytes)`.
     `io`, `asyncio` and `sys` have no binary API (`sys.run` passes text), so nothing changed there. The checks "a byte must be from 0 to 255" in these modules are gone: bytes cannot hold anything else, so the error moves to `bytes(list)`. MsgpackError E83 ("a byte must be an int from 0 to 255") and the os write code 4 can no longer happen (the E83 line stays in `std/errors.txt`, since ids are line numbers).
- Alternatives: (a) Keep `list[int]` and only change the signatures to `list[uint[length=8]]` (the review's fallback): rejected by Davis's decision; it rejects 300 but keeps no literal, no `decode`, and changes again when `bytes` comes. (b) `bytes` as a list of small ints in the runtime (a compact list with its own type): rejected, an immutable type needs no capacity and gains nothing from the list code, while the text layout gives hashing, comparison, concatenation and dict keys for free. (c) A global `bytes_from_hex(text)`: rejected for Python's `bytes.fromhex`, which the compiler can recognise on the type name. (d) `b64encode` returning bytes as Python does: rejected (see 7). (e) Keeping the old `list[int]` functions beside the new ones until 2.0: rejected by Davis (one way, breaking is fine before 1.0). (f) `bytearray` now: left for later (it only adds).
- Cost: a new built-in file (`std/builtin/bytes.tom`), about 300 lines of runtime C (index, slice, repeat, search, repr, hex, `fromhex`, the constructors, decode and encode), the type in the compiler (literals, `b[i]` and `for` as inline loads, the operators, `in`, printing, the constructors and method dispatch) and the lexer (`scan_bytes`, the literal's value travels as hex digits so the compiler never holds bytes that are not UTF-8 in a text). Programs that used the binary APIs with lists must change: `bytes(xs)` turns a list into bytes and `list(b)` back. 20 new lines in `std/errors.txt` (E624 to E643).
- Found while migrating (fixed here): `xs.extend(f())`, `xs.pop(f())` and `xs.insert(f(), v)` on a list read from a field did not free that list when the argument raised (the list was never held for the error path): `tests/programs/list_method_raise.tom`.
- Found by the independent audit, not changed (follow-ups; D128a settles the constants, the literal hints and the empty join): `bytes` constants (`const KEY = b'...'` is E261: a constant is a number, text or bool); `B'...'` and triple-quoted `b'''...'''` are not literals (the error is the generic one); `b'-'.join([])` needs a typed empty list (`bytes([])` works); an unknown escape such as `b'\N'` is an error, where Python keeps the backslash (the same rule as text, D17a).
- Found while migrating, not changed (follow-ups): `sorted(xs, key=f)` with a key function that returns bytes is refused (the key must be int, float or text; D128a accepts bytes); the internal MessagePack writer (`_pack` in `std/json.tom`) still builds into a list of ints that `dumps_msgpack` turns into bytes once.
- Tests: `tests/programs/bytes_type.tom` (every operation, method and error above, optionals, unions, dict and set keys, a class field, generics, a function value), `tests/negative/bytes_*.tom` (six rejected programs: text mixed with bytes, a non-ASCII and a short `\x` literal, `bytes('abc')`, a missing method, text on the left of `in`), `tests/errors/bytes_literal_non_ascii.tom` (the whole message), `tests/runtime/bytes_index.tom` and `bytes_decode_invalid.tom`, the `bytes_literals` test of `tests/lexer_spans.tom`, and every test of the migrated modules (`base64_codec`, `hex_codec`, `hashlib_vectors`, `hmac_vectors`, `wave2_bytes`, `struct_*`, `uuid_basic`, `secrets_basic`, `random_basic`, `bytes_files`, `os_browser`, `socket_local`, `socket_udp`, `socket_fair`, `msgpack_*`, `requests_local` (a body of bytes with a zero byte), `terminal_logic`, ...); in debug, AddressSanitizer with leak checks, release mode and WebAssembly.
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes language design review 8.1; the binary parts of D66a (MessagePack), D76c (sockets), D80a (`os.read_bytes`), Wave 2 pack D (binary helpers) and the `requests` decision now use `bytes`; D17a's escapes are unchanged for text (`\xHH` in a text is review 8.2, not done here).

### D126a — The 1.x compatibility promise: the language is frozen, std follows Python's rule (remove only after a minor release warned), the unstable list has 6 features: decided (Davis, 2026-11-27, relayed by his lead agent; the details marked "agent" are the writer's)

- Evidence: D109a triaged `docs/provisional.md` and left 13 judgment calls to Davis (`docs/provisional-triage.md`); D116a and D117a answered 1 to 4 on the same day. Davis answered judgments 5 to 11 and 13 later on 2026-11-27 and gave the rule behind them. In his words: "Any thing can change in a 1.x" (for the unstable items) and "Like python a module can be removed"; asked how std removal should work, he chose "deprecate one minor, then remove"; and the principle for all of it: "I prefer to freeze less than more." Judgment 12 (the review's other 1.0 blockers) was not part of the answers and is still open.
- Decision:
  1. **The language is frozen in 1.x** (syntax and rules; the whole of 1.0.0, 1.1.0, ... and not only 1.0.x): a program that works on 1.0.0 means the same on every later 1.x release. Frozen by name: P7 (a block starts no scope), P8 (conditions must be `bool`), P14 (a name declared again with the same type is accepted, with another type E292) and P17 (a subclass may not redeclare a field). The earlier adopted allowances stay (each in a minor release, under Breaking changes): enforcing a decided rule the compiler does not enforce yet, which is why P15 and P16 stay out of the promise (judgment 10, as recommended; D117a); the `x in T` warning becoming an error (D23i rule 5); an error moving to another class (D117a); reserved names, namely the built-in type names and the marker list (D108a, D113a). *(agent: these four are carried over from the old paragraph of `docs/releasing.md`; they are where "frozen" is read as "not changed except as already adopted", and Davis may strike any of them.)*
  2. **Std follows Python's rule.** A std module or function may be removed in a minor release (never a patch release), but only after at least one earlier minor release warned about it at compile time (deprecate in 1.N, remove in 1.N+1 or later); otherwise new things only add. The edge cases of the number-theory helpers P67, P70, P71, P73, P77, P78 and P79 are **not** frozen: a minor release may change them (Breaking changes); they are not on the unstable list, so a patch release does not.
  3. **A deprecation warning does not exist today** (checked: `deprecated` is only a reserved marker name, `compiler/src/parser.tom`; the compiler has no warning for a deprecated name and std has no way to mark one). Building it is a condition of the first removal: the `TODO.md` item "Build the compile-time deprecation warning for std" (an `@deprecated` marker in std source, or a data file the compiler reads, as the unstable list is, D121a). Nothing in std may be removed before it is built.
  4. **The unstable list grows from 4 to 6** (it may change in any release, a patch included, with no deprecation first): U1 error message wording, U2 the host input protocol, U3 the text `progress_bar` draws and U4 `random.choice`/`shuffle`/`sample` stay (judgments 5 and 6, as recommended); new are **U5**, the operator and protocol marker names beyond `@add` (`@sub @mul @div @floordiv @mod @pow @eq @lt @le @gt @ge @bitand @bitor @bitxor @invert @neg @rmul @hash @set`, and `@len @get @slice @contains @reversed @iter`; judgment 7, against my recommendation to promote; the names stay reserved), and **U6**, the order async tasks run in (P85, judgment 8, against my recommendation). *(agent: `@str`, `@init`, the entry markers and the mechanism are read as not covered, because judgment 7 named none of them; the rest of P85 stays promoted.)* The rows P23, P65 and P85 stay promoted for everything else, so the 91 rows still add up (61 promoted, 4 on the list as rows, 26 removed).
  5. **Outside the promise** (as recommended): the debug-mode leak report and `TOMATO_GC_STATS` (judgment 11); the Unicode version, which the changelog names when it changes (judgment 13).
- What the compiler does: nothing changes (docs only). `compiler/src/unstable.tom` still lists U1 to U4, because adding rows to it needs a bootstrap IR refresh and a wasm rebuild; U5 and U6 are in `docs/provisional.md` and are listed as pending in `tests/run.sh` (`t_unstable_features`, the variable `pending_unstable`), which keeps the agreement check consistent. `TODO.md` has the items for the deprecation warning and for the U5 and U6 warnings.
- Alternatives: (a) promote the marker names and the async order, as I recommended: rejected by Davis (changing a marker name later breaks every class that uses it, but he prefers to freeze less). (b) Freeze std as well (no removals): rejected, he chose Python's rule. (c) Remove a std module without a warning first: rejected, he chose one minor of deprecation. (d) Put the number-theory edges on the unstable list: not done, "changeable in a minor" is lighter than "any release" and fits what he asked.
- Cost: the unstable list covers more of the language than before (markers, async order), so a class that uses `@eq` or a program that starts tasks has no stability promise for those names and that order until a later decision freezes them; two TODO items (the deprecation warning, the U5/U6 warnings) are open.
- Supersedes / superseded by: replaces the compatibility paragraph of D84a and D109a's "the 1.0.x promise excepts only the list" (the count 7 became 4 in D116a and is now 6); completes D109a's judgments 5 to 11 and 13; adopts the Compatibility text of `docs/releasing.md`.

### D127a — A union is narrowed by a type test in any `elif`, not only when the first condition of the chain is the test: built (a gap in D55a found while migrating `requests` to bytes, D17e; nothing new is decided)

- Evidence: D55a narrows a union variable tested by `x is T` in the branch, in the `else`, in later `elif` bodies and after the `if`, but the compiler began tracking the variable only when the *first* condition of the chain was the test. `if flag: ... elif data is bytes: ... else: data != ''` therefore gave E190 in the `else` (and `len(data)` or `data + 'x'` on `str | int`), while the same code with the test first, or the `else` nested in an `if data is bytes:`, compiled. Found while migrating the `requests` module (D17e); the TODO item "Narrow a union in the `else` after an `elif` type test".
- Decision: the union variable of an `if` chain is the one tested by the first condition that is a type test (`x is T`, `x is not T`, `not (x is T)`, `x not in T`) of a union member, wherever it stands in the chain. The conditions before it say nothing about the variable, so it is still the whole union in their branches (the same as before); from the testing `elif` on, the rules of D55a hold unchanged: the tested member in its branch (`is not`: the others), the members left in later `elif` bodies and in `else`, and after the `if` the members of the branches that fall through. A branch before the first test that does not leave (no `return`, `raise`, `break` or `continue`) keeps all the members possible after the `if`, so the variable is not narrowed after it; when every branch before leaves, what the test rules out stays out to the end of the block, as for a first-condition test. The same holds for classes and interfaces (D112a) (optionals are narrowed by D102a's own rules, which the new tests also cover in this position). Only the first type-tested variable of a chain is tracked, as before; a second union variable tested later in the chain is not narrowed by the chain (nest the tests).
- Alternatives: (a) Track every union variable tested anywhere in the chain: rejected for now, because the chain keeps one list of members left and one of members kept; several variables need one pair each and a rule for a condition that tests two; the one-variable rule covers the case found (a plain condition, then the test). (b) Leave it and tell the user to nest the test in the `else`: rejected, since `if a: ... elif x is T: ... else: ...` is the natural way to write it and D55a promises the `else` is narrowed.
- Cost: none for programs that compiled; programs that were E190 or E221 in such an `else` or after such an `if` now compile.
- Tests: `tests/programs/union_narrowing_elif.tom` (`str | bytes`, `str | int`, a test two conditions deep, `is not`, three members with a condition between the tests, classes, narrowing after the `if` when every earlier branch leaves and when one falls through, optionals, a loop; also an AddressSanitizer build, a release-mode run and the WebAssembly run) and `tests/errors/union_elif_first_branch`, `union_elif_else_union` and `union_elif_falls_through` (the whole error text: the variable is the whole union before the test, a union of the members left in the `else` of a three-member union, and the whole union after an `if` whose earlier branch falls through).
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes D55a and D23i for chains whose first condition is not the test; closes the follow-up recorded under D17e.
### D128a — `bytearray`, the mutable byte buffer (a library class beside `bitarray`), and the four small gaps of the D17e audit closed: built (the details marked "agent" are the implementer's and not yet confirmed by Davis)

- Evidence: D17e left `bytearray` out ("a mutable form only adds") and listed four small gaps that the independent audit of `bytes` found; two TODO items asked for them (2026-11-27, relayed by Davis's lead agent), with the instruction to design `bytearray` conservatively, consistent with `bytes` and with the reserved std names (`std/reserved.txt` had `bytearray planned`, D115a).
- Decision, `bytearray`:
  1. **A library class in Tomato, not a new compiler type (agent).** `bytearray` is `std/builtin/bytearray.tom`, loaded without an import exactly as `bitarray` is (D64a): a file that uses the name gets `from bytearray import bytearray`, unless it imports the module or declares a `bytearray` of its own (then that is its own, as for `bitarray`); `import bytearray` and `from bytearray import bytearray` work too. The compiler's lazy loading of such a module is one list (`auto_modules`, `auto_names`) instead of the two names hard-coded for `bitarray`. In messages the type is `bytearray.bytearray`, as `bitarray.bitarray`. The module name moves from `planned` to `std` in `std/reserved.txt` and `compiler/src/modnames.tom` (36 std modules and 54 planned names, where D115a counted 35 and 55).
  2. **Storage (agent).** One `bytes` value that the object owns (its length is the capacity, `n` of it are the contents), changed in place by small runtime functions (`tomato_bytearray_slab`, `poke`, `move`, `reverse`, `take`, `slice`, `compare`, `index`) and replaced by one twice as big (at least 16 bytes) when it is full, so appending is linear and the buffer costs one byte for each byte. The slab is never shared: `bytearray(b)` and `bytes(ba)` copy, a slice makes its own, and a constant `b'...'` is never a slab, so nothing else can see a change.
  3. **Making one.** `bytearray()` is empty, `bytearray(n)` is `n` zero bytes (`ValueError` E646 for a negative `n`), `bytearray(b)` copies bytes, `bytearray(items)` takes a `list[int]` (an item outside 0..255 is the `ValueError` E391 of `bytes(items)`), `bytearray(other)` copies a bytearray; text is a compile error, as for `bytes`. `bytearray([])` needs a typed list (`xs: list[int] = []`), because the parameter is a union; `bytearray()` is the empty one. `bytes(ba)` is the bytes of it, a copy (the compiler calls the class's `_to_bytes`); `list(ba)` works through the iterator.
  4. **Operations (agent: Python's names and rules).** `len(ba)`; `ba[i]` is an `int` and `ba[i] = v` takes an int from 0 to 255 (`ValueError` E648; a negative index counts from the end, and outside the range is `IndexError` E647, "bytearray index 3 is out of range for a bytearray of 3"); `ba[i:j:k]` is a new bytearray (the slice rules of `bytes`; a zero step is the `ValueError` of lists); `+` with bytes or a bytearray, and `*` (either side), make a new bytearray (a count of 0 or less gives an empty one); `==` and `!=` and the orderings between two bytearrays, byte by byte; `x in ba` takes a byte (an int, `ValueError` outside 0..255) or bytes; `for x in ba` gives ints; `print`, `str()` and f-strings show `bytearray(b'...')`, as Python does. `a += x` is `a = a + x`, a new bytearray (as for every class with `@add`), where Python extends in place: use `a.extend(x)` to change the buffer that other references see. Methods that change it: `append(v)`, `extend(bytes or bytearray)` (`a.extend(a)` is fine), `insert(i, v)` (Python's clamping), `pop(i=-1)` (`IndexError` E649 when empty), `remove(v)` (the first one; `ValueError` E650 when it is absent), `clear()`, `reverse()`, `copy()`. Methods that only read, with the arguments and results of `bytes` (`sub` is bytes only): `find`, `rfind`, `count`, `startswith`, `endswith`, `hex(sep='')`, `decode(encoding='utf-8', errors='strict')`.
  5. **Bytes and bytearray never mix by themselves (agent).** There is no `ba == b'..'` (a compile error: convert one side), a function that takes `bytes` is given `bytes(ba)` (no std API takes a bytearray, and no `@bytes` conversion marker was added, unlike `@bitarray`), and `ba + b'..'` is the one mixed operator (a bytearray results). This is the conservative choice: it can be loosened later, and tightening could not be done.
  6. **Not hashable (agent).** A bytearray can change, so it is not a dict key or set element (a list is not either); the compiler's message for it is the general one about `@hash` (E472).
  7. **Left out, each a follow-up (agent):** slice assignment `ba[1:3] = b'..'` and `del`; the methods that make new bytes-like values (`split`, `join`, `replace`, `strip`, `lstrip`, `rstrip`: use `bytes(ba)` and the bytes method); `index`; `extend` with a list of ints; bytearray arguments where bytes are taken (`ba.find(other_bytearray)`); in-place `+=` and `*=`; `bytearray(text, encoding)`; a buffer view (`memoryview`).
- Decision, the four gaps of the audit:
  1. **`const K = b'...'`** is allowed: a constant is a number, text, bytes or `True` or `False` written out (E261's message says so: "must be a number, text, bytes or True or False written out").
  2. **`B'...'` and `b'''...'''`** each have their own error with a hint (E645 "a bytes literal starts with a lower case b, not B"; E644 "a bytes literal cannot have triple quotes", help: write it on one line with `\n`, or join pieces) in place of the generic "expected the end of the line". Only these two spellings are recognised (agent): `rb'..'`, `br'..'` and triple-quoted text keep the generic message. Bytes literals stay as D17e item 2 has them: no upper case prefix, no multi-line form, no raw form.
  3. **`b'-'.join([])`** already worked in every form tried (the empty list takes the type `list[bytes]` from the parameter), so nothing changed and a test keeps it (`tests/programs/bytes_gaps.tom`). The text form `'-'.join([])` is still E289 ("the type of an empty list cannot be guessed"): that is text's rule and was not asked for (follow-up).
  4. **`sorted(xs, key=f)` and `xs.sort(key=f)`** accept a key function that returns bytes, compared byte by byte as text keys are (E278's message names "an int, a float, text or bytes").
- Alternatives: (a) `bytearray` as a built-in type with its own runtime layout: rejected for now, because it needs the compiler to know index assignment, `append`, reference counting, boxing, printing and the key refusal of one more type, for the same visible behaviour; the class can become a built-in later without changing a program. (b) A `list[int]` as the storage: simple, but 8 bytes for each byte and a loop for each `bytes(ba)`. (c) A `@bytes` marker (Python's `__bytes__`) so that any class converts to bytes: rejected, more language than asked for. (d) A bytearray accepted wherever bytes are (every std API, `==`, the arguments of the methods, as Python does): rejected as the less conservative choice. (e) `+=` extends in place: rejected, it needs an in-place operator marker that no class has.
- Cost: one new built-in file (`std/builtin/bytearray.tom`, about 250 lines), eight runtime functions, a few lines in the compiler (`bytes(ba)`, the lazy module list, a bytes constant, a bytes sort key, two lexer errors), seven new lines in `std/errors.txt` (E644 to E650; the lines of E261, E278 and E632 changed their text), and `bytearray` in the three highlighter lists. A program or module that defines its own `bytearray` is unaffected (its own wins, as for `bitarray`); a project file `bytearray.tom` now shadows a std module, with the warning of D115a, where it was silent.
- Tests: `tests/programs/bytearray_type.tom` (every operation, method and error, growth by 100,000 appends, aliasing, fields, optionals, unions, lists, sorting, generics, a function value), `bytearray_names.tom` (a from-import, `import bytearray` in a library module, a module that declares its own), `bytes_gaps.tom` (a bytes constant, both sort keys, the empty join); `tests/errors/` (the whole text of nine rejected programs: a bytearray with bytes, with text, given to a function that wants bytes, made from text, a slice assignment, a dict key, `bytes(1.5)`, a constant that is not a literal, a list as a sort key, and the two literal hints), `tests/negative/bytearray_*` and `bytes_literal_capital_b`, `tests/runtime/bytearray_*`, the `bytes_literals` test of `tests/lexer_spans.tom`; the programs also run in AddressSanitizer with the leak check, in release mode and through WebAssembly.
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes D17e's item 6 ("No `bytearray`") and its audit follow-ups on constants, the literal hints and the sort key; changes D115a's list (`bytearray` goes from planned to std: 36 and 54).

### D129a — The compiler warns for a deprecated std name, and for U5 and U6 of the unstable list: built (the std rule is Davis's, D126a; the warning's design is the agent's)

- Evidence: D126a (Davis, 2026-11-27) lets a std module or function be removed in a minor release only after an earlier minor release warned about it at compile time, and put the marker names beyond `@add` (U5) and the order async tasks run in (U6) on the unstable list. Neither warning existed: `deprecated` was only a reserved marker name, and `compiler/src/unstable.tom` held U1 to U4. Davis prefers to freeze less than more, so both are the smallest thing that works, and no real std name is deprecated by this change.
- Decision (agent), the deprecation warning: a warning of the kind `deprecated`, `warning: 'old_double' is deprecated (module.old_double, since 1.1)` with a help line `use module.double instead` (or `nothing replaces it`), once for each name in a build, at its first use, shown before the program runs and silenced like every warning: `# allow: deprecated` on the line of a use, `--no-warnings`, `TOMATO_WARNINGS=off`; the repl, a seed's code and a std module never show it. What counts as a use is what D121a counts (`from module import name`, a dotted `module.name` or `alias.name`); for a whole deprecated module (a row with no dot) it is any import of the module, at the first one. A use on a line with the allow comment is skipped and the next use warns, as for `unstable`. It does not change what the program does or its exit status.
- One list: `compiler/src/deprecated.tom` (a module of data the compiler imports, as `unstable.tom` and `modnames.tom` are), one row for each name: `name|since|use`. `tests/run.sh` (`t_deprecated`) checks that every row names an item std exports (a function, class or constant; or a std module) and that the rows are those of the table "Deprecated names" in `docs/errors.md`, which the website shows. To deprecate a name: a row in the file, a row in the table, a line under **Deprecated** in the changelog of the minor release (the steps are in `docs/releasing.md`); the later removal takes the row out.
- The fixture: two rows are test-only, for the modules `deprecated_fixture` and `deprecated_fixture_module`, which are files of `tests/deprecated/` and not std; they let the suite try the warning (native, `tomato run`, WebAssembly) while no real name is deprecated, and `t_deprecated` exempts them from the "exists in std" check. A project that has a module of one of these names would get the warning too (harmless; the names are not ones anyone writes).
- Decision (agent), U5 and U6: `unstable.tom` has rows U5 and U6, whose names are `@sub,@mul,...` (the U5 markers, not `@add`, `@str`, `@init`, `static` or `bitarray`, which judgment 7 did not name) and `start`. The tree walk of `warn_unstable` takes a method whose marker is one of those (the warning points at the marker's own line, found above the method, so the allow comment goes there) and a `start` expression (at the word `start`). Reading the same list as D121a means the "once for each feature" rule is by feature, not by name: the first marker warns and the others of U5 do not.
- Tests: `tests/errors/unstable_markers`, `unstable_start` and `unstable_quiet` (now with `@add`, `@str`, `@init`: no warning), `tests/deprecated/` (`use`, `module`, `allow`, `quiet`: the whole text), `t_deprecated` (the list against std and the docs, the text), `t_cli_deprecated` (`tomato run`, the three ways to hide it, the repl), `t_wasm_unstable` (all of these through WebAssembly), and `t_unstable_features` no longer has a `pending_unstable` list: it checks every U5 name against the parser's marker names and the "what may change" column of the table.
- Alternatives: (a) an `@deprecated("use X")` marker on a std definition: rejected, it needs parser and AST changes (the marker is on a function, not a class or a module), and every std file then carries the facility; a data file is one place and was the way D121a worked. (b) A data file `std/deprecated.txt` read at run time: rejected as D121a rejected `std/unstable.txt` (the install, the release build, `site/wasm/build.sh` and the wasm file list would each carry it). (c) For U5 and U6, a warning at every marker or `start`: rejected, once for each feature is D121a's rule and a program with twenty `@eq` has one warning. (d) Edit the ~45 files of `tests/programs` that use a marker or `start` to carry `# allow: unstable` on every use: not done, `tests/run.sh` ignores the unstable warning (and only it) when it checks that a program of `tests/programs` compiles with no warning (`own_warnings`); the three examples that use them (`http_get`, `iteration`, `tasks`) carry the comment, since an example is what a reader copies.
- Cost: a bootstrap IR refresh and a wasm rebuild; a program that writes `@eq` or `start` now sees a warning on stderr until it adds the comment (one comment for each use after the first one that warns, so a program that starts tasks in five places writes five comments, or uses `--no-warnings`); the warning text of U5 and U6 may be reworded like every warning (U1).
- Supersedes / superseded by: completes the follow-ups of D121a (the two rows that were pending) and of D126a (the deprecation warning); leaves the compatibility promise as D126a wrote it, with the "does not exist yet" sentence of `docs/releasing.md` replaced.
